## ETUDE DES MECANISMES DE DISPERSION ATMOSPHERIQUE DE CONTAMINANTS:

Cas de la réutilisation d’eaux usées traitées épandues par aspersion [SOURCE: Page_2]

Soutenue publiquement le 20/12/2022 devant le jury composé de : [SOURCE: Page_2]

M. Yves Brunet, DR, INRAE Bordeaux Président du jury & Rapporteur Mme. Anne Oppliger, DR/PD & MER, Univ. Lausanne Rapporteure Mme. Sandrine Bayle, HDR, IMT Mines Alès Examinatrice M. Jean-Paul Douzals, DR, INRAE Montpellier Examinateur Mme. Dominique Courault, DR, INRAE Avignon Directrice de thèse Mme. Séverine Tomas, HDR, INRAE Montpellier Co-directrice de thèse M. Bruno Molle, PhD/IR, INRAE Montpellier Encadrant de thèse [SOURCE: Page_2]

1 [SOURCE: Page_2]

Résumé La réutilisation d’eaux usée traitée représente une alternative intéressante pour pallier le manque d’eau et contourner les périodes de pénuries de plus en plus fréquentes ces dernières années. On constate cependant que cette pratique est peu utilisée en France, en raison d’une réglementation contraignante, et une crainte 50   que ces eaux puissent contenir encore des contaminants ou pathogènes pas complètements éliminés par des traitements standards. Il manque de données sur ce dernier point en particulier concernant les bioaérosols qui sont émis suite à l’irrigation par aspersion. Très peu de mesures existent et les protocoles ne sont pas standardisés. L’irrigation par aspersion utilisant des eaux usées traitées peut présenter des risques sanitaires potentiels liés aux bioaérosols émis dans l’atmosphère. Le transport de ces aérosols est un phénomène complexe sur lequel on manque encore de connaissances sur les processus de dispersion. Il dépend de l'effet combiné de plusieurs paramètres dont la taille et la proportion de fines gouttelettes (<0,15 mm) et la vitesse du vent, qui sont les vecteurs les plus importants pour la transmission potentielle de pathogènes. L'objectif de notre étude a été de mieux comprendre les mécanismes de dispersion atmosphérique des 60   contaminants émis dans l'air par les eaux usées lorsque l’on irrigue par aspersion. Les principales questions auxquelles nous avons cherché à répondre ont été : -Quels sont les principaux facteurs et conditions affectant la dispersion des contaminants lors de l'irrigation par aspersion ? -Quelles techniques et méthodes doivent être utilisées pour la détection et l'échantillonnage des (bio) aérosols générés par l'irrigation par aspersion ? – Comment modéliser le transport et la dispersion des aérosols provenant de l'irrigation par aspersion des eaux usées traitées ? Pour répondre à ces questions, différentes expériences ont été réalisées en conditions contrôlées et en extérieur pour diverses conditions environnementales. Des comparaisons entre différents types de collecteurs (actifs (AGI-4 et SKC BioSampler) et passifs (fils de PVC)) ont été faites en utilisant deux traceurs. Avec un colorant fluorescent (Acid Brilliant Flavine), les résultats ont montré que l'efficacité 70   physique de l'AGI-4 était presque 16,3 % plus élevée que celle du SKC BioSampler dans la plage de 5 à 30 minutes. Des expériences ont également été menées avec E.coli. L'efficacité du SKC est apparue supérieure à celle de l'AGI-4 pour les différentes conditions étudiées. L'augmentation du temps d'échantillonnage de 5 min à 15 min, montre que la viabilité d'E.coli est réduite dans les deux AGI-4 et SKC d'un facteur de 37% et 14%, respectivement. Des expériences en soufflerie ont permis d’analyser le comportement d’un nuage de fines gouttes. En conditions extérieures, neuf expériences ont été menées avec différentes conditions environnementales. En injectant de l'ABF dans l'eau, la distribution des aérosols produite à partir d'un asperseur soumis à une pression de fonctionnement de 350 kPa a été mesurée verticalement (1–10 m au-dessus du sol) sous le vent et horizontalement dans un périmètre équivalent à 4 fois la portée de l'asperseur (20–40 m). La distribution 80   transversale au vent du panache de gouttelettes provenant d'un asperseur suit une distribution gaussienne, et le taux de dispersion dépend de la vitesse du vent et de l'écart type de la direction du vent. Différents modèles ont été évalués en faisant varier les facteurs climatiques mesurés. Deux modèles empiriques combinant des variables spatiales et les principaux paramètres météorologiques, notamment la vitesse du vent, l'écart type de la direction du vent, la température et l'humidité relative (pour le transport advectif), fournissent les meilleures corrélations entre les valeurs de transport simulées et les données observées (R$_2$=0,9). Ces modèles peuvent être utilisés pour de futures études visant à évaluer les risques liés à la réutilisation des eaux usées traitées pour l'irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_4]

Mots clés : Eaux usées traitées, Echantillonnage d’aérosols, Irrigation par aspersion, Modèle empirique, 90   Soufflerie, Transport advectif [SOURCE: Page_4]

3 [SOURCE: Page_4]

Abstract Study of atmospheric dispersion mechanism of contaminants: Case of treated wastewater reuse land applied sprinkler [SOURCE: Page_5]

There is a wide variety of residual pathogens in treated wastewater (TWW), which often go undetected or underestimated in aerosols. There is a lack of knowledge of the potential dissemination of contaminant pathogens remaining in the treated wastewater. The use of sprinkler irrigation for distributing treated wastewater may cause potential health risks linked to human contact with these contaminants through inhalation, dermal contact or ingestion. Transport of aerosols resulting from sprinkler irrigation of treated 100   wastewater is a complex phenomenon due to the combined effect of several parameters including fine droplets (<0.15 mm) proportion and wind speed, which are the most important vectors for pathogen transmission. The objective of our study was to better understand the atmospheric dispersion mechanisms of contaminants emitted in the air from TWW by sprinkler irrigation. The main questions we wanted to address were: -What are the main factors and conditions affecting the dispersion of contaminants during sprinkler irrigation? -What techniques and methods should be used for the detection and sampling of (bio)aerosols generated by sprinkler irrigation? - How to model the transport and dispersion of aerosols from spray irrigation of the treated wastewater? To address these questions, different experiments were carried out both in controlled and field conditions for various environmental configurations. Comparisons between different active samplers were done using different tracers. Using a fluorescent dye (Acid Brilliant 110   Flavine), the results have shown that the physical efficiency of AGI-4 was almost 16.3% higher than the SKC BioSampler in the range of 5–30 min. Experiments were also conducted with E coli with the same active samplers. Efficiency of SKC BioSampler was higher compared to AGI for the different studied conditions. Increasing sampling time from 5 min to 15 min, reduced the culturability of E.coli in both AGI– 4 and SKC BioSampler by a factor of 37% and 14%, respectively. Wind tunnel experiments have made it possible to analyze the behavior of a cloud of fine drops. Nine experiments have been conducted outdoor under different environmental conditions. By injecting ABF into the water, the distribution of aerosols emanating from an impact sprinkler under 350 kPa operating pressure was measured vertically (1–10 m above the ground) downwind and horizontally within a perimeter equivalent to 4 times the sprinkler range (20–40 m). Crosswind distribution of the droplet 120   plume from a sprinkler was found to be Gaussian, and its dispersion rate depended on wind speed and the standard deviation of the wind direction. Different models have been evaluated by varying the factors considered. It was found that two empirical models, which combine spatial variables, and the key meteorological parameters, including wind speed, the standard deviation of wind direction, temperature, and relative humidity (for airborne transport), provide the best correlations between the simulated transport values and the observed data (R$_2$=0.9). Therefore, these models can be used for future studies to evaluate the risks linked to treated wastewater reuse for irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Keywords: Air sampling, Airborne transport, Empirical modeling, Sprinkler irrigation, Treated wastewater, 130   Wind tunnel [SOURCE: Page_5]

580   Introduction [SOURCE: Page_27]

Context The reuse of treated wastewater is a common practice in various countries such as Israel, Tunisia, Spain, Australia or the USA. Depending on the region, wastewater reuse is considered controlled (i.e. the wastewater is treated to minimize risks) or uncontrolled (i.e. the wastewater is discharged directly in water bodies and reuse, or directly reused). Thebo et al, (2017) estimated the worldwide surface area irrigated with controlled treated wastewater to 6 million hectares, when the area receiving partially or untreated wastewater represents up to 30 million hectors. This, in total, represents approximately 12% of irrigated areas. The practice is not a lot developed in France, partly because of the relative abundance of resources, and partly because of the strict regulation, mainly due to the lack of knowledge of the potential 590   dissemination of contaminant pathogens remaining in the treated wastewater. [SOURCE: Page_27]

The French regulation prepared by ANSES and soon replaced by a European text, even more restrictive, considers the specificity of sprinkler, that disperses water in the air to form a kind of rainfall. As such irrigation can occur under windy conditions, the pathogens contained in the dispersed droplets that can be released into the air may induce risks of contamination. Thus, managing risks requires a good knowledge of droplets formation and path in real conditions through dedicated measurement methods. [SOURCE: Page_27]

Sprinklers disperse water in the air in the form of one or several jets that generates droplets. Depending on their size these droplets may drift under the influence of wind and induce the formation of aerosols. These could be bio-aerosols as they may contain various contaminants some of them being harmful. But, today there is no accepted standard sampling method for aerosolized particles issued from a sprinkling 600   system and the contaminants they may contain. [SOURCE: Page_27]

Very few in field characterizations of bioaerosols from irrigation with wastewater are reported in the literature. Transport of aerosol induced by sprinkler irrigation of treated wastewater is a multi-parameters phenomenon not yet sufficiency analyzed . [SOURCE: Page_27]

26 [SOURCE: Page_27]

Objectives of the thesis In this context, the present work will investigate knowledge to address the following questions: [SOURCE: Page_28]

•    What are the factors and conditions affecting the dispersion of contaminants during sprinkler irrigation? [SOURCE: Page_28]

•    What techniques and methods should be used for the detection and sampling of 610                    (bio)aerosols generated by sprinkler irrigation ? [SOURCE: Page_28]

•    How to model the transport and dispersion of aerosols issued from irrigation sprays of the treated wastewater? [SOURCE: Page_28]

To address these questions, we have carried out different experiments that will be described in Parts II and III of this report. This will be preceded by a Part I that presents a literature review, with the Chapter 1 dedicated to an overview of the experiences of reuse of treated wastewater for irrigation, and the Chapter 2 focusing on the description of air sampling methods developed. [SOURCE: Page_28]

Part II was devoted to the laboratory experiments and divided in two chapters. Chapter 3 lists and describes the different sampling methods that have been developed to date to measure the quantity of airborne particles in controlled and low wind conditions, and the impact of measurement parameters on the 620   survival of E.Coli. Chapter 4 focusses on the characterization of fine droplets spray in controlled conditions (wind tunnel experiment). [SOURCE: Page_28]

The part III was dedicated to the in-situ experiments (Chapter 5) evaluating the aerosol losses to which an agricultural impact sprinkler is subject to, using passive samplers covering a distance corresponding to approximately 4 times the sprinkler radius of throw. [SOURCE: Page_28]

27 [SOURCE: Page_28]

Part I. State of the art Treated wastewater and sprinkler Air sampling methods-Overview irrigation [SOURCE: Page_29]

Part II. Laboratory Experiments Performance assessment of active Droplet size distribution in moving air samplers in calm air [SOURCE: Page_29]

Part III. In-situ Experiments [SOURCE: Page_29]

Transport and distribution of aerosols from sprinkler irrigation-Field study [SOURCE: Page_29]

General conclusion and Perspective Figure Int. 1. Schematic representation of the outline of this thesis [SOURCE: Page_29]

28 [SOURCE: Page_29]

Part I. State of the art [SOURCE: Page_30]

630 [SOURCE: Page_30]

29 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Treated wastewater and sprinkler irrigation–Overview [SOURCE: Page_31]

Water scarcity and the reuse of treated wastewater Water scarcity is already one of the global challenges (EEA, 2018; Mekonnen and Hoekstra, 2016), caused by urbanization and economic development, population growth, together with climate change and the mismatch between the cropping season and effective rainfall (Dehghani et al., 2022; He et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_31]

The reuse of treated wastewater (TWW) in agricultural and landscape irrigation has therefore become an important solution worldwide to mitigate the impacts of water scarcity and the degradation of 640   conventional water resource quality (Nikolaou et al., 2020; Shevah, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_31]

Water consumption and water scarcity in the world Worldwide, 70 percent of total freshwater withdrawals is destined for the agriculture sector, which tends to increase (FAO, 2021; Ritchie and Roser, 2017). Rising temperature on the growing season driven by climate change, much visible in the last decades, could cause more variable water availability and exacerbate evapotranspiration and crop water requirement and subsequent the risks of agricultural production losses (DeNicola et al., 2015; Hristov et al., 2021). An estimated 3.2 billion people live in agricultural regions with high to very high water stress or scarcity, of whom 1.2 billion people (about one- sixth of the world’s population) live in severely water-constrained agricultural areas (FAO, 2020). Figure 1.1a shows that the countries of Central Asia, the Middle East-West Asia and North Africa are at the highest 650   levels of water stress for the agricultural sector (FAO, 2021). It was projected that by 2040 (Figure 1.1b), the number of countries exposed to severe water stress in all sectors will generally increase due to climate change, bio-energy demands and expected population growth (Gosling and Arnell, 2016; Stenzel et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_31]

30 [SOURCE: Page_31]

(a) [SOURCE: Page_32]

(b) Figure 1.1. (a) Level of water stress due to the agricultural sector by basin, 2018 (Source: FAO, 2021); (b) Water stress by country: 2040. Note: Projections are based on a business-as-usual scenario using SSP$_2$ and RCP$_8$.5 (Source: World Resources Institute, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_32]

Reuse of treated wastewater for irrigation 660      Reuse of treated wastewater can alleviate water stress on the agricultural sector and constitute a significant component when operating integrated water resources management (Intriago et al., 2018; Stathatou et al., 2014). The practice of domestic/municipal wastewater reuse (both treated and untreated) for agricultural and landscape irrigation has a long development history (Jones et al., 2021; Qadir et al., 2007). Today, advanced techniques for treatment of wastewater and reuse projects are being planned and implemented more rapidly throughout the world (Angelakis et al., 2018). An estimated at least 300 million hectares in 50 countries, or 10% of the total irrigated land, which is 17% of the total arable land, are irrigated with partially treated wastewater worldwide (Jiménez, 2006; Ungureanu et al., 2020). Drechsel and Evans (2010) stated that estimates of the total area that is being irrigated with wastewater (both treated [SOURCE: Page_32]

31 [SOURCE: Page_32]

and untreated) are still fragmentary, the numbers mentioned are likely to range between 5 and 20 million 670   hectares (WWAP, 2017). In addition, the use of treated wastewater for irrigation purposes has further increased in recent years. Based on the last data obtained by AQUASTAT (FAO AquaStat, 2022), approximately 8.5 million hectares in 38 countries (recorded data from 2019) are equipped for irrigation by direct use of treated municipal wastewater. Table 1.1 also displays the top 22 countries that reuse treated municipal wastewater for irrigation purposes (109 m^3.yr−1), placing Mexico in first place (with 1.38×109 m^3.yr−1), followed by China (with 1.26 ×109 m^3.yr−1). Note that the data was not available for all countries, including two important agricultural producing countries (India and USA). [SOURCE: Page_33]

FAO (FAO Statistics, 2021) estimates that in the entire world 342 million hectares are equipped for agricultural irrigation in 2019 (Figure 1.2). Asia accounts for 70 percent of the total area of the world among which a large part corresponds to paddy rice. Among the three main types of irrigation (surface 680   irrigation, sprinkler irrigation and localized irrigation), sprinkler irrigation is applied on about 11 % of area equipped for irrigation, followed by localized irrigation about 4 % in worldwide. As shown in Table 1.1, the type and diffusion of irrigation technologies are quite diverse in the countries. In France, sprinkler irrigation is the most widely-used irrigation system [almost 90%]. Irrigation is also practiced in urban dwellings, if the shrubs and trees are mostly irrigated with localized irrigation techniques, the surfaces covered by grass (public parks, private lawn, and sport fields) are mostly sprinkler irrigated. [SOURCE: Page_33]

Sprinkler irrigation Sprinkler irrigation is a method in which water discharges from nozzle(s) or small orifices under pressure into the air in the form of a water jet. The water jet then breaks up in droplets following the process of atomization (divided into primary and secondary atomization), which fall on the soil or crop canopy like natural rainfall. Note that in the case of the sprinkler irrigation, injection velocity is high (around 10–35 m.s-1), so the breakup regime is that of atomization: a complete jet disruption, producing ligaments that divide in droplets out of the nozzle (Lefebvre and McDonell, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_35]

The primary step of the atomization regime, initial flow deformation and the subsequent production of 700   liquid fragments, is strongly influenced by geometry of the nozzle, liquid viscosity, liquid cavitation at the nozzle entrance, jet velocity and turbulence, and surface tension (Dumouchel, 2008; Stevenin, 2012; Wu et al., 1995). Aerodynamic drag forces, air turbulence and surface tension dominate the secondary one: continued distortion and disintegration of liquid fragments until the production of stable droplets. (Diemuodeke and Sher, 2016; Dumouchel, 2008). Figure 1.3 illustrates the break up process at a jet’s centerline (average jet velocity=35 m.s-1) ranging from 𝑥/𝑑𝑛 = 0 (model nozzle point) to 𝑥/𝑑𝑛 = 800 (Felis-Carrasco, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_35]

Figure 1.3. A set of shadow images at the jet’s centerline from 𝑥/𝑑𝑛 = 0 to 𝑥/𝑑𝑛 = 800, where 𝑥 is the distance from a model nozzle exit in mm, and 𝑑𝑛 is the diameter of the nozzle (𝑑𝑛 = 1.2 mm) from 710                                             Felis-Carrasco (2017). [SOURCE: Page_35]

Sprinklers irrigation systems are classified according to each of the their special features, based on spraying mechanism, portability, precipitation rates, radius of throw (low [<13 m], medium [13–25 m] or high [> 25 m]), principle of operation, and movement. Based on their movement, sprinkler irrigation systems are classified into following two general categories: [SOURCE: Page_35]

---

- Parent_Topic: 4078033

- Summary: Section focusing on 1. set system: sprinklers remain at a fixed position while irrigating;

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

---

### 1. Set system: sprinklers remain at a fixed position while irrigating;

2. Continuous-Move system: sprinklers are fixed on a supply line which moves continuously in a particular direction (ISO 15886-1, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_35]

Figure 1.4 presents a diagram of major types of sprinkler irrigation systems related to each category, some of which are shown in Figure 1.5. The specifications and application conditions of each of these 720   systems have been well explained in most of the irrigation books and manuals, such as Irrigation [SOURCE: Page_35]

34 [SOURCE: Page_35]

Handbook, published by USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service (2016) [see Part 623, Chapter 11 - Sprinkler Irrigation]. [SOURCE: Page_36]

Figure 1.4. Classification of the major types of Sprinkler irrigation systems based on their movement. [SOURCE: Page_36]

Wheel - Move            Linear-Moving Lateral               Travelling Gun [SOURCE: Page_36]

Hand Move System                   Solid Set          Wheel Chassis Booms Systemhttps://images.google.com). Figure 1.5. Examples of several types of sprinkler irrigation systems (source: [SOURCE: Page_36]

Many kinds of sprinkler heads are available, some are shown in Figure 1.6. The selection of an 730   appropriate sprinkler and nozzle combination requires knowledge on system types, sprinkler spacing, operating pressures, soil, topography (e.g., slope), the type and stage of growth of the crop, and meteorological conditions. Until now, various studies have been carried out on the performance of the sprinklers to improve their water distribution uniformity and to limit run-off and drift losses (Bartoli et al., 2021; Ge et al., 2020; Kadem et al., 2008; Kincaid et al., 1996; Molle et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_36]

35 [SOURCE: Page_36]

Big Gun                     Impact sprinkler              Rotator® sprinkler for Pivot [SOURCE: Page_37]

Bubbler-LEPA*                   Rotor              Micro-sprinkler                Fogger Figure 1.6. Examples of the types of sprinkler heads available. *LEPA: Low Energy Precision Application (Sources: Big Gun–https://www.kometirrigation.com/; Rotator®– https://nelsonirrigation.com/; Bubbler-LEPA–https://www.senninger.com; Impact sprinkler and Rotor– 740                https://www.rainbird.com/; Micros prinkler–https://www.antelco.com/; Fogger– https://www.rivulis.com/) [SOURCE: Page_37]

Sprinkler irrigation with treated wastewater As a sprinkler reproduced a rainfall the use of such irrigation for distributing treated wastewater may cause potential health risks linked to human contact with contaminants with TWW through inhalation, dermal contact or ingestion because of exposure to aerosols (Adegoke et al., 2018; de Matos Nascimento et al., 2020; Fattal et al., 1986). The investigations into the dispersion of aerosolized pathogens such as viruses and bacteria during and after the sprinkler irrigation of TWW have shown their decreased viability and airborne concentration with distance from irrigation source (Fattal and Teltsch, 1982; Katzenelson and Teltch, 1976; Kritana et al., 2014; Linnemann et al., 1984; Margalith et al., 1990). A study by Shuval et al. 750   (1989) detected a low concentration ranging from 1–100 (CFU.m-3) for bacteria and 0.03–2 (PFU.m-2) for viruses over distances up to 730 m downwind of wastewater (partially treated) irrigated field. [SOURCE: Page_37]

Aerosol definition The term “aerosol” refers to suspension of liquid droplets or fine solid particles in the air that are generated by spray irrigation of TWW. Bioaerosols are also defined as biological particles containing living and dead organisms (such as protozoa and helminths, bacteria, and viruses) and various fragments or excretions in aerosols (Figure 1.7) and are typically surrounded by a thin layer of water. [SOURCE: Page_37]

36 [SOURCE: Page_37]

Figure 1.7. Characteristic size ranges of atmospheric particles and bioaerosols (A) protein (B) virus, (C) bacteria, (D) fungal spore and (E) pollen grain (Fröhlich-Nowoisky et al., 2016) [SOURCE: Page_38]

760          Factors affecting the fate and viability of aerosolized TWW microorganisms Exposure via sprinkler irrigation strongly depends from the factors that influence the fate and viability of pathogen-containing aerosols. These primary factors can fall into the following categories (Brooks et al., 2005): the physical characteristics of aerosol (Maier et al., 2000; Rojas-Valencia and Mier, 2013), their aerodynamic behavior (Derkachov et al., 2008), their chemical composition related to the nature of wastewater, the level of treatments (Harris et al. 2012), and more important, atmospheric parameters: relative humidity, solar radiation and temperature (Brown and Mohr, 2016; Teltsch and Katzenelson, 1978). All other stresses are defined as secondary factors, like initial shock associated with the atomization process (internal geometrical spray nozzle characteristics and pressure behind them), sampling and analysis methods (Cox and Wathes, 1995; Donnison et al., 2004; Ghosh et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_38]

770      Furthermore, the extent of resistance or die-off due to these factors also depends on the type of pathogens (Bertrand et al., 2012; Courault et al., 2017; Cox, 1966; De Giglio et al., 2019; Hamilton et al., 2018). For example, experimental studies involving aerosols of infectious samples implemented by van Doremalen et al. (2020) and Fears et al. (2020) showed that SARS-CoV-2 can remain viable for up to several hours. [SOURCE: Page_38]

Additionally, viruses and other pathogens which are not completely eliminated by common water treatment and transported into the soil by irrigation water could also be aerosolized later during windy conditions (Girardin et al., 2016). In another study, Jones and Harrison (2004) found that minimum wind speeds of approximately 0.4 m.s-1 are required for microbial aerosol emission from plant canopies. [SOURCE: Page_38]

Bioaerosol dispersion via spray irrigation of wastewater: Field studies 780      A review on field studies conducted to evaluate the factors affecting the dispersion of bio-aerosols from sprinkler irrigation of (partially or untreated) wastewater has been realized, where their results have been summarized in Table 1.2. The majority of these studies established that low humidity and high solar [SOURCE: Page_38]

37 [SOURCE: Page_38]

radiation reduce bacteria survival in bioaerosols. Therefore, the highest downwind concentrations were observed when irrigation occurred at night. Hardy et al. (2006) also stated that worst-case conditions that lead to the greatest exposure and potential risk of infection are nighttime low-wind stable conditions, which maximize the inhalation pathway, and high-wind conditions, which maximize the deposition and produce ingestion pathway. [SOURCE: Page_39]

It is important to note that the wastewater in use, in most of the studies, was untreated or partially (poor) treated. In addition, apart from the specific configurations, most of the experiments were carried out in a 790   restricted number of regions (United states and Israel), so the results may not be generalized in other ones because of differences in meteorological conditions. [SOURCE: Page_39]

Devaux et al. (2001) found no health risks for farmers associated with spray irrigation using treated wastewater with water concentrations equal to 103 CFU/100mL in Clermont-Ferrand, France, compared with non-exposed family members or farmers not using treated wastewater. [SOURCE: Page_39]

As can also be seen from the table below, in recent years, very few data have been published on pathogen or faecal indicator transport by spray and aerosols resulting from aspersion with treated waste water (Brissaud et al., 2008). Although, a number of studies on the exposure and risks for spray irrigated livestock manure have been recently presented (Burch et al., 2017; Dungan, 2014; Dungan et al., 2011; Nag et al., 2021), but human pathogens do not find in livestock manure and its treatment process differs 800   from municipal wastewater. Moreover, the SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) problem has exploded the number of papers relating to the dispersion of aerosols in indoor and outdoor air (Asdaq et al., 2022; Azuma et al., 2020; Kifer et al., 2021; Li et al., 2020; Peng et al., 2022). Albeit, the results gave extremely useful contribution in understanding the effect of the various factors on the airborne transmission of the virus via respiratory droplets and the fate of the virus in the atmosphere, but the data cannot be generalized for the case of TWW spray irrigation. That is because the atomization process of sprinklers, and subsequently, the initial shock to the viruses (presented earlier in section 1.3.2), the ballistic behaviors of airborne droplets and their dehydration differ completely from the spread of droplets containing SARS CoV-2 virus, which have been released in the air by respiratory activities [e.g., breathing (UInitial =1 m.s-1), coughing (UInitial =10 m.s-1), sneezing (UInitial =100 m.s-1)](Katre et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_39]

810 [SOURCE: Page_39]

Quantitative Microbial Risk Assessment 820      Quantitative Microbial Risk Assessment (QMRA) is a risk assessment approach, consisting in the following four steps: i) hazard identification; ii) exposure assessment; iii) dose-response modeling; and iv) risk characterization. Several studies have employed the QMRA models to investigate the infection (enteric or respiratory pathogens) risks associated with sprinkler irrigation with treated wastewater for farmworkers or individuals inhabiting downwind from these agricultural plots. They used Gaussian models (see 1.5.1) with specific site, meteorological data or computational fluid dynamics based simulation models, CFD (see section 1.5.4). Some of these researches have been discussed here, but given that this assessment approach is outside the scope of our thesis, the procedure for each step was not elaborated. The results of the present work will contribute to such risk evaluation. [SOURCE: Page_43]

The research of Courault et al. (2017), which have been presented in Table 1.2, consisted in an 830   assessment of the health risk associated with inhaled norovirus using a Bayesian QMRA approach that included an atmospheric dispersion model. It was determined that if the annual probability of infection at 500 m and a constant virus emission rate of 8 GC.m-3.s-1 is high, i.e., 6.3E-04 at the 95th percentile (a wind speed of 1 m.s-1), the risk is increased by 2 log at 6 m.s-1. This probability decreases by 3 log for a wind speed of 1 m.s-1 when the distance to the emission source is doubled (1000 m). [SOURCE: Page_43]

Hamilton et al. (2018) develop a generalized model for long-range transport with reclaimed water containing Legionella under a range of meteorological conditions that does not rely upon intensive site specific information. Risks (infection or clinical severity infection) were compared using data from nineteen United States reclaimed water utilities measured with culture-based methods, quantitative PCR (qPCR), and ethidium-monoazide-qPCR (EMA-qPCR based). A maximum trajectory height of 6 m and a 840   distance of 24.4 m to reach the apex of the sprinkler stream was added to estimates for sprinkler (sprinkler height <1 m) setback distances. The results showed that for annual infection residential population risks, setback distances would be between <75 m (culture based) and about 625 m (qPCR-based) based on median values for comparison. 95th percentile annual clinical severity residential population risks were <10-4 for culture-based and EMA-qPCR methods, but not for qPCR (corresponding to about 225 m setback distance). Occupational annual infection risks indicate a setback distance almost 5025 m (culture-based) or ≥10,000 m (EMA-qPCR or qPCR based) and occupational clinical severity risks correspond to distances ranging from <75 m (culture-based) to around 1225 m (qPCR based) using a 95th percentile comparison and <75 m using a median comparison. [SOURCE: Page_43]

Mori and Smith (2022) conducted a QMRA to determine an individual adult’s risk of infection with L. 850   pneumophila from a single exposure to agricultural spray irrigation, over the growing seasons. Each unique combination of receptor distance (1–7 km) and a spray irrigator with two operating pressure of 10 psi or 69 kPa (low) and 30 psi or 207 kPa (high) with the aerosol concentrations of 0.2506 and 0.495 µg.m-3 air (median values), respectively, have been referred to as a “scenario”. It was found that the mean risk of [SOURCE: Page_43]

42 [SOURCE: Page_43]

infection for a single exposure exceeded the safety threshold of 10–6 infections/exposure up to 1 km from a low-pressure irrigator and up to 2 km from a high-pressure irrigator, although no median risk exceeded the threshold for any distance or irrigator pressure. [SOURCE: Page_44]

This is an important consideration for irrigation management as when calculating the risks for reclaimed water reuse in irrigation many QMRA studies only consider the crop consumption scenario, which may not be the scenario entailing the highest risk. However, the risks for accidental aerosol ingestion were 860   calculated using very conservative estimates, considering a high number of exposures per year and that the irrigation was via sprinklers. This is often not the case in irrigation management, for example, at Shafdan and the Bolivar sites, drip irrigation is more frequently used than sprinkler irrigation (Ayuso-Gabella et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_44]

Regulations and politics Regulations and guidelines were defined by international organizations such as World Health Organization (WHO 2006), the European Environment Agency (EEA, 2020), the United States Environmental Protection Agency (US EPA, 2012), Australia (EPHC/NRMMC/AHMC, 2006). In France, different regulations and decree have been adopted (ANSES, 2012; NOR: TREL1803081A, 2018; NOR- AFSP1410752A, 2014). Table 1.3 represents guidelines and regulations for the safe use of spray irrigation 870   with treated wastewater. In most countries, no setback distance (buffer zone) are set when using spray irrigation and they focus more on a E. coli content criterion. As shown in Table 1.3, the regulation of the reuse of treated wastewater for irrigation in France is relatively strict, and will be reinforced in 2023 by European regulations. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Despite these guidelines, there is still lack of knowledge about 1) health risk due to dispersion of pathogens by sprinkler irrigation because of too little measurements of bioaerosols and experimental data, and 2) robust methods to evaluate bioaerosol transports (Zardari et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_44]

Unlike a subsurface dispersal system, a spray distribution system requires the greatest level of wastewater pretreatment because of the risk of human contact. This in turn increases the cost of the advanced pretreatment system, because it must produce a high-quality effluent and includes a disinfection 880   component. [SOURCE: Page_44]

43 [SOURCE: Page_44]

900        Transport and wind drift There is a wide variety of pathogens in reclaimed wastewater, which often go undetected or underestimated in aerosol from sprinkler irrigation. The simplest and most practical way of estimating microbial risks is to provide relevant information on the fraction of TWW spray flow that becomes aerosolized, and their dynamic movement (Hardy et al., 2006; Molle et al., 2016). This spray volume component is defined by the term of “transport”, which in the present study corresponds to aerosolization efficiency. Transport is therefore the volume of water that escapes from the wetted radius under windy conditions in the form of aerosols (Figure 1.8). For irrigation purposes, it corresponds to an application rate of less than 0.26 mm.h-1 that cannot be quantified directly using rain-gauges (ISO 15886–3, 2012). Note that the term of “wind drift” defines as the volume of water that escapes from the wetted perimeter 910   under the influence of the wind, which corresponds to an application rate greater than 0.26 mm.h -1 (see Figure 1.8). [SOURCE: Page_48]

Figure 1.8. Schematic representation of the notion of transport, wind drift and evaporation losses [SOURCE: Page_48]

Factors affecting the aerosol transport Transport is an intricate phenomenon combining the effect of several parameters (Figure 1.9), among others: i) the proportion of droplets inferior to 0.1–0.15 mm, which can completely evaporate (Hardy et al., 2006; Molle et al., 2012; Xie et al., 2007), and become aerosolized under moderate humidity conditions, ii) wind that plays a major role in droplet transport (Courault et al., 2014; Molle et al., 2016) and that can [SOURCE: Page_48]

47 [SOURCE: Page_48]

920   cancel out gravity effects for smaller particles. Thus, these two parameters with other meteorological parameters (temperature, relative humidity, air stability and solar radiation) which have been studied in this thesis (presented in Chapter 5) have been explained as follows. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Figure 1.9. Factors affecting the transport from sprinkler irrigation [SOURCE: Page_49]

---

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Parent_Topic: 4078033

- Summary: Section focusing on 1.4.1.1. droplet characteristics

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation Research

- Confidence_Score: High

---

### 1.4.1.1. Droplet characteristics

Droplet size The size of droplets is the critical parameter in the transport phenomenon (Pepper and Gerba, 2018; 930   Wang et al., 2020). The airborne infection is relevant to dried-out infectious droplet nuclei [< 10 µm (Xie et al., 2007)] derived directly from droplet less than 0.15 mm (Hardy et al., 2006; Molle et al., 2012; Xue et al., 2021), which remain suspended in the air for a long time and could be carried over long distances by the air current (Figure 1.10). Although there is no specific droplet size range that is likely to totally evaporate before falling under all conditions, certain researchers suggest droplets smaller than < 0.1 mm (Gil et al., 2014; Li et al., 2018; Wells, 1934; Yin et al., 2022). [SOURCE: Page_49]

48 [SOURCE: Page_49]

Figure 1.10. Lateral movement of water droplets (Hofman and Solseng, 2004) [SOURCE: Page_50]

Droplet evaporation 940      During the process of droplet movement, droplet evaporation take place under several physical and environmental factors including low relative humidity and high air temperature that cause a rapid reduction in the size and the weight of droplets, and consequently increase losses due to transport (Tobi et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_50]

Goering et al. (1972) presented the following equation to model the decrease in diameter D of a spherical droplet in the air due to in-flight evaporation (so called D$_2$ law), based on heat and mass transfer theory (Marshall, 1954; Ranz and Marshall, 1952a): [SOURCE: Page_50]

𝑑𝐷𝑝        𝑀𝑣 𝐾 𝜌𝑎 ∆𝑃 = −2(   )( )( )( )𝑁                                                                            Eq. 1-1 𝑑𝑡       𝑀𝑚 𝐷𝑝 𝑝𝑤 𝑃𝑓 𝑢 and [SOURCE: Page_50]

𝑁𝑢 = 2.0 + 0.6𝑅𝑒𝑝 1/2 𝑆𝑐1/3                                                                         Eq. 1-2 𝜈𝑎 𝑆𝑐 =                                                                                                Eq. 1-3 𝐾 [SOURCE: Page_50]

Where 𝐷𝑝 is droplet diameter in m; 𝑡 is time of flight in s; 𝑀𝑣 is the molecular weight of water vapor in g; 𝑀𝑚 is the molecular weight of air in g; 𝐾 is the diffusivity of vapor in air in m^2.s-1; 𝜌𝑎 is the density of air in kg.m-3; 𝑝𝑤 is the density of water droplet in kg.m-3; ∆𝑃 is the difference between saturation pressure 950   at the wet bulb temperature of air and vapor pressure at the dry bulb temperature in Pa; 𝑃𝑓 is the partial pressure of air in Pa. Classical correlation expressing 𝑁𝑢 number (Nusselt number) as a function of particle/ droplet Reynolds, 𝑅𝑒𝑝 (Eq. A- 3) and Schmidt number, 𝑆𝑐; and finally 𝜈𝑎 is kinematic viscosity of air in m^2.s-1 (Yan et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_50]

Note that the evaporation of droplets containing particles involves complicated multiphase heat and mass transport. The morphology of the dry aggregate particles are controlled by the Peclet number, 𝑃𝑒 (defined as the ratio of evaporation rate constant of pure water droplet to the particle mass diffusivity in [SOURCE: Page_50]

49 [SOURCE: Page_50]

the liquid phase [see Eq. A- 23 and Eq. A- 25]). Figure 1.11 shows the evolution of morphology during the evaporation process of a water suspension droplet. As evaporation proceeds, the particles are trapped at the droplet surface due to the droplet shrinking (Figure 1.11b). Then islands of particles are formed at the 960   droplet surface (see Figure 1.11c). For longer time, large islands and high-coverage patterns form a layer or a shell of nanoparticles (Figure 1.11d). Further evaporation leads to collapse of the shell and a solid structure is formed (Wei, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_51]

Figure 1.11. Morphological evolution from numerical simulation illustrating the evaporation dynamics of an aqueous droplet containing 200 nm polystyrene particles (Derkachov et al., 2008) [SOURCE: Page_51]

A variety of physical-mathematical modelling approaches have been proposed in the literature to describe droplet motion dynamics (Zerihun et al., 2016). The models are used to predict the uniformity and water distribution patterns of sprinklers within the wetted area. Moreover, these models are used to predict the kinetic energy influences of large droplets (> 3 mm) on runoff and soil erosion, as well as to predict the wind drift and evaporation losses of fine droplets (< 0.6–1 mm in diameter) (Edling, 1985; Kincaid et al., 1996). Lorenzini (2004) and Thompson et al. (1993) developed two reliable models based on travel distance and “time of flight” of sprinkler droplets in turbulent flow conditions. The results of these studies 970   showed reasonable agreement for cases in which the droplets have intermediate diameters in the range of 0.9–1.8 mm, while poor agreement was found for a droplet diameter of 0.3 mm. Therefore, for studies focusing on irrigation efficiency or water saving, volume percentages of sprayed droplets smaller than 0.1– 0.15 mm has been rarely estimated and analyzed in terms of their potential for bioaerosol transport (Hardy et al., 2006). For example, the model proposed by Pepper and Gerba (2018) for assessing the inhalation volume of recycled wastewater droplets from a sprinkler was derived from: i) the extrapolation of the exponential function and parameters provided by Kincaid et al. (1996) to determine the statistical distribution of the droplets smaller than 10 µm; ii) an empirical drift distance-droplet size model, which was only calibrated for a unique set of meteorological parameters. [SOURCE: Page_51]

50 [SOURCE: Page_51]

1.4.1.2. Wind speeds and direction 980      Upon a droplet released in the atmosphere from the sprinkler head, wind direction and its fluctuation is an important factor that dictates the fluctuation of direction of the transport. Regarding wind speed, it may affect the production of aerosols, in particular when the rotating jet is upwind and crosswind, and significantly contributes in the transport of aerosols as well as larger droplets (Fan et al., 2022; Molle et al., 2016; Xie et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_52]

Cornacchia et al. (2019) used a fogger as a spray generator with the volume median diameter of 0.11 mm to investigate the effect of wind speed on airborne transport of the spray under saturated conditions (above 90%) using a fluorescent tracer in a wind tunnel. The results showed that the mean transport increases from 0.13 to 1.18 L.h−1 m−2 at 7.7 m from the source as the wind velocity increases from 1 to 3 ms−1. [SOURCE: Page_52]

990      The most recommendations are to stop spraying if wind speeds are exceed 4 m.s-1 (ANSES, 2012; Molle et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_52]

1.4.1.3. Atmospheric stability The stability of the atmosphere is the major determinant of small droplet movement, on long-range transport (Miller and Stoughton, 2000). Atmospheric stability classifications are defined from extremely unstable (A) to the moderately stable (F) regarding wind speed, surface roughness, incoming solar radiation and cloud cover (EPA, 2000; Turner, 1997). [SOURCE: Page_52]

Although, the effect of the atmospheric stability is rarely taken into account in transport/ spray drift models such as CFD (Hong et al., 2021), and is mostly considered in horizontal and vertical a Gaussian plume model. [SOURCE: Page_52]

1000      One of the main limitations of wind energy research is that all extrapolation of data generally assumes a neutral, uniform wind profile. [SOURCE: Page_52]

Stable conditions occur mostly at night and early in the morning; unstable conditions occur during daytime periods with low winds; and neutral conditions occur during periods of relatively high wind speeds or overcast conditions. In fact, temperature inversion (stable atmospheric condition) can occur when solar radiation is no longer strong enough to heat the Earth's surface to a temperature equal to or greater than that of the surrounding atmosphere (Stull, 2015). This can result in a layer of cool and still air being trapped below warmer air, and the fine droplets can be concentrated in the cool layer close to the ground where they remain isolated from the surrounding weather conditions (Thistle, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_52]

Miller et al., (2000) addressed the interactions of the atmospheric stability and spray drift applied by an 1010   orchard air-blast sprayer. The static and dynamic stability was measured with fast response turbulence sensors. It was found that on average, the total amount of drift collected by the samplers in very stable [SOURCE: Page_52]

51 [SOURCE: Page_52]

conditions was 5.9, 3.6, and 2.1 times the amount of drift in unstable conditions for the plates, the high volume air samplers, and the strings, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Gil et al. (2007) found that in stable atmospheric conditions, the spray droplets preferentially drifted along the mean wind direction, whereas in unstable conditions, the sprayed plume was wider and more dispersed to higher altitudes during air-assisted spraying. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Tang et al. (2022) presented the results of CFD simulations of the velocity field and droplet movement (water droplets with 3% non-volatile) with different atmospheric stabilities in aerial application (release height = 5 m). The results indicated that droplets (most of them < 100 µm) in the stable conditions move 1020   more rapidly downwind than those in the unstable case by nearly 300% around at downwind distances of 100 m, 180 m, 700 m, and ∼200% at around 220 m and 550 m. However, droplets dispersed more easily in unstable conditions because of the greater turbulence kinetic energy. [SOURCE: Page_53]

These aspects will be discussed in more detail later in Chapter 5 (see 5.2.4). [SOURCE: Page_53]

1.4.1.4. Solar radiation Many parameters related to atmospheric dispersion (e.g., atmospheric stability and heat flux) depend on the incoming solar radiation and the radiation balance. It is therefore important to understand the radiation processes in the atmosphere which affect such parameters. As can be seen in Table 1.4, a mix of different wind conditions and solar radiation/vertical temperature gradient (for nighttime conditions) results into three basic states of the atmosphere: unstable, stable, and neutral, that largely influence the 1030   concentration of aerosols. Besides, solar UV radiation can potentially sterilize the ambient air from bioaerosols, a phenomenon more effective for airborne bacteria (Xie et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_53]

Table 1.4. Key to Solar Radiation Delta-T (SRDT) Method for Estimating Pasquill-Gifford (P-G) Stability Categories (EPA, 2000) DAYTIME Wind speed                                     Solar radiation (W.m-2) -1 (m.s )               ≥ 925               925–675               675–175                < 175 <2                   A                    A                      B                     D 2–3                   A                    B                      C                     D 3–5                   B                    B                      C                     D 5–6                   C                    C                      D                     D ≥6                   C                    D                      D                     D NIGHTTIME Wind speed                                  Vertical temperature gradient (m.s-1)                          <0                                          ≥0 <2.0                            E                                           F 2.0–2.5                           D                                           E ≥ 2.5                           D                                           D A, extremely unstable conditions; B, moderately unstable conditions; C, slightly unstable conditions; D, neutral conditions applicable to heavy overcast, day or night; E, slightly stable conditions; F, moderately stable conditions. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Previous field experiments of transport During the 1970s and 1980s, several field measurements were conducted, mostly in Israel (Applebaum 1040   et al., 1984; Bausum et al., 1983, 1982) and in the USA (Camann et al., 1988; Raynor and Hayes, 1976; Sorber et al., 1976) to estimate aerosolization by injecting a fluorescent dye into the irrigation system. Air sampling was performed at predetermined location downwind by active samplers such as AGI-30 or Andersen sampler (presented in sections 2.2.2.1 and 2.2.2.2) compared to calculations of the atmospheric diffusion model (Cramer, 1959) to predict the dye air concentration at each location, assuming complete aerosolization of sprayed water. The ratio between the measured and predicted dye concentrations gave a point estimate of the production of aerosols. In the case of sprinklers, depending on its type, configuration (nozzle size and operating pressure) and sampling position, the authors found that 0.1% to 2% and in one case up to 4.16% of sprayed wastewaters was aerosolized. So, pathogenic microorganisms encased in these airborne water may be spread and transported for hundreds of meters (Teltsch et al., 1980b) or end-up in 1050   dry aerosols under the influence of air evaporation potential. [SOURCE: Page_54]

In recent years, numerous studies have been carried out to develop field measurement of “pesticide spray drift”, that defines the off-target movement of pesticides by the action of air flux during an application using robust airborne sampling methods, currently standard polyethylene or polytetrafluoroethylene lines on which the pesticide deposits following ISO 22866 (2005) presented in the next chapter (section 2.2.1). This method is a good alternative to the transport measurements with dye tracers on a wide scale simultaneously. [SOURCE: Page_54]

Molle et al. (2016) characterized the transport from an irrigation rotating sprayer Rainbird 5000+ through this method, at two sampling heights (1 and 2 m) from the ground at a distance up to 4 times the sprinkler radius of throw, downwind of wetted area. [SOURCE: Page_54]

1060      The study continued by Tomas et al. (2019) under a wider range of operating pressure and meteorological conditions proposed an empirical modeling depending on meteorological parameters and dimensionless numbers that characterize atomization. [SOURCE: Page_54]

Atmospheric dispersion models Gaussian plume Gaussian plume model equations are widely used in atmospheric dispersion (diffusion+ transport) modelling, in particular to estimate three-dimensional concentration of microorganisms in aerosol from spray irrigation of wastewater (Courault et al., 2014; Hamilton et al., 2020, 2018; Katzenelson and Teltch, 1976; Nag et al., 2021; Sorber et al., 1976) to subsequently perform a quantitative microbial risk assessment, QMRA. The distribution of aerosol plume in both horizontal and vertical directions is assumed 1070   to be Gaussian or normal (Figure 1.12). Assuming steady-state approximation (i.e. no parameter is time- dependent) with a negligible wind shear effect on horizontal diffusion, steady state point source (sprinkler), [SOURCE: Page_54]

53 [SOURCE: Page_54]

homogenous, a negligible deposition rate of aerosols and background pollution (Abdel-Rahman, 2008), the standard Gaussian plume equation (with the term of the ground reflection) is expressed as : [SOURCE: Page_55]

𝑄                     𝑦2               −(𝑧 − 𝐻)2          −(𝑧 + 𝐻)2 𝐶(𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧) =                   . [𝑒𝑥𝑝 − (        )] . {𝑒𝑥𝑝 [          ] + 𝑒𝑥𝑝 [          ]}    Eq. 1-4 2𝜋𝑈𝜎𝑦 (𝑥)𝜎𝑧 (𝑥)           𝜎𝑦 (𝑥)2             2𝜎𝑧 (𝑥)2           2𝜎𝑧 (𝑥)2 [SOURCE: Page_55]

where 𝑄 is the emission rate, 𝑈 is the mean wind speed [m.s-1], 𝜎𝑦 (𝑥) and 𝜎𝑧 (𝑥) are the standard deviation of plume concentration distribution in the crosswind and vertical direction [m], 𝑥, 𝑦 are downwind direction along plume centerline, the distance in crosswind, and vertical direction, respectively, and 𝐻 is the height of plume centerline [m]. To predict the fraction of microorganisms that remain viable 𝑥 at a given distance from the source, the term of microorganism die-off factor [𝑒𝑥𝑝 − (𝜆. )] is multiplied 𝑈 [SOURCE: Page_55]

by the Eq. 1-4. The parameter 𝜆 denotes the decay (inactivation) rate of the viable microorganisms [s-1]. [SOURCE: Page_55]

1080 Figure 1.12. Gaussian distribution of aerosol plume in crosswind and vertical direction downwind from a sprinkler. h and d are the sprinkler height and downwind distance from sprinkler head to receptor, respectively. Other parameters have already been presented (US-EPA, 1982). [SOURCE: Page_55]

Until now, the various commercial advanced Gaussian dispersion models have been developed, some of them are : [SOURCE: Page_55]

•     U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA): [SOURCE: Page_55]

AERMOD (Preferred/Recommended Model), includes several improvements compared to standard Gaussian models such as, use of vertical continuous profiles of horizontal and vertical turbulence that are either based on measurements or computed based on similarity theory; simulation of buoyant or non- 1090   buoyant plumes; simulation of dispersion under convective and stable conditions; adapted to flow in the complex terrain. The plume is modeled as a combination of terrain-following and terrain-impacting states (Cimorelli et al., 2004). Dungan (2014) modeled the dispersion of the of the pathogens with AERMOD. [SOURCE: Page_55]

54 [SOURCE: Page_55]

Mori et al. (2021) applied AERMOD model to generate air concentrations of water aerosols during the irrigation season (May–September) for the years 2013–2018. [SOURCE: Page_56]

Screening Procedures, containing generated meteorological conditions which were not based on measured data such as, SCREEN$_3$ (provide ground-level concentrations for point, area, and volume sources and concentrations due to inversion break-up), TSCREEN (for toxic emissions), VISCREEN (calculation of the potential visual impact of a plume of specified emissions for specific transport and dispersion (meteorological) conditions), CTSCREEN (a screening technique for regulatory application to 1100   plume impaction assessments in complex terrain) (cited by US EPA (2017) website). [SOURCE: Page_56]

Note that SCREEN$_3$ can only simulate a single emission source and is most common model used for simulate worst-case scenarios (e.g. high wind speed) at a specified downwind distance from the source (Hardy et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_56]

•   UK-Cambridge Environmental Research Consultant (UK-CERC): [SOURCE: Page_56]

UK-ADMS, advanced steady state Gaussian-based dispersion model, comprises a number of individual models, simulates the dispersion of buoyant or neutrally buoyant gaseous and particulate emissions to the atmosphere. Each individual model deals with either one aspect of the dispersion process or data input and output and can be modified independently to keep it, and hence the whole model, scientifically up-to-date 1110   (Carruthers et al., 1994). It uses current knowledge of the structure of the atmospheric boundary layer, incorporating the Monin-Obukhov length and boundary layer depth (CERC, 2016). This model includes algorithms which take account for the following: non-point sources, continuous plumes, plume rise as a function of distance, jets and directional releases, gravitational settling and dry deposition, decay dose, short duration releases (< one hour) and transport over complex terrain (Douglas et al., 2016; Gloster et al., 2005; Taha et al., 2007). The model is applicable up to about 60 km downwind of the source and provides useful information for distances up to 100 km. [SOURCE: Page_56]

Generally, the Gaussian approach does not provide much resolution in the representation of near-field dynamics. More information about Gaussian plume models and their limitations at short length and time scales can be found in Stocker et al. (2018) and Woodward et al. (2021). [SOURCE: Page_56]

1120 [SOURCE: Page_56]

Lagrangian The Lagrangian approach, as discrete phase tracking, has been widely employed to simulate two phase flows, covering spatial scales up to thousands of kilometers from the release points. It assumes that each source emits a large number of particles/droplets into the air, and each particle follows a random path around the mean wind vector. In fact, the models calculate the individual trajectory of each particle/droplet [SOURCE: Page_56]

55 [SOURCE: Page_56]

with its own aerodynamic characteristics driven by deterministic (wind field and buoyancy) and stochastic (turbulence) effects (Leelőssy et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_57]

The path of particles is updated on each simulation time step as the particles evolve with time under various atmospheric processes. Either running forward or backward in time, this method is a powerful tool 1130   for tracking and understanding the fates, or origins, of particles in atmosphere. Hence, the Lagrangian models are more determinist than Gaussian models, which do not incorporate wind shear or inhomogeneity of the turbulence field, operate within a static spatial grid, as well as do not include evaporation as a factor. [SOURCE: Page_57]

In addition, they are not confined by stability classes or sigma curves, as in the case of Gaussian models and do not require artificial diffusivity, as do the Eulerian models for convective transport. However, a large amount of droplets/particles implies that the individual equations of each particle must be solved, involving significant computational effort and cost of this approach (Leelőssy et al., 2014). In the other hand, if the number of particles/droplets is not sufficient the models can produce discontinuous results and can become unreliable predictors of the major parameters influencing airborne transport according to droplet diameter. This approach has mostly used to simulate downwind deposition of spray drift in 1140   pesticide study purposes (De Cock et al., 2017; Gil and Sinfort, 2005; Teske et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_57]

A new version of the Lagrangian dispersion modelling system Micro-SWIFT-SPRAY (MSS developed by ARIA Technologies and ARIANET) has been developed in order to allow simulation of spray plume, microorganisms, insecticide or pesticide dispersion. (Lacome et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_57]

Puff models Puff models are the intersection between Gaussian and Lagrangian models, assuming that the emission of a stack can be subdivided in a series of puffs with a given volume. Each puff behaves according to the Gaussian dispersion equation and follows the local wind speed and wind direction independently of other puffs, while above the puff size, Lagrangian trajectories are calculated (Holmes and Morawska, 2006; Hong et al., 2021). The most commercial Puff models used in long-range atmospheric dispersion and 1150   include the DERMA, RIMPUFF and NAME in Europe and the Fluent, CALPUFF, INPUFF, OMEGA in the USA (Van Leuken et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_57]

Eulerian Eulerian models are based on a grid that is fixed in space. In each grid point the advection (i.e., wind speed) and dispersion of pollutants is calculated based on local concentration gradients. To illustrate the concept of Eulerian models, a three-dimensional atmospheric transport equation (advection-diffusion) can be expressed as: [SOURCE: Page_57]

𝜕𝐶      𝜕𝐶    𝜕𝐶    𝜕𝐶 𝜕   𝜕𝐶   𝜕     𝜕𝐶  𝜕   𝜕𝐶                                                     Eq. 1-5 =𝑄−𝑢    −𝑣    −𝑤   + [𝑘𝑥 ] +    [𝑘𝑦 ] + [𝑘𝑧 ] 𝜕𝑡    ⏟𝜕𝑥     𝜕𝑦    𝜕𝑧 ⏟ 𝜕𝑥  𝜕𝑥   𝜕𝑦    𝜕𝑦  𝜕𝑧  𝜕𝑧 𝑎𝑑𝑣𝑒𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛                          𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓𝑢𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_57]

56 [SOURCE: Page_57]

This equation represents the change of the concentration at a specified point in time as the sum of the emission rate (source term, 𝑄) [m^3.s−1], the transport via advection by the mean wind speed [m.s−1] (𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑤) in directions [𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧], and the transport via diffusion by turbulent eddy motions where 𝑘 is the eddy 1160   diffusivity in m^2.s−1. [SOURCE: Page_58]

In this approach, the plume is not modeled as a single entity, so that transport over long distance can be modeled with relative accuracy. Although these models have low resolution, often 10-km cell size or more, and the computational resources needed increases very rapidly with increasing resolution. Furthermore, the physics underlying Eulerian dispersion models, gradient transport theory, breaks down at short distances (a few kilometers) from the source, where the size of the largest turbulent eddies is not negligible in comparison with the distance between the source and the receptor (De Visscher, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_58]

Eulerian descriptions are complicate at the beginning of the simulation, due to an increased analytical complication, but prove to be more practical afterwards as the grid does not change with time (De Wrachien and Lorenzini, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_58]

1170           Computational fluid dynamics Computational fluid dynamic (CFD) models provide complex analysis of fluid flow based on conservation of mass and momentum by resolving the Navier-Stokes equation using finite difference and finite volume methods in three dimensions. CFD methods are usually used in combination with Lagrangian aerosol tracking to simulate the transport of particles at smaller scales. [SOURCE: Page_58]

For the particulate/droplet phase, the governing equation for the simplified drift–flux model can be written as: [SOURCE: Page_58]

𝜕𝐶𝑖                                                                                                Eq. 1-6 + 𝛻. [(𝑈 + 𝑉𝑡𝑠𝑖 )𝐶𝑖 ] = 𝑄𝐶𝑖 + 𝛻. [(𝐷𝐵𝑖 + 𝜀𝑝 )𝛻𝐶𝑖 ] 𝜕𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_58]

where 𝐶𝑖 is the particle mass concentration [kg. m−3] (or number concentration, m−3) of particle size group i, 𝑉𝑡𝑠𝑖 is the particle settling velocity, 𝜀𝑝 is the particle eddy diffusivity and 𝐷𝐵𝑖 is the Brownian diffusion coefficient. [SOURCE: Page_58]

1180      It should be mentioned that CFD can in principle calculate both the full flow field and droplet movement from spray break-up to deposition. Droplet movement can be calculated using either a Eulerian or a Lagrangian approach. However, the cost of resolving all the required scales in a single calculation would be very high. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Empirical modeling Considering the multivariable nature of the question addressed, it is so complicated to approach deterministic answers, that an empirical approach, based on statistics inference, has been sometimes [SOURCE: Page_58]

57 [SOURCE: Page_58]

preferred (Tomas et al., 2019; US-EPA, 1982). The empirical model proposed by Tomas et al. (2019) is based on the use of the environmental variables. It will be presented in the Chapter 5. [SOURCE: Page_59]

Conclusion 1190 In this chapter, several researches concerning the reuse of treated wastewater distributed via spray/sprinkler irrigation have been briefly reviewed. We have discussed on the impact of operating and field parameters on the possible dispersion of contaminant in the case of sprinkler irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_59]

A number of numerical and experimental approaches based on ballistics or fluid dynamics are concerned with dispersion mechanisms, but the volume of droplets smaller than 0.15 mm that escapes from the wetted radius with an application rate of less than 0.26 mm.h-1 are rarely estimated and analyzed. In fact, the atomization process and the movement of aerosol droplets generated from a sprinkler jet, in field experiments under unstable meteorological conditions, generally appears different from what was predicted by simple ballistic models. The same applies when measuring the droplet size distribution in 1200   calm/laboratory conditions to precisely understand what happens in the field. [SOURCE: Page_59]

Most of the previously cited field studies focused on punctual measurements of airborne transport by injecting a fluorescent dye into the irrigation system, thus were limited in terms of number of measurement points. The same appears for measurement heights (1 or 2 meters above the ground) when using passive samplers (PVC Strings, see 2.2.1). [SOURCE: Page_59]

Among the different models applicable to reproduce aerosols dispersion, the Gaussian steady-state models is the most widely used to simulate aerosol atmospheric dispersion (diffusion+ transport). In the case of aerosols generated by irrigation sprinklers such models are not adapted to low wind conditions or distances inferior to 100 m from the emission area (sprinkler wetted perimeter). [SOURCE: Page_59]

Thus, one of the objectives of this thesis is to review methods and conduct field experimentations with 1210   different sampling methods to qualify fine droplets dispersion in atmospheric conditions at distances inferior to 100 m from an impact sprinkler, and propose empirical models validated against experimental data to estimate airborne spray transport and surface sedimentation (see Chapter 5) [SOURCE: Page_59]

Various atmospheric dispersion models were investigated in this chapter. In recent years, many efforts were also made to simulate the particles transport using the computation fluid dynamic (CFD), in particular, for different COVID-19 problems (Mohamadi and Fazeli, 2022). For the case of sprinkler irrigation, we suggest further investigations applying the Eulerian Drift-Flux model (considering a spray plume in the size range of 1E-04–0.15 mm) to simulate airborne transport and distribution. [SOURCE: Page_59]

58 [SOURCE: Page_59]

Air sampling methods–Overview [SOURCE: Page_60]

1220        Introduction Accurate and reliable quantification and detection of aerosols from TWW distributed by sprinkler irrigation require air sampling methods that efficiently capture representative samples, match the targeted bio/aerosol (e.g., particle size, stress–sensitivity, and concentration level) as well as appropriate analysis techniques (Alvarez et al., 1995). [SOURCE: Page_60]

The purpose of this chapter was to review the common sampling methods developed in the different domains and to examine the possibility of using them for quantifying the amount of aerosols from sprinkler irrigation with TWW. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Note that some fundamentals are given in Appendix. A to better understand the aerosol motions. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Principal aerosol sampling techniques 1230      Aerosols samplers are generally classified into two categories: passive (deposit on a surface without an air pump or external power) and active (involving a mechanical component such as vacuum pump) samplers as described in the following sub–sections. It should be highlighted that the results from passive and active samplers should not be assumed comparable. Among other techniques, we can mention the optical sampling captures such as aerodynamic particle sizer, scanning mobility particle counter, which are capable of detecting total count of viable bacterial particles or viable + non-viable in real time as well as LIDAR technique for pesticide spray drift studies. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Passive samplers Passive samplers are the most readily available, inexpensive and easy to set up that allow covering very large spatial scales (Lindsley et al., 2017; Manibusan and Mainelis, 2022). [SOURCE: Page_60]

1240      Passive samplers have been designed/reported for measuring Particulate Matter (PM, includes microscopic matter suspended in air) concentration in environmental exposure studies (see Figure 2.1). [SOURCE: Page_60]

59 [SOURCE: Page_60]

Agar settling plate (Phillips and Runkle, 2019; Rodriguez et al., 2017), Vinzents (Jacobsen et al., 2013; Vinzents, 1996), UNC-PAS sampler (Arashiro and Leith, 2013; Castillo et al., 2019; Wagner and Leith, 2001a, 2001b), and Electrostatic dust fall collector, EDC (Delort and Amato, 2017; Kilburg-Basnyat et al., 2016; Zahradnik et al., 2011) are among the common devices that can be mentioned (Figure 2.1). Ideally, these techniques would be useful in large epidemiological and ecological studies with high replication necessary (especially for industrial and urban areas) (Adams et al., 2015). However, it should be noted that most of these sampling methods capture a priori airborne PM after the gravitational settling (e.g. UNC- PAS) or an electrostatic precipitator (EDC), while ambient aerosol particles are generally heterogeneous 1250   and depending on physical properties, as discussed in the Appendix. A. Besides, the environmental conditions such as air velocity, humidity, and temperature also influence their behavior. Thus, the methods are still under development in particular for the detection of ultrafine particles (Nash and Leith, 2010) and bioaerosols inferior to 5 µm such as viruses, bacteria, most fungi, and smaller fragments or byproducts of biological particles (Reponen et al., 2011; Therkorn et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_61]

(a)                                             (b)                      (c) Figure 2.1. Passive samplers for measuring Particulate Matter. (a) Vinzents sampler, (b) UNC-PAS sampler, and (c) Electrostatic dust fall collector [SOURCE: Page_61]

Numerous studies have been carried out to obtain field measurements of “pesticide spray drift” that define the off-target movement of pesticides by the action of air flux during an application using a wide 1260   range of passive samplers such as: [SOURCE: Page_61]

−   Strings: Cotton strings or cord (Martin et al., 2019; Whitney and Roth, 1985), nylon string (Fritz, 2006; Kirk, 1999), Soda straws (Fritz et al., 2006), wire (Riley and Wiesner, 1990), and standard polyethylene or polytetrafluoroethylene strings, (Ø1.98 or 2 mm) (Al Heidary et al., 2014; Gil et al., 2007; Nuyttens et al., 2010; Torrent et al., 2017); [SOURCE: Page_61]

−   Flat tapes (Fox et al., 1990; SDTF, 1994); [SOURCE: Page_61]

−   Nylon screens (Salcedo et al., 2021) [SOURCE: Page_61]

−    Water-sensitive papers (Panneton et al., 2005; Chen et al., 2013; Xue et al., 2021; Gregorio et al., 2016a); [SOURCE: Page_61]

60 [SOURCE: Page_61]

−    Filter papers (Qi et al., 2008; Bueno et al., 2017; Brusselman et al., 2004; Gregorio et al., 1270               2016a), and filter cloths (Brusselman et al., 2004); [SOURCE: Page_62]

−    Blotting papers (Garcerá et al., 2017) ; [SOURCE: Page_62]

−    Paper cleaner (Kasner et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_62]

There is a complex interaction between the size of the droplets, the passive samplers in its path, and their relative velocity (Matthews et al., 2014). Several studies have been conducted across the world in order to evaluate collection efficiency (𝜂𝑐 ) of passive samplers with different shape and physical characteristics for small droplets (<100 μm) traveling in different air velocities as well as their recovering efficiency for tracer materials captured from their surface (Miller, 2003). Note that for small droplets (<100 μm) suspended in air, and ignoring gravitational, electrostatic, and any other external forces, the collection efficiency (𝜂𝑐 ) of a passive sampler is a combination of inertial impaction efficiency, interception 1280   efficiency, and diffusion efficiency (only for droplets less than 1 micron in size). [SOURCE: Page_62]

Most of the theoretical and experimental studies of collection efficiency of passive samplers have been focused on inertial impaction. Table 2.1 represents some of the empirical equations to estimate airborne droplet collection efficiency of passive samplers. [SOURCE: Page_62]

Miller and Hadfield (1989) concluded that passive collectors (for pesticide spray drift studies) should not be used if local wind speeds are less than 2 m.s-1. [SOURCE: Page_62]

Parkin and Young (2000) evaluated the collection efficiency of cylindrical lines of various diameters (1 to 10 mm) in a wind tunnel, exposing the lines to the plumes of electrically neutral monosized droplets of various diameters (10 to 63 µm) at a range of known air velocities (1 to 4 m.s-1). They found that when [SOURCE: Page_62]
