1290   the collection efficiency of the lines was plotted against Stokes number (𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 ) the results did not collapse to a single sigmoidal curve above 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 > 1, which did not follow the May and Clifford’s model. In addition, the results indicated the collection efficiency reached a series of limiting values that decreased with line diameter. They used also a time-dependent computational fluid dynamics (CFD) to examine collection efficiency if the adhesion of a droplet to a surface can be predicted or discounted (Figure 2.2). [SOURCE: Page_63]

Figure 2.2. Droplet trajectories upstream of a 10 mm diameter cylinder in a 4 m.s-1 free airstream simulated using computational fluid dynamics. (a) 1 μm droplets. (b) 35 μm droplets. Data from Parkin and Young (2000) [Bateman et al. 2014]. [SOURCE: Page_63]

Herbst and Molnar (2002) concluded that cylindrical collectors with a diameter of 2 mm, characterized 1300   by a smooth and well-defined surface, were the most suitable collectors for aerial spray drift. [SOURCE: Page_63]

Fox et al. (2004) found that the collection efficiency of monofilament nylon screens depends on droplet size and wind speed. [SOURCE: Page_63]

Gil et al. (2005) concluded that under a wind speed of less than 3.5 m.s-1, the efficiency of 2-mm PVC plastic lines was higher than 77%. [SOURCE: Page_63]

Fritz and Hoffmann (2008) reported a comparison of soda straw collectors and a monofilament line, with the latter having a collection efficiency increased from 52.3% to 82.6% with an increase in air velocity from 0.45 to 4.0 m.s-1. This protocol was tested by comparing the theoretically and experimentally derived of soda straw collectors. Results showed no statistical difference between the theoretical and experimental 𝜂𝑐 at each air velocity tested. As the air velocity increased from 0.45 to 4.0 m.s-1, the 𝜂𝑐 for the 1310   monofilament line increased from 52.3% to 82.6%, respectively, while the 𝜂𝑐 of the soda straw also increased from 6.7% to 45%, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_63]

Therefore, 2 mm standard polyethylene or polytetrafluoroethylene lines (ISO 22856, 2008; ISO 22866, 2005) is a good alternative to the pesticide spray drift and transport measurements with dye tracers on a wide scale simultaneously. [SOURCE: Page_63]

It should be mentioned that under still or low-windy conditions, even small droplets will fall by gravity on to a horizontal surface after traveling a distance (Pulley and Walters, 1990). Among the passive samplers commonly used to measure the sedimented spray on the ground and mounted horizontally that can be cited are: Petri dishes, filter papers, paper surface and microscope slides (ISO 22856, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_63]

62 [SOURCE: Page_63]

Active samplers 1320      Currently, numerous types of active samplers are available for collection of bio/aerosols from both outdoor and indoor environments (Cage et al., 1996; Verreault et al., 2008). They employ various collection mechanisms, including impaction, impingement, filtration, cyclone, Condensation technique, and electrostatic precipitation (Ghosh et al. 2015; Haig et al. 2016; Lindsley et al. 2017), some of them are illustrated in Figure 2.3 and three of the most common ones have been reviewed in this chapter. Mainelis (2020) also summarized the current state of the art in bioaerosol research with emphasis on bioaerosol sampling, and the need to develop a more standardized set of guidelines for protocols used in bioaerosol research. Moreover, more information about the several commercial samplers have been analyzed in Yates et al., (2016), which can be found in (Appendix. B). Verreault et al. (2008) has also reviewed the different samplers applied to detect viruses in bioaerosols. [SOURCE: Page_64]

(a)                (b)             (c)             (d)                 (e)                   (f) Figure 2.3.(a) Cascade impactor, (b) Slit sampler (Impactor), (c) SKC BioSampler (Impinger), (d) Coriolis μ air sampler (cyclone), (e) Cassette samplers and filters (filter), (f) Electrostatic precipitator. [SOURCE: Page_64]

1330   2.2.2.1. Impaction The impaction method separates particles from the air by utilizing the inertia of the particles to force their deposition onto a solid or semi-solid surface. The collection surface for bioaerosol sampling is usually an agar medium (Yates et al., 2016). It changes the flow into an abrupt 90° turn and particles with sufficient inertia are not capable of following the change in the direction of streamlines and collide with the surface. The cross-section of a stage of impactor is shown in Figure 2.4. The history of the impactor development has been reviewed by Marple (2004). Slit impactors with a single suction nozzle in a rectangular shape have been widely used in agricultural and domestic environments with high contamination, and employed for detecting large airborne particles such as large fungal spores and pollen grains (Ghosh et al., 2015). Two criteria are considered to design a conventional single slit sampler: 1) the jet-to-plate (agar) distance 1340   divided by nozzle diameter, S/W, should be larger than 1.5; 2) The Reynolds number of the air flow in the jet nozzle throat should be between 500 and 3000 (Grinshpun et al., 2007). Although, unlike Sieve- impactors and Cascade impactors, slit impactors do not provide information about the particle size. Cascade impactors such as six-stage Andersen cascade impactor (Andersen, 1958) are most commonly used to measure particle size distribution (Nichols et al., 2013) and to simulate cultivable bioaersol concentrations in different region of human respiratory system. The stages have progressively smaller [SOURCE: Page_64]

63 [SOURCE: Page_64]

nozzles, which create higher particle impaction velocities onto the culture plate, “nasal cavity” = stage 1: 7 µm and above, “Pharynx” = stage 2: 4.7–7 µm, “Trachea and Primary Bronci”= stage 3: 3.3–4.7 µm, “Secondary Bronchi” = stage 4: 2.1–3.3 µm, “Terminal Bronchi” = stage 5: 1.1–2.1 µm, and “Alveoli” = stage 6: 0.6–1.1 µm (Xu and Yao, 2013). The impactor MOUIDI (Micro orifice Uniform Deposit Impactor) 1350   collect the particles down to 0.056 μm in aerodynamic diameter, and gives a uniform particle deposit on the plates (Marple et al., 1991). [SOURCE: Page_65]

The dominant parameter describing the collection efficiency of an impactor is the Stokes number (Yao and Mainelis, 2006). The cut-off size (𝑑50 ) is related to the Stokes number (i.e., particle inertia), 𝑆𝑡𝑘50, that gives 50% collection efficiency. It means that the mass of particles below cut-off size collected equals the mass of particles getting through larger than the cut-off. Marple and Olson (2011) determined the cut- off size, 𝑑50 , using the following equation, [SOURCE: Page_65]

9𝜇𝑎 𝑊                                                                                Eq. 2-1 𝑑50 = √            √𝑆𝑡𝑘50 𝜌𝑝 𝑈𝑛 𝐶𝑐 [SOURCE: Page_65]

where 𝑈𝑛 is the average air velocity from “n” separate nozzles at the same stage (multiple–stage impaction), and as mentioned above, 𝑊 is average nozzle diameter (or width) for a given stage. The recommended values for √𝑆𝑡𝑘50 is 0.49 for circular jet (Hinds, 1999). Note that, the shape and the density 1360   of particles are unknown in most cases for a given aerosol, in particular, in field studies. Particle size at each stage of the impactor is therefore expressed through an aerodynamic diameter. The density of particles is also considered equal to one. [SOURCE: Page_65]

Figure 2.4. Schematic of cross-section of a stage of impactor depicting the trajectories of particles of different sizes; W is the nozzle exit diameter, and S is the distance from the nozzle exit to collection surface (Drawn by Zardari). [SOURCE: Page_65]

Yao and Mainelis (2006) evaluated the cut-off size of seven impactors (SMA, BioCulture, MAS-100, Microflow, SAS Super 180, Millipore Air Tester, and RSC High Flow). The results showed that for most samplers, the theoretical 𝑑50 was lower than the experimental value, which was likely due to the dissipation [SOURCE: Page_65]

64 [SOURCE: Page_65]

1370   of impactor jets and the influence of cross-flow in the multi-nozzle impactors. All samplers collected 10% or less of 0.5 µm particles (bacterial cells) onto the agar medium, but they would be more efficient when collecting larger fungal spores. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Despite the advantages of the inertial impactors as a particle size classifier and direct collection of bioaerosols onto culture plates that results in reduction of post-sampling processing, there are also several limitations. The bioaerosol stress during the samplings such as shear forces on the bioaerosols within the nozzle and on impaction with the agar plates (Cox et al., 2020), the desiccation of pathogens on solid surface in high velocity impactions or sampling time (Mainelis and Tabayoyong, 2010), overloading of agar plates with colonies can lead to counting errors and microbial growth inhibition effects (Marple and Olson, 2011; Reponen et al., 2011), influence the bio-efficiency of impactors. The losses of re-entrainment 1380   and particle bounce after impaction, especially for particles larger than the cut-off diameter affect the collection efficiency (Lindsley et al., 2017). The particle bounce is particularly a problem with dry solid collection surfaces, which can be overcome by mineral-oil-spread on agar plates (Xu et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_66]

A virtual impactor is a type of inertial impactor, but with the difference that instead of an impaction plate, it has a collection probe that divides the minor flow and the major flow inside the virtual impactor (Lim et al., 2020). The presence of collection probe prevents issues of particle bounce, overload, and re- entrainment. Furthermore, the minor flow concentrate the particles of a certain size, which aspirate from the impactor inlet. However, virtual impactors are subjected to collection losses near to the size of the cut- off diameter at the inlet of the probe (Haig et al., 2016). The researches for improving the performance the virtual impactors have been investigated in Lim et al. (2020). [SOURCE: Page_66]

1390   2.2.2.2. Impingement The impingement technique is similar to impaction expect that the aerosols are collected into a liquid medium (Figure 2.5), which make it more effective at preserving the viability of sensitive viruses (Harstad, 1965; Verreault et al., 2008). Most impingers have a curved inlet tube designed to mimic human nasal passage, making these samplers useful for studying the respiratory infection potential of bioaerosols (Yates et al., 2016). The sample can be directly analyzed with culture and analytical methods without the need to extract the microorganisms from a surface or filter (Xu et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_66]

Stephenson et al. (2004) found that the impingers appear to detect 2–10 times greater airborne endotoxin than glass fiber or polycarbonate filters, when comparing the sampling methods in a wastewater treatment plant. [SOURCE: Page_66]

1400      However, the evaporation of collection liquid (buffered water medium) during the sampling can have an important impact on viability or inactivation of microorganisms. This is the main problem of most currently available impingers (Henningson et al., 1988). Re-aerosolization also occurs when collected airborne particles in the collection liquid are re-entrained into the airflow, leading to the loss of collection efficiency. Turbulent motion of collection liquid during sampling provides enough energy for the already [SOURCE: Page_66]

65 [SOURCE: Page_66]

collected particles to become re-entrained into rising bubbles (Grinshpun et al., 1997; Han and Mainelis, 2012; Lin and Li, 1999; Riemenschneider et al., 2010; willeke et al., 1998). In addition, the particles (large particles with sufficient inertia) that impact onto bottom of the impinger may be rebound into the airflow because of low collection liquid volume or high sampling flow rate. Therefore, for preventing particle bounce, Lin et al. (1997) proposed to coat the bottom surface with 0.1 to 0 .2 mL of petroleum jelly/ hexane 1410   solution. [SOURCE: Page_67]

Chen et al. (2021) and Hermann et al. (2006) have discussed on the parameters affecting the impinger collection efficiency. Among these parameters, the following could be mentioned: impinger design (Kesavan et al., 2010), sampling flow rate (Lin and Li, 1998), composition of collection medium (liquid) (Chang and Chou, 2011), sampling time (Mainelis and Tabayoyong, 2010), aerodynamic diameter of airborne particles (Dart and Thornburg, 2008; Yu et al., 2016), morphology of pathogens (Tseng and Li, 2005), and ambient or collection medium temperature (Springorum et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_67]

Figure 2.5. Airborne particles collected by impinger into the liquid (Drawn by Zardari) [SOURCE: Page_67]

Impinger design– Among the various type of the devices for air sampling into liquid (see Appendix. 1420   B), all-glass impingers (Ace Glass Inc., Vineland, NJ) and BioSampler (SKC Inc., Eighty Four, PA) are two types of the most used samplers (Figure 2.6). All glass impingers (AGI) collects airborne particles by their impingement vertically through a nozzle into a liquid collection. The distance between the jet nozzle outlet and the surface of the liquid as well as the airflow influence the diameter of the particles and the viability of microorganisms that will be collected (Haig et al., 2016). May and Harper (1957) found that AGI-30 (its jet nozzle placed at 30 mm from the bottom of the collection vessel) preserved collected viable bacteria (S. marcescens) better than AGI-4 (at 4 mm from the collection vessel). Henningson et al. (1988) likewise reported that when only collection efficiency is considered, AGI-4 is more preferable than AGI- 30 (> 95% and = 97 compared to > 70% and = 85%, if Dp > 0.5 µm and Dp=0.9 µm, respectively). Nevertheless, a significantly lower survival Escherichia coli (0.6 ± 0.2) was found in the AGI-4 compared 1430   to the AGI-30. Lin et al. (1997) confirmed this fact that the AGI-30 collects sub-micrometer particles considerably less efficiently than the AGI-4, but may causes less biological stress on the collected [SOURCE: Page_67]

66 [SOURCE: Page_67]

microorganisms. Either way, the bubble bursts is the most detrimental to bacterial viability when tested with both impingers. [SOURCE: Page_68]

BioSampler that combined the forces of impingement and centrifugation was developed to generate very few bubbles and therefore causes minimal re-aerosolization of particles and impact on viability of already collected microorganisms (Kesavan et al., 2010; Lin et al., 2000; willeke et al., 1998). Therefore, it frequently used as a reference when investigating other samplers for collection of viruses (Anderson et al., 2016; Cao et al., 2011; Fabian et al., 2009; Kasner et al., 2018; Li et al., 2018; Su et al., 2020; Zheng and Yao, 2017). Most recently, the presence of SARS-CoV-2 in air was determined through SKC 1440   BioSampler (Ahn et al., 2020; Kenarkoohi et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_68]

Hermann et al. (2006) investigated the collection of aerosolized porcine reproductive and respiratory syndrome virus (PRRSV) in air-sampling systems, and analyzed the samples by quantitative reverse transcriptase PCR (QRT-PCR). As estimated by QRT-PCR, the mean collection efficiency of PRRSV were 0.022, 0.016 and 0.009 for the SKC BioSampler (12.5 L.min-1), AGI-30 (12.5 L.min-1) and AGI-4 (6.0 L.min-1), respectively. [SOURCE: Page_68]

Figure 2.6. Schematic diagram of the impingers: (a) SKC BioSampler, (b) AGI-4 or -30 (Drawn by Zardari). [SOURCE: Page_68]

Aerodynamic diameter of airborne particles– The lowest cutoff diameter of the most common 1450   impingers was about 0.3 µm (Dart and Thornburg, 2008). For the common liquid impingers (AGI-30 impinger, SKC BioSampler and a frit bubbler), Hogan et al. (2005) indicated that the collection efficiency of particles in the range of 30–100 nm, is inferior to 10 % because of their insufficient inertia to be collected in liquid and high diffusion and small inertial requirement to transfer into bubbles from liquid. It should be mentioned that the common size of a virus is fallen into this range. The following solutions were therefore provided to enhance the efficiency of the impingers for the collection of nanoparticles: [SOURCE: Page_68]

67 [SOURCE: Page_68]

-   Yu et al. (2016) found that applying 3 mm packed glass beads in vessel collection can be successfully improved the efficiency of AGI30 sampler for the collection of ultrafine particles in the size range of 20–400 nm and viral aerosols (MS$_2$). Chen et al., (2021) also reported that the tracer (0.15% Uranine as a tracer) collection efficiency of the AGI-30 samplers without and with 1460          packing glass beads ranged from 1.20 ± 0.46 to 14.09 ± 3.41% and from 1.91 ± 1.27 to 44.49 ± 15.86%, respectively, under various sampling times, collection liquid, and sampling flowrate. While the PFU (viable Bacteriophages MS$_2$) collection efficiency of the AGI-30 sampler without and with packing ranged from 0.62 ± 0.06 to 6.41 ± 1.04% and from 0.59 ± 0.05 to 11.57 ± 3.55%, respectively. The lower recovery of viable MS$_2$ compared to the tracer could be due to the higher-pressure drop across the impinger. [SOURCE: Page_69]

-   Lin et al. (2018) paired the steam-jet aerosol collector (SJAC), which is based on condensation growth method, with AGI-30 and SKC BioSampler to collect viral aerosol (MS$_2$ bacteriophage around 28 nm). The results indicated that the system was 7 and 22 times more efficient for viability preservation of MS$_2$ bacteriophage than the commonly used AGI-30 and BioSampler, respectively. 1470          In addition, the system applied at a low aerosol concentration (105 PFU.m-3) was 4.7 times more efficient for viability preservation than using AGI-30 alone. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Note that the size of a virus does not represent the size of the bioaerosol sprayed from irrigation of treated wastewater, which contains many components such as proteins and cellular debris and larger particles in addition to the virus (Cox et al., 2020). Stobnicka-Kupiec et al. (2022) cited that the highest concentrations of airborne noroviruses and rotaviruses at workplaces in wastewater treatment plants were noted for particles with diameters of > 4.5 µm and 9 µm, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Sampling time– The impingers are not sensitive to under or overloading during sampling because the liquid sample can be diluted or concentrated after sample collection, but due to evaporation of collection liquid resulting in loss of viability and reaerosolization of already collected particles, sampling time should 1480   be carefully selected (Chattopadhyay, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_69]

Lin and Li (1998) found that the relative survival of fungal spores collected in AGI-30 impingers became lower as the sampling time and flow rates increased. Total recovery of the AG$_1$-30 impinger at the flow rate of 12.5 L.min-1, was 24 ± 9% for 1min samples and decreased to 3.9 ± 2.5% for 60 min samples. In fact, the selection of appropriate sampling time depends on the resistance of the microorganisms to stress and the following parameters: composition of collection medium, sampling flow rate, temperature and relative humidity. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Composition of collection medium– Several liquid media have been used for sampling aerosols in impingers. The choice of suitable sampling liquid is important because each liquid has its own specifics and subsequently, its advantages and drawbacks. The choice of the medium is also dependent on the 1490   targeted pathogen. Most used liquid media in previous experiments are as follow: [SOURCE: Page_69]

68 [SOURCE: Page_69]

−   Filtered/ sterilized deionized water as a common collection medium for microorganisms (Chang et al., 2010; Chang and Chou, 2011; Miljevic et al., 2009; Zheng and Yao, 2017) [SOURCE: Page_70]

−   Mineral oil as a non-evaporating liquid which can be used for long- term sampling, and because of its high viscosity and low freezing point (Springorum et al., 2011; willeke et al., 1998), [SOURCE: Page_70]

−   Peptone water as a nutrient broths for stabilizing the collected airborne viruses, maintaining the viability of the microbial cells and increasing their detectability (Hemmes et al., 1960; Lin and Li, 1998), [SOURCE: Page_70]

−   Phosphate-buffered saline based surfactant (Dybwad et al., 2014; Fabian et al., 2009; Veillette et al., 2018), and [SOURCE: Page_70]

1500           −   Tween 80 mixture (deionized water with 1% peptone, 0.01% Tween 80 and 0.005% antifoam A) (Chang and Chou, 2011; Chen and Li, 2005; Lange et al., 1997). Tween 80 could reduce the surface tension and decrease the impact on microorganisms (Chen et al., 2021). Besides, antifoam is generally added to prevent excessive liquid losses and in dampening foam formation (Lembke et al., 1981). [SOURCE: Page_70]

Concerning the deionized water, Rule et al., (2009) observed that PBS yields higher (p <= 0.01) viable counts compared to sterile deionized water. However, Chang et al. (2010) demonstrated that deionized water obtained more culturable L. pneumophila than Tween mixture by a factor of 4.1–12.5 in SKC BioSampler and 2.2–6.8 in AGI-30 during 3–60 min sampling (P <0.0001). Moreover, deionized water replenishment every 15 min during sampling significantly improved the recovery of culturable L. 1510   pneumophila in both AGI-30 and BioSampler by a factor of 3.3 and 7.1, respectively (P=0.001). Tseng and Li (2005) also found that among the three evaluated collection media (sterile deionized water, nutrient broth, and peptone broth), deionized water provided better virus infectivity retention at 4 and 25 °C. [SOURCE: Page_70]

The use of mineral oils are suitable due to its minimal evaporation, but, willeke et al., (1998) reported that the AGI-30 impinger cannot be used with high viscosity liquids due to formation of large bubbles. Therefore, the collection efficiency of samplers such as SKC BioSampler (which can use glycerol) in conditions with high ambient temperature and for long-term sampling may be more suitable than AGI-30. Nevertheless, Springorum et al., (2011) evaluated the effect of temperature and relative humidity on the final volume of three liquid media (mineral oil, peptone water, and PBS) during 30 min of sampling with AGI-30. The results showed that mineral oil has the lowest losses, followed by PBS and peptone water. [SOURCE: Page_70]

1520      Sampling flow rate– The change of impinger flow rates could change the cut-off diameter (Asking and Olsson, 1997), bubble formation and re-aerosolization of collected particles (Grinshpun et al., 1997). The results of Riemenschneider et al. (2010) showed that re-aerosolization of MS$_2$ bacteriophage (around 28 nm in diameter) from AGI-30 increases as flow rate increases. [SOURCE: Page_70]

69 [SOURCE: Page_70]

For viral sampling, Zheng and Yao (2017) showed that higher sampling flow rate, i.e., 30 L.min-1, together with 5 mL non-evaporating liquid may perform much better than the recommended standard operating conditions (12 L.min-1) when using the BioSampler. In contrast, for fungal spores, lower sampling flow rate with smaller amount of collection liquid could be a better option. For bacterial aerosols, the recommended standard option could be the choice. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Raynor et al. (2021) reported that high flow rate generally collected greater quantities of virus than low 1530   flow rate when using six impinger/cyclone air samplers. However, low flow rate samplers generally measured higher and likely more accurate, airborne concentrations of infectious virus and viral RNA than high flow samplers. To assess airborne viruses in the field, a two-sampler approach with different flow rates may work well. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Temperature or relative humidity-controlled – High relative humidity is helpful to improve the collection process of airborne nanoparticles (Su et al., 2020). Springorum et al. (2011) insulated the AGI- 30 with an holder and controlled the temperature of the collection fluid by means of a Peltier element, under laboratory and field conditions. The results showed that tempering strongly affected the residual volume of the liquid (i.e. reduced evaporation) after sampling and the number of culturable bacteria collected in the sampling liquid. In the tempered impingers, three times higher CFU (colony-forming unit) 1540   per cubic meter of air were found than in the untempered ones. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Su et al. (2020) evaluated the volume loss rate and the temperature of collection liquid of SKC BioSampler as a function of time under conditions of 𝑇𝑎 = 19°C, 𝑅𝐻 = 0%, 𝑄𝑠 = 11.9 L.min-1 and 𝑃 = 48 kPa (pressure drop). The results showed that the liquid temperature drops rapidly from air temperature to around 4.3 °C in the first 15 min and becomes stable later, having the same trend with volume loss rate (with the opposite symbol). [SOURCE: Page_71]

2.2.2.3. Filtration Filters are widely used because of their simplicity of handling, transport, and low cost (Paba et al., 2013). As presented in Figure 2.7 (Lindsley, 2017), the efficiency of filter media for the filtration of airborne particles consists of five mechanisms: impaction, interception, diffusion, sedimentation and 1550   electrostatic attraction. The last occurred when filters carry an electrostatic charge, which can attract charged airborne particles. They could provide high physical collection efficiency (superior to 85%) for a broad range of airborne particles sizes from viruses (<0.3 µm) to clusters of spores (>7 mm) (Walls et al., 2017). However, Filters are not recommended for collecting viable aerosols due to desiccation of the sample during air sampling as well as damage and reduce the amount of detectable genetic material (Verreault et al., 2008). The sampling time should therefore be short, around 15–20 min or even shorter for microorganisms (Pan et al., 2019; Walls et al., 2017). Note that the type, size, and porosity of the filters determine typically the required power (or pressure drop) to supply a particular flow rate and consequently to define the sampling time to collect the airborne particles (Mainelis, 2020). Numerous type of filters such [SOURCE: Page_71]

70 [SOURCE: Page_71]

as fibrous filters (glass fiber filter), porous membrane (mixed cellulose esters or polytetrafluoroethylene 1560   (Teflon, PTFE), nucleopore, capillary pores (Polycarbonate, PC), and gelatin filters have been investigated, based on the characteristics of bio/aerosols and analysis technique (Fabian et al., 2009; Herrmann et al., 2017; Jeong and Kim, 2021; Wang et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_72]

The glass fiber filter has been applied in various fields because its high physical collection efficiency (Kesavan et al., 2007; J. Li et al., 2018), in particular for particles in sub-micron (e.g. > 93% for 0.3 µm dioctyl phthalate) (VanOsdell et al., 1990). [SOURCE: Page_72]

For bioaerosol sampling in survivability studies, among others, the use of high moisture content of gelatin filters causes minimum sampling stress (Pan et al., 2019; Zuo et al., 2014), and it can be completely dissolved in water. Although, Lin et al. (2019) observed that even with the use of high moisture content of gelatin filters, the filtration-based technique still causes detrimental effects on yeast survivability. Fabian 1570   et al. (2009) also reported that the infectious assayed and total concentration of virus (Influenza virus RNA) recovered from gelatin were 10 and 63 %, respectively of the number assayed and total recovery from SKC BioSampler. Nevertheless, the results of Wu et al. (2010) showed that the physical collection efficiency of gelatin filter can be outperformed the SKC BioSampler for the influenza A virus when sampling time is less than two minutes. [SOURCE: Page_72]

Filters usually have a diameter of 25, 37, or 47 mm, and are loaded in various sampling heads or filter holders such as Button Aerosol Sampler, IOM cassettes, and Cassette blanks with two or three section housings for filters (see Figure 3.3b). The use of appropriate filter size as well as filter holder with specific inlet characteristics allows to collect the interesting bio aerosol size and to control the aspiration and transmission losses. More information about the characteristics and performance of filter holders and 1580   sampling head as well as aerosol filtration efficiency could be found in the literatures (Bulejko, 2018; Görner et al., 2010; Jeong and Kim, 2021; Lindsley, 2017; Paik and Vincent, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_72]

Figure 2.7. Schematic presentation of collection mechanisms at a filter (Lindsley, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_72]

### 2.2.2.4. Efficiency of active samplers

71 [SOURCE: Page_72]

As shown in Figure 2.8, regardless of fundamental design differences, the overall sampling efficiency of an active sampler is product of the aspiration (𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 ), transmission (𝜂 𝑇 ), collection ( 𝜂𝑐 )/retention (𝜂𝑅𝐸𝑇 ), recovery efficiency (𝜂𝑅𝑒𝑐 ), and biological efficiency (𝜂𝐵𝑖𝑜 , for microorganisms) (Kesavan and Sagripanti, 1590   2015), defined as: [SOURCE: Page_73]

𝜂𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑙𝑙 = ⏟ 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 × 𝜂 𝑇 × 𝜂𝑐 × 𝜂𝑅𝐸𝑇 × 𝜂𝑅𝑒𝑐 × 𝜂𝐵𝑖𝑜                                                       Eq. 2-2 𝜂𝑃ℎ𝑦𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_73]

The overall sampling efficiency of each active sampler is examined by two critical parameters: [SOURCE: Page_73]

•   Physical collection efficiency, 𝜼𝑷𝒉𝒚𝒔 , describes how many particles can be extracted from the collection medium (solid, semi-liquid, or liquid) or filters and be measured, regardless of whether these particles are still viable and infectious. [SOURCE: Page_73]

•   Biological efficiency, 𝜼𝑩𝒊𝒐 , is expressed as the ratio of viable microorganisms to total (inactivated or viable) microorganisms penetrated into collection medium/filter at the completion of a test. As mentioned earlier, preserving the microorganism’s viability depends on a set of factors, including the most efficient sampling to minimize the stress and desiccation on microorganisms during sampling, the extraction method and selected analysis technique. [SOURCE: Page_73]

1600 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Figure 2.8. Schematic of the examinable efficiencies of an active sampler during physical aerosol sampling steps (Kesavan and Sagripanti, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_73]

The physical efficiency parameters have been discussed in the following subsections. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Aspiration efficiency (𝜼𝒂𝒔𝒑) The aspiration efficiency, 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 , is expressed as the ratio of the particle flow concentration, 𝐶𝑎 , aspirated through the sampler’s inlet to the particle flow concentration in the undisturbed air environment, 𝐶0 . [SOURCE: Page_73]

72 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Grinshpun et al. (1993) introduced a general equation being valid for all air flow conditions ranging from calm-air to high-air velocities, as follow: [SOURCE: Page_74]

𝐶𝑎 𝑆0,𝑝 . 𝑈𝑝                                                                           Eq. 2-3 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 =      = 𝐶0   𝑆𝑖 . 𝑈𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_74]

where, 𝑆0,𝑝 denotes the particle flow cross-sectional area, also known as the upstream critical area : the 1610   location containing all particles that terminate inside the sampler, 𝑆𝑖 denotes the sampler’s inlet area, 𝑈𝑝 denotes the particle's initial velocity, and 𝑈𝑠 is air sampling velocity (see Figure 2.9). 𝑆𝑖 and 𝑈𝑠 can be determined by inlet geometry and flow rate of samplers. 𝑆0,𝑝 and 𝑈𝑝 may be achieved theoretically or experimentally (Chattopadhyay, 2017; Grinshpun et al., 1997). [SOURCE: Page_74]

Figure 2.9. Schematic diagram of the limiting particle trajectory (Grinshpun et al., 1993) [SOURCE: Page_74]

The aspiration efficiency depends a certain physical parameters, which can be summarized in the below relationship diagram (Figure 2.10). [SOURCE: Page_74]

1620      Figure 2.10. Diagram representation of the physical parameters affecting the aspiration efficiency [SOURCE: Page_74]

73 [SOURCE: Page_74]

Based on the above physical parameters and assuming an inlet of simple geometry (circular area), and negligible settling velocity of the particles compared to flow velocities, the aspiration efficiency of an inlet may be a function of four key dimensionless variables: 𝑈0 •   Air velocity ratio 𝑅 =         in which 𝑈0 is the free air streamline velocity and 𝑈𝑠 is air sampling velocity; 𝑈𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_75]

𝐷𝑂𝑢𝑡 •   Dimensional ratio 𝑟 =             in which 𝐷𝑂𝑢𝑡 and 𝐷𝑖𝑛 are outlet and inlet orifice diameter, respectively; 𝐷𝑖𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_75]

•   Reynolds number (see Eq. A- 2 ) in which the parameters of characteristic air velocity and length-scale and are replaced by 𝑈0 and 𝐷𝑜𝑢𝑡 respectively; [SOURCE: Page_75]

•   Stokes number of particles near the active samplers can be calculated using the Eq. A- 22 ( 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 = 𝜏𝑝 .𝑈0 ). 𝐷𝑖𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_75]

1630       Before processing, it should be mentioned that in this part of the thesis, only aspiration efficiency of a 𝐷 thin-walled ( 𝑂𝑢𝑡⁄𝐷 < 1.1), sharp-edged tube in isoaxial sampling conditions from moving air have 𝑖𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_75]

been reviewed. [SOURCE: Page_75]

Figure 2.11 shows the typical limiting streamline patterns near the aspirating sampler’s inlet. Note that, the limiting streamline divides the sampled from the unsampled air: [SOURCE: Page_75]

1) Isokinetic sampling conditions, in which the sampler face directly into the laminar air flow, and the air velocity ratio is equal to one (𝑈0 = 𝑈𝑠 ). Thus, there is no divergence of streamlines near the inlet, and a stagnation could be only found at the edges of the entry. Hence, the isokinetic sampling provides a representative assessment of the particulate matter that is in the upstream, and minimize the sampling error (Figure 2.11a). [SOURCE: Page_75]

1640       2)     Super-isokinetic sampling occurs when the air velocity ratio is lower than one (𝑈0 < 𝑈𝑠 ) and the sampler draws the air from a greater area than its inlet own. A constriction force the gas to increase in velocity and be focused in the center of the entrance region of sampler. This affect is referred to as a vena contracta, and after that, the air flow expands again to establish an equilibrium pattern and the value of the air velocity thereafter remain constant (Figure 2.11c) (Brockmann, 2011). In this case, the small particles with insufficient inertia converge into the sampler’s inlet, but the inertia of large particles keep them from following the streamline that converge into the sampler (Wilcox, 1956). [SOURCE: Page_75]

3) Sub-isokinetic sampling conditions corresponds to air velocity ratio higher than one (𝑈0 > 𝑈𝑠 ), in which air the streamlines diverge from the edge of the inlet, and a stagnation occurs inside the 1650              sampler inlet (Addlesee, 1980). The air velocity in the approaching air freestream along the axis of the inlet decreases continuously, only become steady at some distance into entrance region of [SOURCE: Page_75]

74 [SOURCE: Page_75]

sampler (Vincent, 2007). In this condition, the large particles with sufficient inertia can cross the limiting streamline to be collected by the sampler, while the small particles follow the streamlines (Figure 2.11b). Therefore, the aspiration efficiency increase from 1 to 𝑅 for large particles (Brockmann, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Figure 2.11. Schematic diagram of isoaxial sampling with a thin-walled nozzle, with sample flow gas velocity 𝑈𝑠 and free stream ambient gas velocity 𝑈0 under (a) isokinetic (𝑈0 = 𝑈𝑠 ) sampling conditions, (b) sub-isokinetic (𝑈𝑠 < 𝑈0 ) sampling conditions, and (c) super-isokinetic (𝑈𝑠 > 𝑈0 ) sampling 1660                                       conditions (Kulkami et al.2011). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Table 2.2 presents a series of equations developed to predict the aspiration efficiency for isoaxial sampling with a thin-walled nozzle. [SOURCE: Page_76]

According to the empirical equations, for particles with an aerodynamic diameter of < 1 µm, the 𝑈0 isokinetic sampler is no longer an issue (as long as        is kept within 0.5 and 2). 𝑈𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_76]

Transmission efficiency (𝜼𝑻 ) [SOURCE: Page_77]

Transmission efficiency (𝜂 𝑇 ) is the efficiency of transport of particles aspirated from the inlet of sampler 1670   to the filter or collection medium through nozzle exit (see Figure 2.6 and Figure 2.8). Transmission and deposition behavior of aerosols inside the inlet is influenced by a large amount of factors including Reynolds number (turbulence or laminar flow cases), Stokes number, sampling angle, wall material, bluntness ratio, and conventional gravitational deposition parameter. Okazaki and Willeke (1987) developed un model from the experimental data to estimate the gravitational settling transmission efficiency for thin-wall tubular inlets. Hangal and Willeke (1990) introduced an inertial transmission efficiency model for super-isokinetic sampling. Several theoretical and numerical models have also been made to predict the inertial deposition of aerosol particles in 90° bends (Nicolaou and Zaki, 2016; Pui et al., 1987; Sun and Lu, 2013). Generally, particle wall losses increase with particle size (US-EPA, 2012; Zhao et al., 2015). Seshadri et al. (2009) pointed out that sampling flow rate will significantly influences 1680   the particle wall loss of the BioSampler using (adenosine triphosphate) ATP bioluminescence method. [SOURCE: Page_77]

However, the value of 𝜂 𝑇 is difficult to predict and almost all practical recommendations for aerosol sampling have as a goal to minimize or completely avoid the uncertainties caused by transmission aspiration, when particles of unknown bounce properties are sampled. for instance, the internal wall loss effect can be minimized by use of a short sampling line (Grinshpun et al., 1993). [SOURCE: Page_77]

Collection efficiency (𝜼𝑪 ) Collection efficiency by collection medium/filter represents the ratio of the particles drawn into the sampler from nozzle exit during a test and the fraction of the particles that successfully spike into collection medium/filter at the completion of a test. [SOURCE: Page_77]

76 [SOURCE: Page_77]

𝐶𝐿                                                                               Eq. 2-4 𝜂𝐶 = 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 . 𝜂 𝑇 . 𝐶𝑢𝑝 [SOURCE: Page_78]

Retention efficiency (𝜼𝑹𝑬𝑻 ) 1690      Retention efficiency indicates how well collected particles are retained by the collection medium as a function of time, and defined as follows (Kesavan et al., 2010): [SOURCE: Page_78]

𝑁𝐿,𝑡                                                                                 Eq. 2-5 𝜂𝑅𝐸𝑇 = 𝑁𝐿,0 [SOURCE: Page_78]

where 𝑁𝐿,0 and 𝑁𝐿,𝑡 are the number of particles introduced into the collection medium at the start of sampling and recovered from medium at time t of sampler operation, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Kesavan et al. (2010) evaluated directly the retention efficiency of four active samplers by spiking a batch of liquid with solid polystyrene spheres (PS with size of 1 or 10 µm in diameter) into the sampler, and measurement of the tracer after running and operating the sampler for 30 s as a 𝑁𝐿,0 from Eq. 2-5. The values of 𝑁𝐿,𝑡 were measured based on analyses of subsequent batches of liquid at time-intervals of 15, 30, 45, and 60 min. For the AGI-30 and the batch-type wetted wall cyclone with compensation for evaporation (BWWC-EC), the retention efficiency of 1 µm PS particles after 60 min was less than 30%, while that of 1700   the SKC BioSampler was 59%. This value for SKC BioSampler with 10 µm PS particles was greater than 90% even at 60 min sampling time. They continued the experiments with two set of samplers (AGI-30 and SKC BioSampler) for 1 μm Bacillus atrophaeus spores, and 0.7 μm Pantoea agglomerans vegetative cells. The results for the spores were about the same as those for 1 µm PS and show lower retention efficiencies for the vegetative bacteria. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Han and Mainelis (2012) investigated the effect of particle type (polystyrene latex particles of 0.9 and 3.2 mm, B. subtilis bacteria and C. cladosporioides fungal spores), particle concentration, and collection liquid volume (for SKC BioSampler) on retention efficiency of AGI-30 and SKC BioSampler after 15 and 30 min sampling time. The results indicate that the average retention efficiency for BioSampler with 5 mL liquid, BioSampler with 20 mL liquid and AGI-30 were 71.5%, 74.4% and 81.6%, respectively. Longer 1710   sampling time led to lower retention efficiency and higher concentration resulted in higher retention efficiency compared to lower one: 83.6 ± 12.4% compared to 70.6 ± 26.0%, on average. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Su et al. (2020) too tested the retention efficiency of a Continuous-Extraction BioSampler (CEBS) for different fluorescence-labelled polystyrene latex size range (0.1–10 µm) after sampling time of 10 and 15 min. The results showed that the value of retention efficiency after 15 min of sampling is lower than that after 10 min. The retention efficiency decreased with decreasing the particles diameter first and fell to almost 0.85 [-] in submicron size range, then recover to nearly unity. [SOURCE: Page_78]

77 [SOURCE: Page_78]

Recovery efficiency (𝜼𝑹𝐞𝐜) The Recovery efficiency demonstrates how effective sample can be recover as a function of proper 1720   adequacy of the method of extraction, transport and storage of the particles and viable microorganisms between collection and analysis, as well as, of analysis method. For instance, Li and Lin (2001) recommended a sample collected by impingement methods should be refrigerated and processed as soon as possible to avoid an increase of bacterial culturability (E.coli and B. subtilis). Moreover, it was demonstrated that the storage effect by Nuclepore filtration depends on the type of bioaerosol, so that for B. subtilis was insignificant, but E.coli recovery decreased as storage time increased. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Paba et al. (2013) reviewed the sampling and analysis methods for the assessment of exposure to airborne endotoxins and reported that the main factor affecting the endotoxin measurements is the extraction method but only few studies (from 82 papers) have focused on the standardization of recovery and analysis methods. [SOURCE: Page_79]

1730      The subsection 2.2.4 was briefly mentioned the various analysis technique that generally used to quantify or detect the collected bioaerosols and their compatibility with bioaerosol samplers. The more information about the characteristics of the analytical techniques and their efficiency to identify the selective species under different conditions (e.g. type of collection medium and environment) could be found in literatures (Dungan and Leytem, 2009; Ghosh et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_79]

Other techniques Optical particle counters (OPC) are very widely used for real time aerosol analysis due to their ability to deliver particle-size distribution over a wide range. The OPCs detect and size particles by measuring the amount of light scattered by individual particles as they pass through a beam of light, so, they measure the optical diameter rather than the aerodynamic diameter. Consequently, these instruments need to be 1740   calibrated for each targeted particle type. This makes them poorly adapted for atmospheric pollution as it contains particles of various natures (Djoumi et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_79]

BioTrak® is a real-time viable particle counter that combines an optical particle counter (OPC) system with a fluorescence detector to determine if a particle is biological based on a built-in discrimination algorithm (see 3.3.4). The counter’s utilization has been validated by the USFDA (Kirschman and Lloyd, 2014) and allows an easy and fast monitoring of total viable airborne bioaerosol with a cut-off size of 2 μm (Soret et al., 2018). However, further investigations are needed to better understand the response of this new technique to different types of biogenic aerosol particles and to quantity potential interferences with non-biogenic particles. Therefore, this instrument can be compared to other bioaerosol monitoring instruments. [SOURCE: Page_79]

1750      An alternate to the OPC is the aerodynamic particle sizer, which provides real-time measurement of aerodynamic diameter, and is especially useful where the filtration, deposition, and inhalation properties of the particles are important. The size, shape, and density of the particles can be measured with the [SOURCE: Page_79]

78 [SOURCE: Page_79]

electronic aerodynamic particle sizer. In this instrument, the transit time of a particle traveling through a pair of parallel illuminated planes is measured. The transit time is a function of the aerodynamic diameter of the particles. The aerodynamic particle sizer has been found to correlate well to cascade impactors (Welker, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_80]

LIDAR (light detection and ranging) is a powerful tool for quantifying aerosols' (both solid and condensed) backscatter and extinction coefficients (extinction is defined as the radiation that does not reach the sensor due to scattering and absorption), and operates at the UV-VIS-NIR range (Gil et al., 2013; Kira 1760   et al., 2016). LIDAR has recently been developed by Gregorio et al. (2014) for monitoring of spray drift in real time, with high range and temporal resolution (Gregorio et al., 2016). The lidar system was also used for monitoring the evolution of the spray flux over the canopy and to generate 2-D images of these plumes (Gregorio et al., 2016a). However, spray drift measurements using LIDAR can be affected by droplet size and the prototype is under development. In addition, more spray drift measurements are needed to compare its results with those obtained using passive collectors. [SOURCE: Page_80]

Enumeration technique Conventional methods for evaluating performances of bioaerosol samplers frequently rely on culturing microorganisms or counting them with a microscope. Culture techniques are widely used to enumerate fecal indicators such as E. coli in irrigation water (Becerra-Castro et al., 2015; Holvoet et al., 2014; 1770   Truchado et al., 2016). However, the results obtained from culturing techniques can only represent the culturable fraction of the microorganisms, which often significantly underestimates the microbial loads in the environments (Wu et al., 2010). In recent years, there is an increased interest in applying culture- independent approaches such as enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA), Flow cytometry (FCM), Epifluorescence microscopy (capable to count both culturable and nonculturable cells), and quantitative polymerase chain reaction (qPCR)-based technologies for analyzing bio-aerosols. These techniques have high levels of sensitivity and specificity (Ghosh et al., 2015). Although, the major disadvantage to qPCR is that it cannot distinguish between viable and dead cells (Tseng et al., 2014). Propidium monoazide quantitative polymerase chain reaction (PMA-qPCR) is one of the promising methods for timely detection of Viable but non-culturable (VBNC) non pathogens in environmental samples (Golpayegani et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_80]

1780 [SOURCE: Page_80]

79 [SOURCE: Page_80]

Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_81]

In this part a brief review of air sampling methods from various fields and the sampler efficiencies have been presented. Various samplers have been used to quantify bioaerosols. Active samplers with pumps are the most popular in various environmental conditions. The use of samplers with higher collection flow 1790   rate, that results in higher power consumption, which would shorten sampling time capacity, are generally preferred. Higher flow rate also means more intense desiccation of the collecting medium which may lead to a lower recovery of the sensitive organisms. [SOURCE: Page_81]

The accurate quantification of the volume of airborne droplets containing pathogens is of critical importance to estimate the health risks due to sprinkler irrigation with treated wastewater. Despite numerous studies on performance of the bioaerosol samplers and peer-reviewed literature, a standard bioaerosol sampling protocol has not yet been provided. [SOURCE: Page_81]

It is difficult to conclude on the best samplers or on the best protocols to collect bioaerosols. Our review has shown results obtained in different experiments, with various sampling times, collect medium and targeted pathogens monitored in different environments. There are not a lot of studies comparing the most 1800   common samplers in the same conditions thus no standard design or protocol to refer on. [SOURCE: Page_81]

This analysis is the object of the following chapter [SOURCE: Page_81]

. [SOURCE: Page_81]

80 [SOURCE: Page_81]

Part II. Laboratory Experiments [SOURCE: Page_82]

Contribution to the thesis project: This part was divided in two chapters (Chapter 3 andChapter 4). The aims were: [SOURCE: Page_82]

•   To compare different active sampling methods of airborne particles in controlled conditions in order to assess which one is the most suitable for monitoring aerosols from sprinkler irrigation of TWW. [SOURCE: Page_82]

•   To quantify the physical collection efficiency in low wind velocity, using a fluorescent tracer. [SOURCE: Page_82]

•   To investigate the effect of sampling time on the collection efficiency and on the culturability of the E.coli cells sampled by the impingers [SOURCE: Page_82]

•   To characterize the effect of air velocity and nozzle height on the airborne droplet size distributions downwind from the source using Phase Doppler Particle Analysis, in a small scale wind tunnel (Chapter 4) [SOURCE: Page_82]

The results of Chapter 3 have been published in the proceedings of the World Environmental and Water Resources Congress, held in Henderson, Nevada, May 17–21, 2020 (Zardari et al. 2020) [SOURCE: Page_82]

81 [SOURCE: Page_82]

Performance assessment of active samplers in calm air [SOURCE: Page_83]

Introduction This chapter was firstly focused on evaluation of the physical collection efficiency of two impingers, SKC BioSampler and AGI-4 for tiny droplets containing a fluorescent dye, ABF (see 3.5.1.1) under various 1810   sampling time. We selected this tracer because it has been used in previous experiments of our team (Molle et al., 2016; Tomas et al., 2019) to determine the airborne spray transport. [SOURCE: Page_83]

Moreover, as previously mentioned in the Chapter 1 (see Table 1.3), Escherichia coli (E. coli) is a member of the faecal coliform group and is a more specific indicator of faecal pollution than other faecal (Francy et al., 1993). It is adopted in most European regulations as a basic tool to assess the microbiological quality of treated municipal wastewater (Kellis et al., 2013; Vergine et al., 2015), because of the availability of affordable, fast, sensitive, specific and easier to perform detection methods for E. coli (Odonkor and Ampofo, 2013). It is also used as one of the standards for testing bioaerosol sampling and air purification devices (Macher, 1997; Simon et al., 2011). Furthermore, many studies focused on the generation and transportation of indicator microorganisms, in particular, E.coli in bioaerosols (Carducci et al., 2000; Chan 1820   et al., 2019; Y. Han et al., 2013; Nag et al., 2021). Therefore, in this study, a non-pathogenic E. coli strain was selected as a model bacterium for investigating the bioaersol sampling of AGI – 4, SKC BioSampler, and BioTrak®, under two sampling time of 5 and 15 min. [SOURCE: Page_83]

Note that this chapter started with the presentation of a series of the preliminary experiments to understand necessary precautions, strengthen the interpretations and to ensure the repeatability of the experiments. [SOURCE: Page_83]

82 [SOURCE: Page_83]

Description of aerosol generation and test facility To address the objective of this chapter, it was necessary to work in controlled conditions with a system that generates controlled size and discharge of aerosols, to further benchmark four different collection techniques. [SOURCE: Page_84]

1830      Our experiments have been conducted in laboratory of Engineering of the Industrial Environment and Industrial and Natural Risks (LGEI in IMT Mines Alès), which has developed in partnership with the companies Europe Environment and Bruk'Air, a sampling chamber of 1.234 × 0.63 × 0.8 m 3 dimension presenting a laminar flow with a range of air velocity (𝑈) of 0–0.09 m.s-1. In this chapter, only 𝑈 = 0.09 m.s-1 (dry airflow Q_DRY=400 L.min-1) was investigated (Figure 3.1). [SOURCE: Page_84]

The aerosol generator was composed of an ultrasonic humidifier (U7146, Boneco) that has been installed in a 40 L tank. The liquid suspension of each test tracer was aerosolized by the ultrasonic humidifier. A 60 L.min-1 airflow (Q_NEB) controlled by a mass flowmeter (Brooks, SLA 5800 series, USA) transported the synthetic aerosol outside the generator where it was diluted by a dry airflow (Q_DRY=400 L.min-1). Afterward, the aerosols passed through a mixing zone and entered the sampling 1840   chamber (Figure 3.2). The temperature and relative humidity were recorded in the sampling chamber by the Thermo-Hygrometer (HD50, KIMO® INSTRUMENTS, France). [SOURCE: Page_84]

Figure 3.1. Schematic diagram of the aerosol generation and test facility. *: sampling zone. [SOURCE: Page_84]

83 [SOURCE: Page_84]

Figure 3.2. Photo of the test facility [SOURCE: Page_85]

Description of the aerosol samplers AGI-4 The All-Glass Impinger, AGI–4 (Ace Glass Inc., Vineland, NJ, USA) has a nominal air sampling flow rate of 6 L.min-1 passing through a 59.44 mm² aperture area when the pressure drop across the nozzle is at 1850   least 16.14 inHg (~0.54 bar) for achieving sonic velocity (Figure 3.3a(left)). The nozzle is directed perpendicularly to the bottom of the bottle, with a 4 mm distance from the nozzle exit to the bottom surface. Typically, the initial liquid volume (demineralized water (DW) or sterile DW) in the glass bottle is 20 mL. As previously mentioned (see 2.2.2.2), sampling duration is usually 10–30 min. The AGI–4 and similar conventional impingers are commonly used with low viscosity liquids such as water (Lin et al., 1999). [SOURCE: Page_85]

SKC BioSampler SKC BioSampler (SKC Inc., Eighty Four, USA) consists of a 90° curved inlet section with approximately 70.88 mm^2 aperture area, a nozzle section with a three tangential 0.63 mm nozzles (sonic orifices) at a distance of 30 mm from to the bottom surface of the liquid reservoir, and a collection reservoir. Each nozzle permits 4.2 L.min-1 of ambient air to pass through for a total nominal flow rate of 1860   approximately 12.5 L.min-1 at a pressure drop of 15 in Hg (~0.5 bar). This device has been developed to prevent the loss of sampling liquid during operation and to prevent damage to bacterial cells during collection (Figure 3.3a(right)) and can be operated continuously for up to 8 h without a decrease in collection liquid when a non-evaporating liquid (mineral oil) is used (Lin et al., 1999). The SKC BioSampler has been used previously in bioaerosol field studies (Fabian et al., 2005) and has a collection efficiency of 79% for particles larger than 0.3 μm and 100% for particles larger than 2 μm (Willeke et al. 1998). [SOURCE: Page_85]

Summary Table 3.1 indicates the aspiration features of the AGI–4 and SKC BioSampler. Note that the nominal flow rate are calibrated by the manufacturer, at standard conditions, at normal temperature and pressure (NTP), which refers to 101 kPa and 293 K [1 atmosphere and 20 °C] and these values could be [SOURCE: Page_85]

84 [SOURCE: Page_85]

varied based on the atmospheric pressure (Pa) and environmental temperature (Ta). Su et al. (2020) 1870   carefully evaluated the pressure drop, mass loss rate and temperature of the collection liquid of SKC BioSampler through literature review and theoretic analysis. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Table 3.1. Aspiration characteristics of the studied impingers at NTP Type               Inlet           Outlet           Wall          Nominal       Sampling air diameter, 𝐷𝑖𝑛   diameter, 𝐷𝑜𝑢𝑡     thickness       flow rate     velocity, 𝑈𝑠 [mm]             [mm]             [mm]          [L.min-1]       [m.s-1] AGI-4                 8.7             11.0             1.15             6             1.68 SKC BioSampler            9.5             12.8             1.65            12.5           2.94 [SOURCE: Page_86]

Glass fiber filter Glass fiber filter (A/E, SKC Ltd, UK) with 1µm pore size and 37 mm diameter supported by cellulose pads are loaded into two piece plastic cassettes (Figure 3.3b). It maintains high physical collection efficiency across all measured particle sizes, sampling flowrates, and sampling times. It typically provides more than 85% physical collection efficiency for particle sizes from viruses (< 30 nm) to clusters of spores (>7 µm) (Walls et al., 2017). Sampling times must be short (≤ 20 min) for some vegetative bacteria that are sensitive to stress. In this study, this glass fiber filter was used as reference when using fluorescent dye 1880   to estimate physical efficiency. A pump is used to force air through the filter with a discharge that reproduce the same suction velocity as for the sampler (section 3.5.1.2). [SOURCE: Page_86]

BioTrak® The BioTrak® particle counter (model 9510–BD, TSI Inc, USA) with a flow rate of 28.3 L.min-1, counts total and viable particles in real-time (Figure 3.3c). This counter uses laser diffraction to measure and count the number of particles in an air sample, in the diameter range from 0.5 to 25 μm with total particulate counting efficiency of 50% at 0.5 µm and 100% for particles > 0.75 µm, according to ISO 21501-4 (ISO 21501-4, 2018). The size of particles detected by BioTrak® is divided into six value intervals: 0.5–0.7 μm; 0.7–1 μm; 1–3 μm; 3–5 μm; 5–10 μm and 10–25 μm. In addition, there are two fluorescent channels and one sizing channel for detection of viable particles with a diameter between 1 and 25 micrometers. For 1890   particle viability detection, the BioTrak® uses Laser-Induced Fluorescence (LIF) technology. In simple terms, when microorganisms (viable particles) are exposed to laser light at 405 nm, they absorb and re- emit light at higher wavelengths; a process called fluorescence. Fluorescent cell metabolites most commonly associated with viability are NADH, riboflavin and tryptophan, which are targeted by the LIF technique (Laflamme et al, 2005; Buschke et al, 2012). The results are expressed in particle viable counts per cubic meter of air (vcnt.m-3). [SOURCE: Page_86]

In this study, this device was used as a reference for normalization of the number of bacterial colonies collected by the impingers. In addition, it allowed to determine droplet size distribution in the sampling chamber. [SOURCE: Page_86]

Preliminary experiments Droplet size distribution in the sampling chamber BioTrak® was used to detect the size distribution of droplets in the sampling chamber. As described earlier, the system was designed to discharge dry air at 400 L.min-1 of air with a constant relative humidity inside the chamber at about 5%. As soon as the aerosol generator was turned on, the humidity relative reached 10 to 15%. Given this range of low relative humidity and consequently evolution of the size of initial droplets in the system, BioTrak® characterized only the final droplet size distribution in the sampling 1910   chamber. It means the one captured by collecting devices. Since the intervals of the particle channel sizes of BioTrak® were not uniform, we assumed that all the droplets in each size range have a diameter equal to midpoint of the size interval. In addition, we normalized the number and volume fraction per the difference of the logarithms of the upper and lower size limits of the size range, as shown in Table 3.2. [SOURCE: Page_87]

The cumulative distribution function, F(Du), of discretized number and volume size distribution of droplets data from BioTrak® was calculated, as follow: 6 𝑁𝑖 (𝑜𝑟 𝑉𝑖 )                                                                Eq. 3-1 𝐹(𝐷𝑢 ) = 1 − ∑ 𝑁𝑡𝑜𝑡 (𝑜𝑟𝑉𝑡𝑜𝑡 ) 𝑖=1 [SOURCE: Page_87]

where 𝐷𝑢 is the upper size limit of the size range. These values were also fitted with cumulative Log– normal distribution (in number and volume), which is the most widely used for aerosol size distribution (Kulkarni et al., 2011), and defined by: [SOURCE: Page_87]

As shown in Figure 3.4a, the curves of the number and volume distribution of aerosols had a sharp peak at 0.6 µm. The cumulative Log-normal distribution with the NMD and VMD values of 0.66 and 0.92 µm, respectively, indicate that 90% of the total number and volume of the aerosols were related to the aerosols 1930   inferior to 1 and 1.4 µm, respectively. However, due to limited number of channels, the cumulative Log- normal function did not fitted very well to the raw data. Therefore, by interpolation from the values of the column 7 of Table 3.2, the 90% of the total volume could be estimated to be inferior to about 2.5 micron. [SOURCE: Page_88]

In addition, the data were moderately mono-dispersed considering their narrow size distribution (0.5– 0.7), as well as the geometric standard deviation of 1.39. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Estimation of the aspiration efficiency of active samplers in calm air When air velocity →0 (calm air), the particle inertia and its stokes number 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑖 depend exclusively on air sampling velocity (𝑈𝑠 ), as follow: [SOURCE: Page_89]

𝜌𝑝 𝐷𝑝2 𝐶𝑐 𝑈𝑠 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑖 =                                                                                               Eq. 3-5 18𝜇𝑎 𝐷𝑖𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_89]

Note that the parameters were already defined (see page 72 and Appendix. A). [SOURCE: Page_89]

Furthermore, Grinshpun et al. (1993) developed the following empirical equation for aerosol aspiration 𝐷 1940   into thin-walled, sharp-edged ( 𝑂𝑢𝑡⁄𝐷 < 1.1) from calm air, which is the based on the numerical 𝑖𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_89]

calculations: 0.5 +1} 4𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑖 {(R𝑐𝑎𝑙𝑚 )                                                             Eq. 3-6 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝(𝑐𝑎𝑙𝑚) = exp [−                              ] + R 𝑐𝑎𝑙𝑚 . 𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜑𝑔 1 + 2𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_89]

where 𝜑𝑔 is inlet orientation in gravitational field (in this study, 𝜑𝑔 = 90°), and 𝑅𝑐𝑎𝑙𝑚 is the velocity ratio in calm air, expressed as: [SOURCE: Page_89]

𝑈𝑡𝑠 𝑅𝑐𝑎𝑙𝑚 =                                                                                              Eq. 3-7 𝑈𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_89]

where 𝑈𝑡𝑠 is the terminal settling velocity of the particle. [SOURCE: Page_89]

1950   plastic cassettes including the filter was about 1.75 and also because of its small inlet diameter (Ø 4.9 mm), a crystal tube (Ø 9 × 12 mm) of 20 mm long have been connected to the entrance. It allowed to reduce the 𝐷𝑂𝑢𝑡 ratio of       ⁄𝐷 by 1.33 and also, to produce an air sampling velocity almost similar to the one of 𝑖𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_89]

Table 3.3 presents the Stokes number and aspiration efficiency of the AGI-4 and SKC BioSampler in calm air for droplets with diameters corresponding the midpoint of size intervals of BioTrak ®. [SOURCE: Page_89]

The results showed that for droplets up to 2 µm, which correspond to the range of droplet size distribution of this study, the stokes numbers were sharply lower than 1. Therefore, the droplets perfectly followed the streamlines, and the aspiration efficiencies of both impingers were closely to unity. [SOURCE: Page_89]
