Evaporation/aerosolization rate of the collection liquid To investigate the impact of temperature and sampling time on the evaporation/re-aerosolization rate of 1980   the liquid collection (DW), the impingers were filled with 20 mL of DW and then, turned on at different time intervals without Aerosol. The samplers (Impinger+ collection liquid) were weighed before and after each test using a balance (KERNPKS, accuracy: 0.01 g, KERN & Sohn GmbH, Germany) .The measurement were repeated three times in order to increase the precision. [SOURCE: Page_91]

As shown in Figure 3.6a, the liquid evaporation/aerosolization rate for impingers, SKC BioSampler and AGI-4 at approximately 25°C was respectively, about 0.19 and 0.14 mL.min-1. Whereas previous studies (Lin et al., 1997; willeke et al., 1998) observed an evaporation rate of 0.2 mL.min-1 for AGI-4 at the flow rate of 12.5 L.min-1 and at a temperature of 25 °C, it can be concluded that it would be necessary to calibrate the liquid evaporation/ aerosolization rate against the experimental impingers. [SOURCE: Page_91]

The experimental results in the aerosol test system show that when impingers operated in their most 1990   common mode, with 20 mL of DW at nominal flow rate and sampling time of 15 min, for every 1-degree increase in ambient temperature, 0.1 mL of water evaporates (Figure 3.6b). [SOURCE: Page_91]

(a)                                                    (b) Figure 3.6. (a) Liquid evaporation/ re-aerosolization rate of impingers at different sampling times; (b) The influence of the temperature on the evaporation rate of the liquid in the impingers (sampling time: 15 min. Relative humidity: 10-15%). [SOURCE: Page_91]

Materials and methods Physical collection efficiency set-up 3.5.1.1. Specification of the tracer To study the effect of sampling time on the physical collection efficiency, 𝜂𝑝ℎ𝑦𝑠 , fluorescent dye Acid 2000   Brilliant Flavine, ABF (WALDECK, Münster) has been applied as a tracer. This tracer with excitation and [SOURCE: Page_91]

90 [SOURCE: Page_91]

emission wavelength of 450 nm and 500 nm respectively is commonly used in quantification of pesticide spray drift analysis both in field and in wind tunnels studies (Cornacchia et al., 2019; Gil et al., 2007; Holterman et al., 2009; Molle et al., 2016). The advantages of this fluorescent dye are: moderate UV degradation, 7% on average between 30 and 180 min of exposure, no impact on the movement of droplet (Schleier et al., 2010), nearly constant fluorescent intensity over the pH range from 6.9–10.4 (Zhu et al., 2005), and its non-toxicity. [SOURCE: Page_92]

The quantification of the ABF concentration (µg.L-1) from all samplers was performed using a fluorescence Spectrometer (Perkin Elmer LS 45, Waltham, USA) with the optimal range of accuracy in the linear trend between 5 and 450 μg.L-1 of the ABF concentration, and verified using a fluorescence- 2010   concentration calibration curve. The limit of quantification was considered as twice the standard deviation of a blank sample, a sample containing only demineralized water, which lies approximately in a range between 1.5 μg.L-1 and 2.5 μg.L-1. At greater than 450 μg.L-1, a dilution of the sample was therefore required to bring the read value within the optimal range of accuracy. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Characterization of Colloidal particles of the ABF in the solvent The ABF is highly hydrosoluble, however, in a separate experiment, the size of colloidal particles of ABF in the solvent (demineralized water) was determined by a nanoparticle size analyzer (VASCOγ, Cordouan Technologies, France), in SQPQV-EMMAH soil laboratory of INRAE Avignon. This instrument is intended for measuring size distribution of colloidal and suspension nanoparticles from 1 nm to 10 µm, based on dynamic light scattering (DLS). Briefly, when sample suspension is irradiated by a 2020   stabilized laser with 657 nm wavelength, the intensity scattered by the nanoparticles fluctuates over time. This phenomenon is related to the thermal agitation, to the Brownian movement of the particles, which induces a loss of coherence of scattered /diffused light. The smaller the particle, the faster the its motion also the higher the frequency of intensity fluctuations. The autocorrelation function (ACF) of the diffused signal highlights a characteristic decay time, 𝛤, which depends on the temperature, the viscosity of the fluid, and the linear diffusivity coefficient of the particles, 𝐷𝑡 . Then the size of particles can be calculated via the Stokes-Einstein formula (see Figure 3.7). [SOURCE: Page_92]

91 [SOURCE: Page_92]

Figure 3.7. Schematic diagram of the principle of DLS technique and the patented technology of VASCO with its DTC and solvent proof cell. [SOURCE: Page_93]

2030      For this purpose, a sample suspension was prepared by dissolving 50 mg of ABF in 100 mL demineralized water (concentration of 0.5 g.L-1) and mixed them using a magnetic mixer for 30 minutes at room temperature. A Few droplets of the sample were directly dropped into the VASCO cell with a micropipette (see Figure 3.8). The Dual Thickness Controller system (DTCTM) was set in the up position (by default) and the measurements at least triplicated, were performed using nanoQTM software with each correlogram acquisition processed by the Pade-Laplace algorithm. All the criteria for optimizing the correlator setting are explained in detail in nanoQ & Vasco Instruction guide 2013. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Figure 3.8. Measurement of colloidal particles size of the ABF in the solvent by using a nanoparticle size analyzer. [SOURCE: Page_93]

2040      Figure 3.9 shows a size histogram expressed in intensity as a function of Hydrodynamic diameter with two peaks and 0.234 and 1.072 µm with 0.46 and 0.53 relative intensities. The water contained ABF particles characterized by the following parameters: 𝐷𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑛 𝑛𝑢𝑚𝑏𝑒𝑟 =0.243 µm, 𝐷𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑛 𝑖𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑛𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑦 =0.653 µm, 𝐷𝑚𝑒𝑎𝑛 𝑣𝑜𝑙𝑢𝑚𝑒 =0.784 µm, in Pade Laplace analysis mode, and 𝐷𝑣10 = 0.224 µm, 𝐷𝑣50 and 𝐷𝑣90 =1.024 µm. The statistics 𝐷𝑣10 , 𝐷𝑣50 , and 𝐷𝑣90 are diameters that respectively represent the cumulative volume less [SOURCE: Page_93]

92 [SOURCE: Page_93]

than 10, 50 and 90% of the volume of the ABF particles. The colloids of ABF could therefore be considered to simulate transport of droplet nuclei containing particles around 1 µm in diameter or bacteria such as E.coli. [SOURCE: Page_94]

Figure 3.9. The size of the colloids of ABF as a function of intensity (a. u). [SOURCE: Page_94]

2050 [SOURCE: Page_94]

3.5.1.2. Experimental setup (physical efficiency) Figure 3.10 shows the experimental set-up to characterize the physical efficiency of SKC BioSampler and AGI-4 at different sampling times (5 min, 15 min, 30 min, and 60 min). To measure this efficiency, the ultrasonic humidifier was filled with 300 mL demineralized water (DW) to which the ABF was added at a concentration of 0.5 g.L-1. The physical collection efficiency, ηphys, defined in Eq. 3-8, was determined through measurement of the mass of the ABF recovered from two glass fiber filters situated: next to the impinger with the interval of 0.1 m (upstream, Mup), and downstream the impinger’s suction line (downstream, Mdown). [SOURCE: Page_94]

𝑀𝑑𝑜𝑤𝑛 𝜂𝑝ℎ𝑦𝑠 = 1 −                                                                                        Eq. 3-8 𝑀𝑈𝑝 [SOURCE: Page_94]

Moreover, the sampling efficiency, 𝜂𝑐(𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒) , that represents the ratio of the mass of ABF in the final 2060   sampling liquid, MC, to that recovered by the glass fiber filters as a reference, Mup was determined as: [SOURCE: Page_94]

𝑀𝐶                                                                                   Eq. 3-9 𝜂𝑐(𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒) = 𝑀𝑈𝑝 Thus, the residual efficiency, 𝜂𝑅 , represents the ratio of the mass total of ABF in the final sampling liquid, MC, added to the downstream filter, Mdown to that of the ABF recovered by glass fiber filter, as a reference, Mup. It is determined as follow: [SOURCE: Page_94]

𝑀𝐶 + 𝑀𝑑𝑜𝑤𝑛                                                                              Eq. 3-10 𝜂𝑅 = 𝑀𝑈𝑝 [SOURCE: Page_94]

93 [SOURCE: Page_94]

Note that all tests related to this study have been repeated at least three times and their arithmetic averages have been calculated. [SOURCE: Page_95]

Figure 3.10. Schematic of the experimental setup used to test physical collection efficiency of SKC BioSampler/AGI-4. [SOURCE: Page_95]

It should be mentionned that in addition to recording the flow rate during the tests (once per 30 seconds), 2070   before each test, the flow rate of the upstream filter has been adjusted based on the average value measured for the corresponding impinger. [SOURCE: Page_95]

In addition, to evaluate possible losses of collected ABF on inner walls of two piece cassettes, after removing the filter, the cassettes were washed with 5 mL of DW and the samples were analyzed using the fluorescence Spectrometer. However, the concentration of all samples was lower than the detection limit of the device. So, in this study, this loss was negligible. [SOURCE: Page_95]

3.5.1.3. Recovery of the ABF from the filters To recover the totality of the ABF from the glass fiber filters, two methods were compared, the method inspired from the study of Robinson et al. (1959), M$_1$, and the methods of Kesavan et al. (2001), M$_2$. [SOURCE: Page_95]

For the two methods, a measured amount of a mixture of DW and ABF was added to six Glass fiber 2080   filters, and directly to three test tubes containing 20 mL of DW as a standard liquid. The filters were air dried and placed individually in the test tubes containing 20 mL of DW. The three test tubes with filters were shaked for 1 hour (M$_2$) with laboratory Orbital shakers (Stuart® SSL$_1$, Cole-Parmer, UK) and the other three remain in the water for 1 hour without agitation (M$_1$). The fluorescence intensity of each test tubes was then measured with the fluorescence spectrometer. Figure 3.11 presents the results of dye recovery from the filters compared with the standard mix. No significant difference between the two methods was observed (33 a.u.). Nevertheless, as the method 1, M$_1$ gives the closest result to the standard mix and it was retained. [SOURCE: Page_95]

94 [SOURCE: Page_95]

Figure 3.11. Comparison of Fluorescence intensity readings for Samples of ABF Recovered from filters 2090                      through two methods with intensity readings for standard mix. [SOURCE: Page_96]

Viability and culturability assessment tests 3.5.2.1. Preparation of bacterial suspension The E.coli (ATCC 15597) strain has been grown in 5 mL nutrient Luria Broth, LB (CONDA S.A, Madrid, Spain) in a shaking incubator at 180 rpm and 37 °C for 18h. Precultured cells were transferred into 100 mL LB, followed by an incubation for 1.5-2 h. The cell suspension LB was tested by spectrometer (DOP600 = 0.5–0.7), then E.coli was harvested from the broth by centrifugation at 8000 rpm for 5 min at 15°C and washed in sterile DW. The cell suspension was diluted and transferred to the ultrasonic humidifier at a concentration of 6.68E+08 to 1.24E+09 CFU. mL-1 depending on the culture method. The preparation steps of bacterial suspension have been listed and presented in detail in Appendix. C. [SOURCE: Page_96]

2100   3.5.2.2. Bacterial collection efficiency set-up The schematic of the experimental setup to evaluate the effect of sampling time on bacteria collection efficiency and according to the type of impingers is shown in Figure 3.12a. The impingers were filled with 20 mL of sterile DW. Two SKC BioSampler and/or AGI-4 were run in parallel at their nominal flow rates: one for 5 min and the other for 15 min. This minimized temperature and relative humidity as well as concentration fluctuations during sampling. [SOURCE: Page_96]

After sampling, the bacterial plates were incubated for 24–48 h hours at 37 °C (Appendix. C, C. 5). The number of the bacterial colonies, Ncul (CFU ) were then counted, and the results are used to calculate the airborne concentrations of the culturable cells, 𝐶𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 (CFU.m-3): [SOURCE: Page_96]

𝑁𝑐𝑢𝑙    𝑉𝑓𝑖𝑛 𝐶𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 = 1000 ×            ×                                                                 Eq. 3-11 𝑄𝑠 × 𝑡𝑠 𝑉𝑀𝑙𝑖𝑞 [SOURCE: Page_96]

Where: 𝑄𝑠 = the sampling flow rate (L.min-1); 𝑡𝑠 = sampling time (min); 𝑉𝑓𝑖𝑛 = Volume of the final 2110   sampling liquid in mL; and 𝑉𝑀𝑙𝑖𝑞 = volume of the liquid used for culturable analysis (µL). [SOURCE: Page_96]

95 [SOURCE: Page_96]

The BioTrak® has been chosen as a reference (Figure 3.12b). Given that the flow rate of the BioTrak® was 28.3 L.min-1, it has been run before and after each test for at least 15 min. The results of the impingers were therefore normalized based on the viable E.coli, CRef (viable), and the total aerosols, CRef(total) detected by BioTrak®. These normalizations are expressed as: [SOURCE: Page_97]

𝐶𝑆𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 𝐶𝐵𝑣𝑖𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒 =                                                                                          Eq. 3-12 𝐶𝑅𝑒𝑓 (𝑣𝑖𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒) [SOURCE: Page_97]

and, [SOURCE: Page_97]

𝐶𝑆𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 𝐶𝐵𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 =                                                                                           Eq. 3-13 𝐶𝑅𝑒𝑓 (𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙) [SOURCE: Page_97]

(a) [SOURCE: Page_97]

(b) Figure 3.12. The schematic diagram of the experimental setup to characterize the effect of sampling time on the viability of bacteria collected by impingers; (b) Experimental setup of BioTrak® in sampling 2120                                                    chamber [SOURCE: Page_97]

Results and discussion Effect of sampling time on physical collection efficiency By using the ABF as the tracer, the physical collection efficiency of the AGI–4 was 14.7, 17.2, 17 and 25.3% higher than the one of SKC BioSampler for 5, 15, 30, and 60 min sampling time, respectively (Figure 3.13a). However, the increase of the sampling time from 5 to 60 min had no significant influence [SOURCE: Page_97]

96 [SOURCE: Page_97]

on the physical efficiency of AGI–4 that could be considered constant (Figure 3.13a). For SKC Bio- sampler, a 8.3% reduction in physical collection efficiency was found from 30 min to 60 min. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Figure 3.13b shows that with increasing the sampling time of the impingers, the sampling efficiency corresponding the ratio of the mass of ABF extracted by the sampling liquid, MC, to that recovered by the 2130   glass fiber filters as a reference, decreased. This reduction was significant for AGI-4 only from 5 to 15 min, approximately 11.2%. For SKC BioSampler, we observe small variations between 5 to 30 min and a more significant decrease at 60 min (6.9%). [SOURCE: Page_98]

Assuming that the transmission efficiencies of the impingers were close to one, it was expected that the physical collection efficiency and sampling efficiency would be equal, but the results did not confirmed this fact. For this reason, Figure 3.13c shows the ratio (Eq. 3-10) of the mass of the ABF drawn into the impingers and that of the ABF detected through the reference, which represent the percentage of residual efficiency. These results were globally similar between the two impingers. Although, from 15 to 60 min, the residual efficiency of SKC BioSampler was slightly higher than the one of AGI–4 (about 6). The effect of the sampling time seems be more important at the beginning (the first 5 min) for AGI 4, and then the 2140   losses seem keep almost constant from 15 to 30 min around (6.65%) compared to the initial solution. These losses can be due to as the adherence of the ABF on the inner walls of inlet tube (transmission losses) or the collection reservoir of the impingers, which were in accordance with the conclusion of Han and Mainelis (2012) about latent losses. [SOURCE: Page_98]

(a)                                                (b) [SOURCE: Page_98]

97 [SOURCE: Page_98]

(c) Figure 3.13. (a) Physical collection efficiency of impingers at different sampling times, 𝜂𝑝ℎ𝑦𝑠 ; (b) Sampling efficiency, 𝜂𝑠𝑎𝑚𝑝𝑙𝑒 ; (c) Residual efficiency, 𝜂𝑅 . Error bars are ±1 standard deviation about a mean (Temperature: 18.9–23.7 °C, Relative humidity: 10-15%). [SOURCE: Page_99]

Characterization of the total losses [SOURCE: Page_99]

2150      A brief statistical analysis has been done by calculating the measurement errors (1-efficiency = loss), their averages and standard deviations. It has been mentioned that standard deviation was calculated as the square root of variance by determining the variation between each measurement error (%) relative to the average. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Table 3.4 shows that average of the measurement errors for the investigated AGI–4 was approximately 7.45%, considering ABF as the reference tracer and that the sampling time had no effect on physical collection efficiency. Comparison of the physical efficiency, the sampling efficiency, and the residual loss reveals that for sampling times of 5 min, the sampling efficiency of AGI–4 was higher than the glass fiber filter (as a reference). Therefore, when the concentration of the ABF in the air was high, the sampling time of 5min is recommended. [SOURCE: Page_99]

2160      SKC BioSampler had the highest measurement error for the sampling times of 60 min. So, when using DW as a collection liquid, the sampling times of between 5 and 30min was recommended. Moreover, the residual loss of SKC (3.76%) was less than AGI–4 (6.65%). [SOURCE: Page_99]

Effect of sampling time on the culturability of airborne Escherichia coli cells sampled by impingers The effect of sampling time on the culturability maintenance of impingers is shown in Figure 3.14. As 2170   mentioned earlier (see sections 3.3.4 and 3.5.2.2), in order to simultaneously compare the culturability maintenance of the impingers, the number of the culturable E.coli recovered by impingers (CFU.m-3) was normalized by dividing it by the total airborne bacteria detected by BioTrak® (#.m-3, with a counting efficiency of 100% for particles > 0.75 µm) in sampling chamber. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Figure 3.14 shows that by increasing the sampling time from 5 min to 15 min, the culturability of SKC BioSampler and AGI–4 decreased. This reduction for SKC and AGI–4 sampling was 14% and 37%, respectively when using total aerosols detected by BioTrak as the reference. Henningson et al. (1988) found that when only collection efficiency was targeted, AGI–4 was preferable, but particle rebound greatly affected the viability of the bacteria in the AGI–4 test. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Lin et al. (2000) stated that the swirling motion of the collection liquid in the SKC BioSampler generates 2180   very few bubbles compared to AGI–4 and therefore causes less re-entrainment of the microorganisms collected. It is in accordance with our SKC BioSampler tested for 15 min sampling duration. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Given that SKC BioSampler has the highest culturability maintenance for E.coli cells at 15 min, the ratio of the culturability obtained by AGI-4 and SKC BioSamplerthe is 0.93. It can therefore be inferred that culturability of AGI–4 is equal to culturability of SKC multiplied by 0.93. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Figure 3.14. Effect of sampling time on culturability of. CBtotal represents the ratio of culturable E.coli recovered by impingers (CFU.m-3) to total aerosols detected by BioTrak® (#.m-3). Error bars are ±1 standard deviation about a mean. [SOURCE: Page_100]

2190 [SOURCE: Page_100]

99 [SOURCE: Page_100]

Comparison of samplers collecting/detecting the airborne live Escherichia coli: Impingers and BioTrak® Figure 3.15 represents the ratio of the culturable E.coli cells collected by impingers (CFU.m-3) to the number of viable airborne bacteria counted by BioTrak® (vcnt.m-3). Given that all the viable Escherichia coli cells cannot be grown using conventional culture media, it was expected that regardless of the sampling duration, the values of these ratios were higher or at least equal to one (Figure 3.15). It can therefore be concluded that BioTrak® provides a fast but less accurate number of viable particles compared to the impingers. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Besides, as can be seen in Figure 3.15, if the number of viable bacteria counted by BioTrak® was taken 2200   as the reference, similar to the previous section (see 3.6.2), from 5 to 15 min, the culturability of SKC BioSampler fell by 22%, while the reduction value for AGI-4 remained constant (37%). [SOURCE: Page_101]

Figure 3.15. (a) Comparison of samplers collecting/detecting the airborne live Escherichia coli: Impingers and BioTrak. CBviable represents the ratio of the culturable E.coli recovered by impingers (CFU.m-3) to viable aerosols detected by BioTrak® (vcnt.m-3). Error bars are ±1 standard deviation about a mean. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Conclusion The objective of this study was to investigate the physical collection efficiency of active samplers when 2210   using different tracers (fluorescent ABF and E.coli) at different sampling times (5-60 min). Furthermore, the effect of impinger types and sampling times (5 and 15min) on the culturability maintenance of the E.coli cells were assessed. [SOURCE: Page_101]

A comparison of the physical efficiency of impingers revealed that the efficiency of AGI-4 was almost 16.3% higher than the SKC Biosample in the range of 5–30 min. As the sampling time is an important parameter in field studies, where the concentration of ABF in aerosol (as a tracer of the droplet dispersion) is limited, it is therefore recommended to use the AGI–4 as an active sampler. Note that this is in the case [SOURCE: Page_101]

100 [SOURCE: Page_101]

when demineralized water is used as a liquid collection. Otherwise, since the SKC BioSampler was developed for using the non-evaporating liquid (mineral oil), its performance will be certainly higher than AGI–4, especially since its residual loss is lower. [SOURCE: Page_102]

2220      Increasing sampling time from 5 min to 15 min, reduced the culturability of E.coli in both AGI–4 and SKC by a factor of 37% and 14%, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Also, it was showed that the BioTrak® can measure in real-time the total number of particles in the air, which can be useful as a rapid technique, especially, for quantifying the potential presence of contaminants, but its accuracy in measuring the viable particles was lower than accuracy obtained with the impingers, particularly in low relative humidity conditions. In addition, it physically detects the presence of viable bacteria but does not identify its type. [SOURCE: Page_102]

101 [SOURCE: Page_102]

Droplet size distribution in moving air [SOURCE: Page_103]

Introduction 2230     In cases where the spray nozzle has been mounted in a downward direction, such as some types of spray nozzles used for center pivot and linear irrigation systems or fogger mist sprinklers, apart from all the factors mentioned in the Chapter 1, the height of nozzle from the target/soil and spray angle can play an important role in the control of downwind dispersion of droplets/aerosols. [SOURCE: Page_103]

In this chapter, the factors affecting the spread of small droplets (<150 μm) from the spray nozzle mounted in downward direction including nozzle height, at least over short distance have been evaluated. They would be of help to healthcare farmers in close proximity of sprinkler irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Materials and methods Description of Wind Tunnel The experiments of this chapter have been conducted in an open circuit wind tunnel, situated in the 2240   Laboratory for the Science of Risks (LSR) at ‘Institute Mines Telecom (IMT)’ Mines Ales, in the south of France (Figure 4.1). Air was drawn into the tunnel from the laboratory environment through an aerodynamically designed cone with the honeycomb wall that provide an almost uniform velocity profile throughout the working section. It then entered the working section and moved through a diffuser before passing through a second honeycomb and then to a variable-speed axial fan. The air leaved the fan, passed through a cylindrical unit and then backed out to the laboratory. The rectangular working section was 0.35 (height) × 0.5 (width) m^2 in cross section and 2 m long. The roof and sides of the working section were made of transparent Plexiglas sheets, which allowed executing the experiment with optical techniques. However, in order to install the equipment, described in the following chapters, it was necessary to drill holes in the roof at several points, causing the Plexiglas sheet to crack, which we replaced it with a wooden 2250   plate. The floor of the working section was also changed into the removable puzzle pieces, which allowed [SOURCE: Page_103]

102 [SOURCE: Page_103]

to place the samplers with the variable heights as well as a container beneath the spray nozzle, in different positions within the working section (see section 4.2.7). Besides, it was covered with an artificial grass (6 mm thickness) to adsorb the deposited water on the floor surface and so to prevent water runoff when using the spray nozzle (see section 4.2.4). [SOURCE: Page_104]

Flow direction [SOURCE: Page_104]

## X         Z

Figure 4.1. Open Circuit Wind-Tunnel overview [SOURCE: Page_104]

Air velocities measurements The wind tunnel was able to generate the wind speed up to 15 m.s-1, which was controlled by a housing 2260   of electronic variable speed drive (Digidrive SK, Lorey Somer) by changing the frequency (accuracy: 0.1 Hz). The frequency values were converted to mean air velocities (Curve-fitting: Fr–U) through a series of the measurements by a hot wire anemometer (Kimo VT 100, maximum error of ± 0.1 m. s –1) across two cross sectional area (Y–Z axis) located 0.95 and 1.80 m downwind from the inlet of the working section. The PIV measurements were also carried out in a field of 91 × 91 mm^2 along the X-Y axis (its center position: Y=0.175 m, Z= 0.25 m) in which the flow field was laden with smoke to characterize the airflow velocity in the center plane of the working section. The scheme of measurement points and the details of the characterization of the wind tunnel airflow have been presented in Appendix. D. As shown in the synthesis Table 4.1, air velocity measurements were performed at the locations where samplers or optical systems would be placed during the planned experiments. The values of mean air velocities in Y direction 2270   (𝑣̅ ) revealed that regardless the airflow velocity, the deviation of the local air velocity from the mainstream velocity was generally less than 0.6 degrees. Given the limitations due to the edge of upper boundary layers, ULB (Appendix. D) , the passive samplers have to be placed up to a maximum height of 0.275 m above the working section floor. The mean airflow velocities denoted by 𝑈, were defined based on the measurements in the center plane. The further experiments were therefore made with wind tunnel operating at 𝑈 of 1.2, 2.25, 3.3 and 5.0 m.s-1. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Laboratory control conditions The laboratory was equipped with a heat reclaim ventilation unit (Daikin HRV Europe, Belgium) and the air temperature was maintained at 20±1°C. Nevertheless, it was not possible to control the relative humidity in the wind tunnel. The relative humidity and temperature were recorded, respectively, by two Hygrometers (testo 48H) and the wire thermocouples at inlet and outlet of the working section (Figure 2290   4.2). The temperature from the thermocouple was read through a background analog input channel (National instruments 9211, CompactDAQ “cDAQ3Mod^2”, Hungary), using LabVIEW software to configure the measurements and acquire the data with a sampling rate of once per second. Table 4.3 shows some of properties of air at a temperature of 20 °C. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Characteristic of spray nozzle As it was not possible with a sprinkler in controlled conditions, an axial-flow hollow cone nozzle sprayer 2300   (Type: 220.085, Lechler GmbH, Germany) with a spray angle of 80° was considered in this part of thesis. This type of the spray nozzle with integrated strainer was selected due to its narrow spray pattern, and extremely fine droplets (fog–like) produced by spiral grooves in the swirl insert (Figure 4.3). The total water volume atomized in the working section of wind tunnel, the spray pressure was maintained at 200 𝑃 kPa to provide a flow rate of 0.025 L.min-1 [𝑉2 = 𝑉1 (𝑃2 )0.5]. The accuracy of the pressure–Flow rate was 1 [SOURCE: Page_106]

controlled by a balance (KERNPKS, precision: 0.01 g, KERN & Sohn GmbH, Germany) situated underneath the nozzle tip weighting the captured droplets for at least 30 minutes. The specifications of the spray nozzle has been summarized in Table 4.4. [SOURCE: Page_106]

2310                      Figure 4.3. Axial-flow hollow cone nozzle; dimension; spray pattern [SOURCE: Page_106]

Spray nozzle specification Spray angle       Orifice diameter   Mesh size   Flow rate at     Initial projection    Spray diameter at [degrees]              [mm]          [mm]        p=200 kPa     velocity at P=200 kPa     H** =100 mm [L.min-1]            [m.s-1]*               [mm] 80                0.25            0.1         0.025                 8.49                 140 𝑞 *𝑈𝑗 = ( ) Where, 𝑞 is Flow rate, a is nozzle cross–sectional area;** H is the axial distance from nozzle top 𝑎 [SOURCE: Page_107]

Initial droplet size distribution from sprayer in laboratory conditions Prior to the experiments in the wind tunnel, a laser-diffraction-based drop size analyzer, Malvern Spraytec (Malvern Panalytical, Malvern, UK), was used to characterize the initial droplet size distribution of the spray nozzle in no wind conditions. The experimentation was carried out at ReducPol testing laboratory, located in the UMR ITAP INRAE Montpellier. As illustrated in Figure 4.4, this instrument consists of: [SOURCE: Page_107]

•        Transmitter which uses a He-Ne (Helium-Neon) laser to produce a collimated laser beam that 2320                   come through the spray atomized to measurement zone; [SOURCE: Page_107]

•        Receiver module which holds the lens assembly and photodiodes detectors to detect the light diffraction and converting the light detected into electrical signals; [SOURCE: Page_107]

•        Analogue and the Digital electronics boards for processing the signals and [SOURCE: Page_107]

•        the Software to analyze the light diffraction patterns by a scattering model to calculate the spray size distribution (Malvern Instruments Ltd., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_107]

The fundamentals of Spraytec spray measurement is explained in detail in Lefebvre and McDonell (2017) and Malvern Instruments Ltd. (2007). [SOURCE: Page_107]

Malvern Spraytec, which is considered as a spatial sampling device, has been widely used for the studies on atomization and spray drift application process (Fritz and Hoffmann, 2016; Kippax et al., 2002; Sirmour 2330   and Verma, 2019), and on the aerosol properties (Dayal et al., 2004; Z. Y. Han et al., 2013; Haynes et al., 2004) thanks to its ability to produce an overall view of size distribution. [SOURCE: Page_107]

However, the results of the laser diffraction technique can be influenced by a large range of factors, including, correct alignment, sufficient density to detect, fitting method to obtain the droplet size distribution, the droplets acceleration and deceleration (Chin et al., 1986) and the probability of overestimation of the number of small droplets, particularly, close to nozzle, due to their low velocity and thus higher concentration in the sample volume (Sijs et al., 2021) or inversely, the increased errors due to droplet evaporation at longer distances (Wang and Lefebvre, 1987), differences in spray breakup mechanisms away from the centerline (Dodge, 1984; Kooij et al., 2018). According to Emekwuru and Watkins (2010) and Wang and Lefebvre (1987), a distance of 50 mm is the best minimum axial position 2340   beyond which the results were consistent. Wang and Lefebvre (1987) estimated that the 150 mm was the [SOURCE: Page_107]

106 [SOURCE: Page_107]

best downstream distance. Therefore, the application of the device inside the wind tunnel at different distances downwind is not indeed pertinent. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Figure 4.4. Malvern Spraytec Particle Analyzer [SOURCE: Page_108]

When measuring droplet characteristics, the nozzle was correctly positioned with the nozzle body whereby, the spray plume downwards into the laser beam. The pressure was set at 200 kPa by a pressure regulator. It was measured using a pressure transmitter (Keller, Switzerland) which has a range of 0–1000 kPa and an accuracy of ± 0.01%. [SOURCE: Page_108]

The nozzle body was attached to a traverse system, which allowed the spray to pass through the laser 2350   beam with travel speed of 2.5 mm.s-1. We used the Malvern Spraytec with a 750 mm lens that can detect droplets from 2 μm to 2000 μm. The diameter of the laser beam was about 15 mm. The Spraytec was set to measure the droplet size distribution at axial distances of 50 mm, 100 mm and 150 mm from the nozzle tip, and radial distances from the spray centerline with one sampling every second assuming that the spray was almost symmetrical. At each distances from the tip nozzle, six full passages have been conducted along the diameter of the spray. The software of the Spraytec also employs the Lorentz–Mie theory and Fraunhofer approximation to account for the contribution to the angular light energy distribution of scattering through small droplets. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Hydraulic circuit To evaluate the size of airborne droplets generated by the axial-flow hollow cone nozzle under 2360   controlled conditions in the wind tunnel, as shown in Figure 4.5, the demineralized water was delivered to the spray nozzle from a pressure vessel (Sartorius Stedim 5L, Germany). It was run at 200 kPa by feeding air compressor unit (BLACK & DECKER®, Mauk). The pressure was controlled through a regulator (SMC, China), installed between the vessel and the compressor by a pressure hose. Besides, the pressure was recorded at 10 Hz frequency using a pressure transmitter (HD 9408T, accuracy: ± 0.25%) at the end of the outlet tube of vessel. Two valves at the two ends of outlet tube controlled the start and stop of experiments runs. Finally, the nozzle tap was connected to a brass bushing at the end of a copper pipe. The [SOURCE: Page_108]

107 [SOURCE: Page_108]

copper pipe held by a tripod clamp, then passed from the top of the working section through the pre-drilled holes, by which spray plume downwards into the working section and could be moved up or down at the desired points (see Figure 4.7). [SOURCE: Page_109]

2370      Note that the temperature of demineralized water within the vessel was maintained at 20±1 °C by a heat mat connected with a temperature regulator. [SOURCE: Page_109]

(a)                      (b) Figure 4.5. (a) Circuit hydraulic overview, (b): Image of the spray nozzle connected to the mobile copper pipe, the tripod clamp, the pressure transmitter and the valve. [SOURCE: Page_109]

PDPA set-up Two component Phase Doppler Particle Analysis (PDPA-2D) from Dantec Dynamic, as shown in Figure 4.6, was equipped by a Flowexplorer DPSS transmitter–compact with 532 nm (green) and 561 nm (yellow–green) laser wavelengths, which have been adjusted on 50 mW output power. Both lasers are first received in a Bragg cell where the initial beams are separated into two equal beams with a frequency 2380   difference of 80 MHz. A standard front lens with 1000 mm focus-length was used for the transmitter, forming a PDPA measurement volume of 0.5293 × 0.5291× 19.6 mm^3, along the principal directions. The scattered light created from a droplet passing this measuring volume forms an interference fringe pattern. The frequency of this scattered light is proportional to the droplet velocity while the spatial frequency of the interference fringe pattern is inversely proportional to the drop diameter. The Fiber PDA receiver probe was set up in a forward refraction mode at an angle of 30° and focal length of the lens of 1000 mm to collect the scattered laser light. Briefly, the Fiber PDA consists of front lens (adjust the measurement distance), an optical mask system A (which makes it possible to differentiate and adapt the collection angle for each of the three detectors, U$_1$, U$_2$, U$_3$), the spatial filters, coupling optics of the different flows collected on multimode fibers. These run in a single cable leading to the Fiber PDA detector unit which 2390   comprises three photomultipliers (PM) and components for calibration and for optical filtering. Three photomultiplier tubes convert the scattered light into electrical signals to be processed for velocity and size information by the Burst Spectrum Analyser (BSA) also from Dantec Dynamic. The BSA software gives [SOURCE: Page_109]

108 [SOURCE: Page_109]

complete control over the presentation of the instruments and acquisition of the data and shows in real- time the PDPA phase, histograms (droplet size, velocity) and profile plots. More information about how to choice the measurement set-up and to validate the detected droplets (through the phase plot visualization in real time) can be found in Dantec Dynamics (2006) and Ofner (2001). The best results were achieved by the configuration presented in Table 4.5. [SOURCE: Page_110]

2400                        (a)                 (b)                 (c)                  (d)                 (e) Figure 4.6. [SOURCE: Page_110]

Configuration of the PDPA experiments The experimental set-up of PDPA is shown in Figure 4.7 with an overall view of the placements of spray nozzle along the X and Y directions. In the present case, the measurement point of PDPA was fixed in the center of cross section (Height = 0.175 m, width = 0.25 m), while the spray nozzle was situated at 2410   four axial distances upstream of the measurement point, 0.45, 0.85, 1.25 and 1.65 meters. Note that these measurement distances were selected according to the standard ISO 22856 (2008), and due to the limitations imposed by the presence of the metal frames/ rods on the roof of the working section (at the distances of 0.5, 1 and 1.5 m from the entrance). The nozzle tip was set in vertical position, at three heights of 0.1, 0.175 and 0.275 m above the working section floor. At each position, the droplet size distribution was measured at the air velocities of 1.2, 2.25, 3.3 and 5.0 m.s-1. At least 5,000 droplets were measured at each position except in the cases where the number of the airborne spray droplets that reached to the measurement point were too limited or negligible. To ensure the stability of measurements, the acquisition time taken in these cases was at least 15 minutes. Each experiment was repeated three times. However, as previously mentioned, the temperature was kept at 20 ± 1 °C and only the data obtained under a relative 2420   humidity of about 40–50% have been retained for the analysis. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Figure 4.7. Schematic of the spatial location of the PDPA set-up and placing the nozzle tip at different positions upstream of the measurement point of PDPA. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Data analysis For each configuration, several parameters were determined from the cumulative volume (number) of droplet size distribution following percentile characteristics calculated: The aerodynamic mean diameter, 𝐷𝑎 ; volume mean diameter, 𝐷𝑣30; Sauter mean diameter, 𝐷𝑣32; volume (numeric) median diameter, 𝐷𝑣50 (𝐷𝑛50 ), also known as VMD (NMD). 𝐷𝑣10 (𝐷𝑛10 ), and 𝐷𝑣90 (𝐷𝑛90 ), which represent the droplet diameter values whereby 10% and 90% of the volume (total number) of spray are in droplets smaller than 2430   these values, respectively, were also calculated. [SOURCE: Page_112]

The Sauter mean diameter is commonly used as an indicator of important processes involving the ratio of inertia to aerodynamic drag forces (Bayvel and Orzechowski, 1993). It is defined as, [SOURCE: Page_112]

𝑖=𝑛                                                                                    Eq. 4-1 𝐷𝑖3 𝐷32 = ∑ 𝐷𝑖2 𝑖=1 [SOURCE: Page_112]

where, 𝐷𝑖 is the diameter of the ith droplet and n is the number of droplets. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Furthermore, the relative span factor (RSF) is found to be one of the dominant parameters indicating the homogeneity of the droplet size, which is expressed as, [SOURCE: Page_112]

𝐷𝑣90 − 𝐷𝑣10                                                                           Eq. 4-2 𝑅𝑆𝐹 = 𝐷𝑣50 [SOURCE: Page_112]

111 [SOURCE: Page_112]

In the present study, the cumulative volume of the droplet distribution was also fitted by Rosin-Rammler function (Weibull, 1951) which is widely used in spray studies (González-Tello et al., 2008; Jain et al., 2014) as defined, [SOURCE: Page_113]

𝐷 𝛿                                                                              Eq. 4-3 𝐹(𝐷) = 1 − exp [− ( ) ] 𝑋 [SOURCE: Page_113]

where, 𝐹(𝐷) denotes fraction of the total volume contained in droplets of diameter less than 𝐷, 𝑋 2440   denotes the characteristic size parameter, defined as the size at which 63.2% [1 − 𝑒 −1 ] of droplets are smaller than 𝑋, and 𝛿 is the droplet uniformity parameter, whose higher value represents less non- uniformity of droplet size. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Moreover, by deriving the Eq. 4-3, a function of the volume distribution 𝑓(𝐷) is yielded as follow, [SOURCE: Page_113]

𝛿 𝛿−1          𝐷 𝛿                                                                         Eq. 4-4 𝑓(𝐷) =      𝐷  exp [− (   ) ] 𝑋𝛿             𝑋 [SOURCE: Page_113]

Results Initial droplet size distribution Figure 4.8 shows the trend of 𝐷𝑣10 , 𝐷𝑣50 and 𝐷𝑣90 variation along the diameter of the spray at three axial distances from the nozzle tip, according to sampling time. Each segment, one of which is enclosed in a green rectangle, represents a full passage of spray nozzle in front of the laser beam of Malvern Spraytec (Figure 4.8). The results show that all droplet size distributions were similar and quasi-parabolic, 2450   symmetrical in respect to the centerline. Regardless of the axial distance from the nozzle tip, the range of the droplets size at the center of the spray was higher than towards the edge of the spray. It was expected that the number of droplets in the center of a hollow cone nozzle sprayer would be limited. But the profile obtained in this case is completely different, which can be explained by the selection of the lowest working pressure for this type of sprayer. [SOURCE: Page_113]

As the axial distance increased from 50 mm to 150 mm, the number of droplets inferior to 50 µm decreased sharply. Although, this increase has occurred at the edges of the spray cone. For this reason, it is seen that the trends of droplet size in term of 𝐷𝑣50 and 𝐷𝑣90 at the distance of 50 mm sloped in the opposite direction from these trends at a distance of 150 mm. In this case, He et al. (2022) pointed out that, during the descending movement of the droplets in a spray, small droplets tend to move to the centerline 2460   due to the air entrainment effect caused by the interaction of droplets with the surrounding gaseous phase with speed difference. The larger droplet was less affected by the air entrainment effect for its large initial kinetic energy, so it had a strong diffusion ability to reach the edge of the spray. [SOURCE: Page_113]

112 [SOURCE: Page_113]

These results allowed us to interpret the crosswind distribution of airborne spray plume at various distances from the nozzle head in the Appendix. E. [SOURCE: Page_114]

(a)                                                  (b) [SOURCE: Page_114]

(c) Figure 4.8. Trend of 𝐷𝑣10 , 𝐷𝑣50, and 𝐷𝑣90 variation along the diameter of spray plume at axial distances of, (a): 50 mm, (b):100 mm, and (c): 150 mm from the nozzle tip. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Malvern Spraytec system generated an overall volume frequency and cumulative volume of droplet size 2470   distributions at three axial distances of 50, 100, and 150 mm from the nozzle tip, presented in Figure 4.9. First, increasing the axial distance from 50 mm to 150 mm shifted the cumulative volume curve towards the larger droplets. The uniformity of the spray (narrower distribution) is also enhanced as it moves away from the nozzle. In fact, as the spray moves axially downward, the frequency (probability) of small droplets experiencing an effective collision force due to collisions between neighboring droplets increases. Moreover, by increasing the axial distances from the nozzle tip the other forces as aerodynamic drag and gravity become more predominant than the inertia force, as mentioned in the literature (Santangelo, 2012; Shrigondekar et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_114]

Table 4.6 shows the values of parameters representing the droplet size distribution of the spray nozzle at three axial distances from nozzle. The values of RSF also confirm that the most uniformity of droplet 2480   size distribution is related to the distance of 150 mm. Furthermore, the volume diameter of almost 90 percent of the droplets was less than 150 microns in all three distances. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Effect of air velocity and nozzle height on droplet size distribution along the centerline of the cross-section using PDPA [SOURCE: Page_116]

4.3.2.1. Comparison of the downwind airborne droplet size spectrum Figure 4.10(a-l) shows the airborne droplet characteristics ( 𝐷𝑣10 , 𝐷𝑣30 , 𝐷𝑣32, 𝐷𝑣50 , 𝐷𝑣90, 𝐷𝑛10, 𝐷𝑛50 2490   and 𝐷𝑛90 ) as a function of air velocity, at locations 0.45 to 1.65 m downwind from the nozzle tip that was installed at heights of 0.1, 0.175 and 0.275 m above the working section floor. As mentioned earlier, considering the main purpose of these experiments, that was to characterize the droplet size distribution near the inlet of the impingers, the measurements had only been conducted along the centerline of the cross-section (Height = 0.175 m, width = 0.25 m). [SOURCE: Page_116]

Regardless of the distance from the nozzle tip, by decreasing a nozzle height from 0.275 to 0.1 m above the working section floor, 90% of the overall volume of droplets being less than or equal to 138 µm for 0.275 m reached 58 µm for 0.1 m (Figure 4.10). [SOURCE: Page_116]

At a nozzle height of 0.1 m above the working section floor (Figure 4.10 [a-d]), and at two air velocities of 1.2 and 2.25 m.s-1, no droplet was observed at any examined distance. For the same nozzle height, 2500   increasing the air velocity from 3.3 to 5.0 m.s-1 did not change the droplet parameters. The effect of distance on droplet characteristics was not significant, apart for the larger droplets which constitute 90% of the total volume (𝐷𝑣90 ) that decreased from 56 µm at a distance of 0.85 m downwind to 41 µm at a distance of 1.65 m downwind when the air velocity increased. [SOURCE: Page_116]

At a nozzle height of 0.175 m above the working section floor (Figure 4.10 [e-h]), and for an air velocity of 1.2 m.s-1, no droplet size was detected by PDPA at a distance of 1.25 m from the source. Similar to the results obtained from a nozzle height of 0.1 m, the large droplets proportion, which are expected to be more affected by gravity force, decreased with distances. Although the almost constant 𝐷𝑛90 values from a distance of 0.85m to 1.65 m confirms that the contribution of the large droplets from the total droplets is few, but this small number plays an important role in the total volume. [SOURCE: Page_116]

2510      And, at a nozzle height of 0.275 m above the working section floor (Figure 4.10 [i-l]), regardless of the air velocity values, increasing the distance from 0.45 m to 1.65 m, decreased the all the airborne droplet parameters values. Comparison of the initial droplet size distribution at axial distance of 100 mm from the nozzle tip (see Table 4.6) and (Figure 4.10 [i-j]) indicates that an air velocity of 5 m.s-1 caused the direct transport of all the droplets inferior to around 135 µm (𝐷𝑣90 ) to a distance of 0.45 m, and the droplets below around 124 µm to a distance of 0.85 m. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Finally, Table 4.7 presents the Pearson correlation coefficients, which estimate the binary linear association between variables of air velocity (𝑈), distance (𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡), and nozzle height and 𝐷𝑣50 and 𝐷𝑣90 of downwind airborne spray. It estimates that the effect of 𝑈 and nozzle height on both 𝐷𝑣50 and 𝐷𝑣90 was statistically significant at 95 % (even 99%) level of confidence, but the distance had no impact on 𝐷𝑣50 at 2520   least up to 1.65 m from the source. [SOURCE: Page_116]

4.3.2.2. Downwind airborne droplet size distribution Figure 4.11 and Figure 4.12 represent function, 𝑓(𝐷), and cumulative, 𝐹(𝐷), volume of downwind airborne droplet size distributions using Rosin-Rammler method as a function of the air velocities (1.2, 2.25, 3.3 and 5.0 m.s-1) at various distances (0.45, 0.85, 1.25, and 1.65 m) downwind from the nozzle positioned at a height of 0.175 and 0.275 m above the floor, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_119]

As shown in Figure 4.11 (a-d), regardless of downwind distance from the point source, peak of the volume distribution curves, defined as a modal diameter, 𝐷𝑀 , represents the greatest number of droplets in the range of 20–40 μm. At the same distance, by increasing the air velocity values, the probability or 2540   volume frequency of 𝐷𝑀 decreased. [SOURCE: Page_119]

At a nozzle height of 0.275 m above the floor [Figure 4.12(a-d)], increasing the air velocities from 1.2 m.s-1 to 5.0 m.s-1 increases the modal diameter values. As the distance increases (from 0.45 to 1.65 m), these values decrease further, and the curves of 𝐹(𝐷) resulting from an air velocity of 2 m.s-1 become closer to the ones related to air velocities of 5.0 m.s-1. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Referring to the Figure 4.12, to assess the effect of air velocity on the droplet collection efficiency of the samplers (Appendix. E), the optimal choice of droplet size distribution based on the equal distribution, was as follows: 2 m.s-1 at 0.45 m, 3 m.s-1 at 0.85 m and 4.5 m.s-1 at 1.25 m. This allowed us to assess the effect of air velocity on the collection efficiency of the active samplers for a given airborne droplet size. [SOURCE: Page_119]

2560         Characterization of airborne spray transport in the center of cross-section along wind direction using PDPA Figure 4.13 shows the effect of nozzle height and air velocity on airborne spray transport (% of spray volume that has been transported) at measurement points downwind from the source using PDPA. In order to simultaneously compare the effect of air velocity on airborne spray transport in downwind direction dispersed from the nozzle positioned at two heights (0.175 and 0.275 m) above the floor, the airborne transported volumes were normalized by dividing them by their respective highest value (1.78E-04 mLdroplet.min-1/mLnozzle.min-1 ). The results indicate that generally, by increasing the height of nozzle from 0.175 m (Figure 4.13a) to 0.275 m (Figure 4.13b), the volume transported to the center of crosswind direction increased about 99%. [SOURCE: Page_121]

2570      Based on the airborne spray transport values downwind of the spray nozzle positioned at a height of 0.275 m above the floor, the optimal configuration (combination of air velocities and distances) for positioning of the active samplers to receive almost equal concentration, was as follows: 2 m.s-1 at 0.45 m, 3 m.s-1 at 0.85 m and 5.0 m.s-1 at 1.65 m (or 5.0 m.s-1 at 1.25 m, subjected to the same droplet size distribution) [see Appendix. E]. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Note that for a nozzle height of 0.175 m above the floor, no downwind distance was found that received the same volume of airborne spray under examined air velocities. [SOURCE: Page_121]

[SOURCE: Page_121]

120 [SOURCE: Page_121]

Conclusion In this chapter, the effect of air velocities (1.2, 2.25, 3.3 and 5.0 m.s -1) and spray nozzle heights (0.1, 0.175 and 0.275 m) on airborne spray transport and droplet size have been evaluated using PDPA. The plume is generated by a spray nozzle producing extremely fine droplets (< 150 μm, supposed to induce to health risks). [SOURCE: Page_122]

The first purpose of this part of the study was to introduce an optimal configuration (combination of 2590   spray nozzle height, distances, and air velocities) for evaluating the performance of the active samplers (see Appendix. E), which was as follows: spray nozzle positioned at a height of 0.275 m above the floor, and applying an air velocity of 2 m.s-1 at a distance of 0.45 m, 3 m.s-1 at 0.85 m and 4.5 m.s-1 at 1.25 m (despite different airborne spray volume). [SOURCE: Page_122]

The results showed that the nozzle height is an important factor influencing airborne dispersion. Regardless of the air velocity values and downwind distances from the nozzle tip, decreasing the nozzle height from 0.275 to 0.175 m, in other word, by bringing the nozzle to the height of the measurement points (i.e. inlet of the samplers), decreases the amount of airborne transport by 99%. [SOURCE: Page_122]

For the nozzle height of 0.175 m above the floor, except for the air velocity of 1 m.s-1, the total droplet size distribution in airborne spray varied very little with air velocity and distances, but the factor of air 2600   velocity significantly impacts (at 95%) on the % of transported volume due to the direct transport of larger droplets in the last 10% of the distribution range (𝐷𝑉90). [SOURCE: Page_122]

For the nozzle height of 0.275 m above the floor, except for the air velocity of 1 m.s-1, the droplet size distribution and consequently airborne spray transport varied according the air velocities, but this variation decreases with distance. [SOURCE: Page_122]

121 [SOURCE: Page_122]

Perspective of Part II [SOURCE: Page_123]

A number of experiments were performed to develop a protocol for characterizing the physical 2610   collection efficiency of the samplers when using Polystyrene Latex Spheres in the range of 0.3 to 2 μm under various concentrations and sampling times, in the laboratory of LGEI (Chapter 3). However, the studies have shown that the ultrasonic humidifier was not capable to aerosolize these beads. Therefore, we replaced a Collison Nebulizer 6 jets (CH Technologies, USA), but because an electrostatic charge neutralizer was needed and due to time constraints, it was not possible to perform these experiments as a part of the present thesis project. [SOURCE: Page_123]

For future experiments in this facility where Glass fiber filter (A/E, SKC Ltd, UK) will be used as a reference sampler next to each of the impingers, it is recommended to modify the head filter holder (two- piece plastic cassettes). It could be designed in such a way that a 70–110 mm long stainless steel probe with a cross sectional area corresponding to the inlet diameter and wall thickness of the impinger will be 2620   set to a cone (tangential design) to minimize aerodynamic blockage. It will be used by inserting the cone into the middle ring of an open-face three-piece cassette containing the glass fiber filter. Note that the exact length of the tube should be calculated according to particle size and required flow rate. In other cases, and further, in moving air, a sharp-edged, thin-walled probe will be used applying isokinetic conditions to measure the aerosol concentrations. This method can be carried out based on models described in the literature (Görner et al., 2010; Paik and Vincent, 2002). After each experiment, any particles/ ABF tracer that may have deposited on the inner tube wall will be rinsed and removed passing the rinsing liquid through a second Glass fiber filter or washing the head filter with demineralized water. The sample will be then measured using a fluorescence Spectrometer for ABF tracer or other techniques such as flow cytometry. [SOURCE: Page_123]

2630      This study was limited to the impingers with flow rates of 6 and 12.5 L.min-1 that can simulate the particles entering either the mouth or nose (worst case situation is the oral breathing of 20 L.min -1). Future studies will focus on even higher flow rates sampling, preferably over 100 L.min-1, using a liquid- based sampler such a BioGuardian Air Sampler that are particularly adapted for our understanding toward the [SOURCE: Page_123]

122 [SOURCE: Page_123]

dispersion of low bioaerosol concentrations from spray irrigation of TWW. In addition, application of other methods such as qPCR or epifluorescent for assessment of the viability of bioaerosols are also needed to strengthen the study. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Due to limitations caused by the dimension of wind tunnel (Chapter 4), it was not possible to executer a physical model to characterize and simulate short-range airborne transport downwind of a sprinkler 2640   irrigation such as the center pivot and linear systems in control conditions (provide a similar operating conditions: initial air velocity, operating pressure, etc.). Nevertheless, given the results from the impact of nozzle height on airborne spray transport, it is possible, as a suggestion, to reduce health risks for the farmers in proximity of radial irrigation by applying a riser height shorter than the height of personnel (1.5 m). [SOURCE: Page_124]

Furthermore, apart from the case of sprinkler irrigation, the data base created in this chapter could be useful for big data analysis of droplet transmission of pathogens such as SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) from the sources of cough [droplet size between 0.62 and 15.9 µm (Dhand and Li, 2020)], sneeze [ droplets between 0.5-12 µm in size (Tang et al., 2006)] or normal speaking [1 to 500 µm in diameter (Stadnytskyi et al., 2020)] in moving air. [SOURCE: Page_124]

2650      Further study (presented in Appendix. E) will focus on characterizing the collection efficiency of two common passive samplers: PVC lines and plastic spheres, and two active samplers: AGI-4 and SKC BioSampler in wind tunnel. The aim will be to select the most appropriate sampler for collecting airborne transport under field conditions. This work will define a methodology for evaluating collection efficiency using a combination of small‐scale wind tunnel and mathematical evaluations for active and passive samplers under various air velocities. [SOURCE: Page_124]

In addition, a preliminary study has been conducted in wind tunnel conditions using Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) technique. The aims were to determine the length of disturbance of air stream upstream the sampler inlet under non-isokinetic, and to estimate the Stokes numbers of defined airborne droplets projected into the air upstream the sampler inlet using PIV experiments. However, the results were not 2660   presented in this thesis, because our perspective is to complete the experiments in a closed circuit wind tunnel in order to i) evaluate the effect of aerosol size (microspheres with diameters between 0.3 and 10 µm) on the aspiration efficiency of active samplers under non-isokinetic conditions and turbulent airflow (Re >2300) and ii) compare the results with validated empirical models for the laminar flow regime. [SOURCE: Page_124]

123 [SOURCE: Page_124]

Part III. In-situ Experiments [SOURCE: Page_125]

Contribution to the thesis project: The objectives of the work presented in this part of thesis were : [SOURCE: Page_125]

•    to characterize the distribution of the fine droplet plume emanating from a sprinkler head in the horizontal and vertical axes under different meteorological conditions and [SOURCE: Page_125]

•    to model i) the short-range airborne and ii) sediment transport based on the combination of meteorological and spatial parameters. Nine experiments have been conducted outdoor in various environmental. [SOURCE: Page_125]

The main part of this chapter has been published in the journal of Irrigation Science (Zardari et al., 2022): Zardari, M., Tomas, S., Molle, B., Courault, D., 2022. Empirical modeling of aerosol transport from sprinkler irrigation. Irrig. Sci. https://doi.org/10.1007/s^00271-022-00822-x [SOURCE: Page_125]

. [SOURCE: Page_125]

124 [SOURCE: Page_125]

Transport and distribution of 2670   aerosols during sprinkler irrigation of treated wastewater–Field study [SOURCE: Page_126]

Introduction Transport of aerosols resulting from sprinkler irrigation of treated wastewater is a complex phenomenon due to the combined effect of several parameters including fine droplets (<0.15 mm) and wind speed, which are the most important vectors for pathogen transmission. [SOURCE: Page_126]

As explained in Chapter 1, numerous experimental and physical–mathematical modeling approaches based on ballistics and fluid mechanics have been proposed to predict the dispersion mechanism of droplets that have the most significant volume. However, the small droplets (<0.15 mm) were not considered thanks to their negligible volume (transported volume). [SOURCE: Page_126]

2680      Besides, most of the previously cited field studies focused on measurements of airborne transport through active samplers or were limited in terms of heights: 1 or 2 m above the ground using passive samplers (PVC Strings). Additionally, the Gaussian steady-state models, which are widely used in atmospheric dispersion (diffusion + transport) of aerosols generated by sprinkler irrigation, were not designed to model dispersion under low wind conditions or distances inferior to 100 m from the sprinklers. Therefore, in this study, nine experiments have been conducted outdoor to characterize the short-range transport horizontally (up to 4 times the sprinkler range) and distribution of the airborne droplet/droplet nuclei plume in the crosswind, and vertically, up to 10 m above the ground, from an impact sprinkler under different meteorological conditions. The approach was based on the evaluation of a combination of methods to quantify the airborne transport (Ta) and sedimented transport (Ts) on 2690   the soil surface. Note that in this study, the expression of “sedimented transport” refers to the droplets escaping from the wetted radius under windy conditions and deposited on Petri dishes. [SOURCE: Page_126]

A second objective was to propose empirical models validated against experimental data to estimate airborne spray transport and surface sedimentation. [SOURCE: Page_126]

125 [SOURCE: Page_126]

Material and Methods Characteristics of sprinkler irrigation A dual-nozzle impact sprinkler (type 5022SD–U, 2.5×1.8 mm “purple”, NaanDanJain Irrigation Ltd) was used for the present study (Figure 5.1). This sprinkler is commonly used for small or irregularly shaped plots for different irrigation purposes such as seed germination, as well as for the watering of vegetable, flowers, horticultural and nursery crops. [SOURCE: Page_127]

2700      An operating pressure of 350 kPa was set, resulting in a flow rate and radius of throw of 0.752 m^3.h- 1 and 10.3 m respectively. This pressure was selected to maximize the production of droplets subjected to transport and evaporation due to their relatively small size distribution. The height of nozzles above the ground level/soil level collectors was 1m. This work was conducted in accordance with the standard ISO 15886–3 (2012) in windless conditions, and the trajectory height and duration of rotation through each quadrant were measured at approximately 3.08 m above the ground and 8.61s, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_127]

Figure 5.1. Photo of 5022SD–U. NaanDanJain impact sprinkler irrigation, used in the present study [SOURCE: Page_127]

5.2.1.1. Water and droplet size distribution under indoor environment conditions Indoor experiments were carried out to characterize the water application rate, in accordance with 2710   ISO 15886-3 (ISO, 2012), as well as droplet size distribution, which are the key parameters governing the evaporation rate, wind losses and transport (Li et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_127]

As shown in Figure 5.2, the impact sprinkler was mounted perpendicularly on a pipe riser with a nozzle height of 1 m above the measurement instruments and positioned inside a round shelter with enough cutout dimensions for unrestricted jet operations in the direction of the collectors so as to not to affect their measurement accuracy. A pressure gauge was installed upstream of the sprinkler, and at the height of the main nozzle to maintain operating pressure at 350 kPa. Discharge through the sprinkler was measured by means of an electromagnetic flowmeter (Endress+Hauser Progmag 33FT40, Sweden) offering an accuracy rate of ± 0.2%. [SOURCE: Page_127]

Water application rate: A row of collectors (0.4 × 0.25 m = 0.1 m^2 surface area and at a 0.43 m 2720   height) were placed along the radius of throw to create a continuous sampling line. The center of the [SOURCE: Page_127]

126 [SOURCE: Page_127]

first collector was placed at a 0.65 m distance from the sprinkler head [Figure 5.2 (left)]. This distance is necessary to avoid the influence of the shelter. In accordance with the ISO 15886–3 (2012) measurement standard, the collected water values considered, are those superior to 0.26 mm.h -1. The experiment duration was 180 min. The distribution was measured along a single collector radius, and repeated three times to obtain an average radial distribution curve. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Droplet size distribution: A dual-beam Spectropluviometer, DBS (Delahaye et al., 2006) was used to measure the equivalent diameter of droplets ranging from 0.1 mm (Akrour et al. 2014) to more than 5mm, at six points within the sprinkler range: 30, 40, 60, 70, 80, and 90% of the maximum radius (Figure 5.2). These distances were chosen based on results from previous experiments conducted in our 2730   laboratory. In general, the 3 first points are best used to characterize the distributed volume, while the 3 others are essential for obtaining a better understanding of phenomena related to the water application rate. At least 5,000 droplets were measured at each position. It should be mentioned that the calibration of the droplet sizer was carried out using steel balls with known diameters in the range of 0.3 to 6 mm. From the droplet size distribution at each position, a set of parameters are calculated: number median diameter (DN50), Volume Median Diameter (Dv^50), the Sauter mean diameter (D32), and the percentage 𝐷3 of volume in droplets above the predefined thresholds. The Sauter diameter (𝐷32 = ∑ 𝐷𝑖2) is commonly 𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_128]

used as an indicator of important processes involving the ratio of inertia to aerodynamic drag forces (Bayvel and Orzechowski, 1993). Additionally, the total droplet size distribution in volume was built by weighting the application rate with the water volume reconstituted from the sampled droplet volumes. 2740   The cumulative volume of the total droplet distribution was also fitted by the Rosin-Rammler function (see Eq. 4-3). [SOURCE: Page_128]

Figure 5.2. Experimental setup used for evaluation of: (left) application rate, (right) droplet size distribution [SOURCE: Page_128]

127 [SOURCE: Page_128]

Experimental site In-situ transport measurements were performed on an INRAE experimental field (Lavalette site, INRAE Montpellier, 43°38’49.5” N; 3°52’21.64” E; Z= 50 m above mean sea level), and located in the South-East of France. Water was pressurized from a pump (Jet DAB 102M, China) attached to a 1m 3 water tank covered with a thermal insulating sheet. An electric sprinkler pump (FPSP800, Castorama 2750   France) was used to keep the fluorescent tracer inside the tank in suspension, and ensure the uniformity of spray concentration throughout each test. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Spray solution The spray solution was a mixture of water and ABF (Acid Brilliant Flavine, WALDECK, Münster), at a 0.4 g.L-1 concentration level. The specification and the size of colloidal particles of the ABF in the solvent (demineralized water) have already been presented (see section 3.5.1.1). [SOURCE: Page_129]

Atmospheric Conditions Measurements Before experimentations [SOURCE: Page_129]

To evaluate temporal and spatial heterogeneities in prevailing wind speed and direction due to 2760   obstacles in the vicinity of the site (hedges and buildings), before carrying out the experiments, for two consecutive weeks, wind speed and their two horizontal components (U–V) were measured simultaneously at four different points within the test site, and at the height of 2 m above the ground. Furthermore, to verify all the sampler position possibilities, the measurement points were varied (at the end of the first week), while keeping a point as reference. The measurements were performed using four three-cup anemometers (A100R and A100L$_2$ models, Campbell Scientific, accuracy: 1% ±0.1 ms-1) and four wind vanes (W200P, Campbell Scientific Ltd, accuracy: ±2°) connected to two data loggers (CR1000X and CR3000, Campbell Scientific Ltd, Great Britain, measurement precision: 0.01units) using a 5-minute interval sampling rate. In addition, the prevailing wind direction, based on 10 years of hourly wind data of the weather station located in the agricultural research site (Lavalette, station N° 2770   34172005), was taken into consideration to control to validate the short term meteorological parameter measurements (Appendix. G, Table G. 2). The results of our analyses were used to determine the location of the experimental set-up so that obstacle impact on wind direction would be minimized. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Experimental work [SOURCE: Page_129]

Nine experiments were carried out from July to October 2020 under daylight conditions characterized by climatic variability (Table 5.1). Meteorological measurements were recorded using a portable meteorological station installed in the immediate vicinity of the experimental set-up, at a lateral location 25 m distance from the sprinkler head. These measurements were comprised of average wind speed (A100R and A100L$_2$ models, Campbell Scientific) and direction (W200P, Campbell Scientific Ltd), air 2780   temperature and relative humidity (CS215, Campbell Scientific Ltd, accuracy: ±0.3 °C at 25 °C), and [SOURCE: Page_129]

128 [SOURCE: Page_129]

were conducted at 2 and 10 m heights above the ground and solar radiation (Pyranometer SP1110, Campbell Scientific, accuracy: typically < ±3%) at 1 m height. All the meteorological parameters were sampled at a rate of 0.2 Hz with data being averaged every 30 seconds during a 30 min experimentation period coinciding with the initiation of sprinkler irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Then, the vapor pressure deficit (VPD) in the air was calculated using the Tetens equation (Tetens, 1930) and is computed by estimating the difference between the saturation vapor pressure (es) and the actual vapor pressure (ea) [Allen et al. 1998]. [SOURCE: Page_130]

In addition, the standard deviation of wind direction was computed using the equation of Yamartino (1984) to estimate the crosswind/horizontal transport and aerosol dispersions (detail of the calculations 2790   are available in the Appendix G. 1. 3). [SOURCE: Page_130]

Based on the measured meteorological parameters (Figure G. 1), it was found that wind speed appeared to be 31 % higher at 10 m compared to 2 m while wind direction deviates by 7.2 degrees on average between the same heights (for eight experiments). Wind direction fluctuations are on average 8% higher at 2 m than 10 m (for six over the eight measurements). As these values at 10 m are steadier, and more representative of the average movement of the air mass, we chose its related standard deviation of wind direction (σƟ) for our models. The same procedure was applied to the other meteorological parameters using the 10 m height (EPA, 2000) reference. At this height, the average temperature recorded was between 18.9 and 32.5 °C. The average relative humidity and solar radiation ranged from 27.2 to 96.5% and 60.5 to 740.2 w.m^2, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_130]

2800   Atmospheric stability characterization [SOURCE: Page_130]

Atmospheric stability is an important meteorological parameter when assessing fine droplet dispersion due to its role in suppressing or enhancing the ambient turbulence. We have evaluated different criteria to quantify this atmospheric state. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Thus, at the first step, the atmospheric stability class of the nine experiments at the 10 m reference height was determined based on the Solar radiation/delta T (SRDT) method for estimating Pasquill- Gifford stability categories (EPA, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_130]

As shown in Table 5.1, atmospheric stability classes ranged from A, the most unstable condition, to D describing neutral conditions. The conditions are considered similar as 6 of the 9 experiments have 2810   been conducted in stability C conditions, while one was attributed to class A, i.e., low wind conditions (1.0 m.s-1), another in class B with 1 m.s-1 difference between 2 and 10 m measurements, and the last is found in class D, mainly as a result of early morning measurements (with low solar radiation). [SOURCE: Page_130]

In order to use atmospheric stability in the empirical modeling, the Bulk Richardson number, as a numerical representation of each class, has been computed. It is a nondimensional stability ratio that is widely used to approximate the ratio of buoyancy to vertical wind shear, and is defined as, [SOURCE: Page_130]

129 [SOURCE: Page_130]

𝜕𝜃𝑝 ⁄ 𝐵 = (−𝑔       𝜕𝑧 𝑧 2 ) (Eq. 5-1) 𝜃𝑝 . 𝑈 2 𝜕𝜃𝑝 where,      ⁄ is the gradient potential temperature (at two heights), U is the wind speed at the 𝜕𝑧 computation height z, which is taken as the geometric mean height between 𝑧1 = 2 m and 𝑧1 = 10, g is the gravitational acceleration [Zoumakis and Kelessis 1991]. To determine the air stability as a function of Bulk Richardson values, Golder (1975) developed a Nomogram (B-z) and can be found in article of 2820   Zoumakis and Kelessis (1991) (Figure 5.3). This nomogram has been extended within the range -0.04 <B< 0.08, representing the most unstable and stable conditions, according to EPA (2000) [see Figure 5.3]. It should be mentioned that the value obtained in experiment N°4 (2.47, neutral conditions according to SRDT) is located outside the limit of the lowest stable of Nomogram [B-stability classes- z]). [SOURCE: Page_131]

Figure 5.3. Classification scheme for the bulk Richardson number, B, as a function of the height z, in terms of the Pasquill A-F stability classes (Zoumakis and Kelessis, 1991). [SOURCE: Page_131]
