Sampler layout The measurement points were placed within a perimeter extending to four times the sprinkler range (Figure 5.4). Aerosol plume assessment was carried out using a set of 2 mm diameter PVC strings (PLASTTUB SAS, France). As mentioned earlier (see section 2.2.1), these strings continue to be the standard methodology (Al Heidary et al., 2014; Nuyttens et al., 2010; Torrent et al., 2017), even though the experimental process is time-consuming and labor-intensive (Torrent et al., 2019). In addition, this method can be used to evaluate very large sampling areas for liquid aerosols that evaporate at a very slow rate. [SOURCE: Page_133]

2840      To characterize aerosol plume distribution in the vertical axis, 7 m long PVC strings were stretched horizontally between a pair of 12 m pneumatic telescopic masts (SQT12-8/HP, CLARK MASTS) at a 20 m distance from the sprinkler. A pair of 6 m telescopic masts were positioned at a 30 m distance, and a pair of 10 m pneumatic telescopic masts (QTX15-9/HP 37212, CLARK MASTS) at a 40 m distance from the sprinkler. Therefore, depending on the mast system used, air samples were taken from 1 to 10 m above the ground, in 1m increments, at the distances of 20 and 40 m from the sprinkler head, and up to 6 m above the ground at a distance of 30 m from the sprinkler. [SOURCE: Page_133]

The PVC strings were positioned as perpendicularly as possible to the estimated mean wind direction at the beginning of the experiments. Due to the fluctuations in wind direction during the experiments, complementary PVC strings were also placed on both sides of the mean wind direction at an angle of 2850   20 degrees, along the previous locations. The latter were positioned at 1 and 2 m heights above ground. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Note that the previous studies, in particular, in wind tunnel experiments (Cornacchia et al., 2019) confirmed that using consecutive PVC strings (with 2mm diameter) at sufficient intervals have no obstacle effect on wind direction fluctuation. [SOURCE: Page_133]

In the same longitudinal and angular directions, the volume of the sediment/ground deposition (sedimented transport, Ts) was quantified using Petri dishes (6361.72 mm^2) installed horizontally 0.25 m above ground at 2 m intervals from 14 (1.5 times) to 40 m (4 times) distances from the sprinkler head. Our goal was to evaluate sedimented transport beyond the wetted perimeter over the field including locations 20 to 40 m downwind of the sprinkler head [distances recommended by ANSES (2012)]. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Additionally, a 120-degree arc of PVC strings (for tests 1 and 5–9), placed 20 m downwind from 2860   the source, and at the heights of 1 and 2 m from the ground allowed us to quantify the aerosol plume distribution in the crosswind. To estimate the sedimented transport profile in the crosswind direction, seven Petri dishes were positioned on the arc at 20 degree intervals. [SOURCE: Page_133]

132 [SOURCE: Page_133]

Figure 5.4. Scheme of sampler and meteorological station downwind from the sprinkler [SOURCE: Page_134]

Spray volume estimation After each experiment, samplers were retrieved immediately after drying. Petri dish lids were closed and the PVC strings were placed in bottles of 50 mL. The collectors were stored in a dark bag until collection was completed (about 2 hours), and then directly placed in the laboratory refrigerator. [SOURCE: Page_134]

2870      Once in the laboratory, quantification of the ABF concentration (µg.L-1) was performed for all the samplers using a fluorescence Spectrometer (Perkin Elmer LS 45, Waltham, USA) with the optimal range of accuracy between 5 and 450 μg.L-1 of the ABF concentration. Airborne transport (Ta) based on PVC string samples and sedimented transport (Ts) using Petri dishes, expressed in mL.m-2.h-1, were calculated as follows: [SOURCE: Page_134]

𝐶𝑠 . 𝑉𝑑𝑖𝑙 𝑇𝑎 𝑜𝑟 𝑇𝑠 =                                                                                   (Eq. 5-2) 𝐶0 . 𝐴. 𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_134]

where, 𝐶𝑠 and 𝐶0 are the fluorescent concentration of each sample and the tank mixture, respectively, in μg ABF L−1; 𝑉𝑑𝑖𝑙 is the sample dilution volume (20 mL demineralized water) in mL, t is sampling duration in hours. In addition, A is the effective sample area in m 2 (for PVC strings and Petri dishes), considering 80% of the collection efficiency for PVC strings. [SOURCE: Page_134]

This value was applied according to the results of theoretical and experimental studies carried out in 2880   wind tunnels investigating the effect of various parameters such as Stokes number, Reynolds of droplets, droplet diameter and the relative velocity between the droplets and the air on the collection efficiency [SOURCE: Page_134]

133 [SOURCE: Page_134]

of 2 mm diameter cylindrical passive samplers (Aylor 1982; Gil et al. 2005, 2007; Parkin and Young 2000; Walklate 1992). [SOURCE: Page_135]

It should be mentioned that the concentrations below the detection limit of the fluorescence Spectrometer (< 5μg.L-1 or <0.0019-0.0025 mL.m-2.h-1) have been considered as zero. [SOURCE: Page_135]

Statistical analysis At the first step, bivariate correlations between the different variables (meteorological data, deposits and airborne sampling from PVC lines, distances from the emission source) were analyzed using the Pearson correlation coefficient methods with the R statistical package, at a significance level (p-values) 2890   of 0.05. Where appropriate, the Pearson method gives the significance level of inter-correlation between input variables used to calibrate the empirical models. [SOURCE: Page_135]

The results of bivariate correlations between meteorological variables measured from eight experiments highlighted that the meteorological parameters can be considered independent (see supplementary Figure G. 2 and Table G. 3). [SOURCE: Page_135]

Two empirical models (power and exponential) from a set of 80 combinations were retained to adjust airborne and sedimented transport measurements based on the following criteria: [SOURCE: Page_135]

•   Level of correlation between the observed and simulated distributions of aerosol plume in the horizontal axis and in the vertical direction at different distances; [SOURCE: Page_135]

•   Previous empirical models proposed by Molle et al. (2016) and Tomas et al. (2019), and the 2900              Gaussian dispersion models; [SOURCE: Page_135]

•   The previous models developed for estimating downwind pesticide spray drift as a function of distance (Dist): German drift Model (%𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑡 = 𝑎𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡 −𝑏 ) and Dutch drift Model (%𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑡 = 𝑎0 𝑒 −(𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡×𝑎1 ) + 𝑏0 𝑒 −(𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡×𝑏1 ) ). [SOURCE: Page_135]

For each combination, the observed and simulated data are fitted through a recursive loop until the residues between the observed and the calculated value are lower than 1.5 times the standard deviation of the observed values. At each iteration, Cook’s distance is calculated for each observation, and if it is found to be greater than three times the mean Cook’s distance the observation is considered as an outlier and omitted. Such a procedure is necessary as some of the measurements may be accidentally “polluted” by dye. The estimated coefficients were validated through the evaluation of t-statistic (ratio of Estimate 2910   to Standard error of the coefficient) and the level of significance (p-value that shall be <0.05). [SOURCE: Page_135]

To select the most appropriate models we have progressively added each parameter (1. spatial parameters, 2. Standard deviation of wind direction, 3. Wind speed or Bulk Richardson number, 4. Temperature, 5. Relative humidity, 6. Solar radiation, and 7. Vapor pressure deficit) until the inclusion of a new parameter, or combination of the parameters, did not improve the coefficient of determination [SOURCE: Page_135]

134 [SOURCE: Page_135]

(R$_2$) while retaining a maximum number of observations. In addition, the Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) was calculated. It has the advantage of expressing the prediction error in the units as the variable, and therefore it provides more information about model accuracy (Legates and McCabe, 1999). [SOURCE: Page_136]

Thus, for each of the two principal models (power and exponential) described in section 5.3.5, twenty-one models, combining the parameters were tested, of which ten of the best at predicting airborne 2920   transport, and nine models for sedimented transport, are highlighted in green and are available in the supplementary Table G. 5 and Table G. 7. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Additionally, in order the evaluate the genericity of the models, we have applied them on another dataset acquired in different environmental conditions [field data of Tomas et al. (2019), and at a nozzle pressure of 350 kPa]. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Results and discussions Water distribution radial curve and Distribution of Droplet Sizes in Laboratory Conditions Figure 5.5a illustrates the water distribution curve (converted to application rate, in mm.h-1) as a function of distance from the sprinkler head along the radius of throw. The curve confirms that the 2930   capacity of this dual-nozzle sprinkler can provide distribution uniformity with adapted spacing. As explained in section 5.2.1.1, this curve was used for calculating the total volume of applied water presented in Table 5.2. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Figure 5.5b represents the range of droplet sizes formed at different distances from the sprinkler. This demonstrates that droplet volumes which were supposed to aerosolize, decrease in line with the increase of the radius of throw (Yan et al., 2010). Montero et al. (2003) stated that the droplet diameter formed in the jet break-up process is inversely proportional to air/water relative velocity. This process produces small droplets that escape from the water jet during its breakup. Conversely, large droplets are produced from the core of the jet where the relative water air velocity is lowest. As shown in Figure 5.5b, the Rosin-Ramler model fits very well the distribution of the cumulated droplet volume. 2940   Nevertheless, the model tends to overestimate the proportion of droplets smaller than 0.5 mm ( Table 5.2), which is in agreement with previous studies(Kincaid et al., 1996) and with the capacities of the droplet sensor that tends to underestimate small (<0.4 mm) droplets. Therefore, using the Rosin-Ramler approach is appropriate for the present study as it results in an overestimation of the droplets that present the highest pollutant and pathogen dissemination risks. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Moreover, as discussed by Molle et al. (2016), the estimation of droplet size distribution is measured in calm/laboratory conditions while in field experiments the wind amplifies spray atomization (Richards and Wetherhead 1983). [SOURCE: Page_136]

135 [SOURCE: Page_136]

Figure 5.5.(a): Water application curve, (b): Cumulative distribution in volume [%] [SOURCE: Page_137]

Horizontal profile of airborne transport and deposit Figure 5.6a to 5.6c show the crosswind distribution of aerosol plume at the heights of 1 and 2 m above the ground and deposited on the surface according to the line direction in regard to average wind direction, for airborne transport (PVC strings) and sedimented transport (Petri dishes). These distributions correspond to a 120-degree arc located at a distance of 20 m from the sprinkler head. Exp.5 and Exp.6 were chosen because of their contrasting standard deviation of wind direction (σθ, of 32.5 and 18.70 degrees, respectively), under slightly different wind speeds (3.1 and 3.6 m.s-1, respectively). Note that the other meteorological parameters were almost constant. Figure 5.6 clearly illustrates the effect of the standard deviation of wind direction on horizontal dispersion of the aerosol plume. The best-fit 2960     curves derived from observations (using CurveExpert 1.4 Basic software) tend to be Gaussian (𝑓(∆𝜃) = (∆𝜃−𝑏)2 𝑎 × 𝑒𝑥𝑝[−0.5 ×        2𝑐 2 ], where a, b and c are the coefficients), which agrees with Pasquill (1961) who [SOURCE: Page_137]

observed that the horizontal dispersion could be estimated from measurements of horizontal wind- direction fluctuations (see Appendix. G Table G. 4 and Figure G. 3 for details). [SOURCE: Page_137]

(a)                                                 (b) [SOURCE: Page_138]

(c) Figure 5.6. Crosswind distribution of aerosol plume at a distance of 20 m at two heights of (a):1 m, (b): 2 m above the ground, and (c): deposited on the ground/surface, for the Exp.5 and Exp.6 [SOURCE: Page_138]

2970          Effect of wind speed on aerosol transport The effect of wind speed on transport was investigated based on four of the experiments carried out in wind speeds ranging between 0.16 and 3.6 m.s-1. The values ranged respectively from zero (<0.0025 mL.m-2.h-1) to 20.8 mL.m-2.h-1, for airborne transport and zero to 2.09 mL.m-2.h-1 for sedimented transport. The higher the wind speed, the higher the transport. The samples taken at a 1m height contain the highest aerosol transport concentrations, while the maximum deposit is recorded at 18m from the sprinkler. [SOURCE: Page_138]

Figure 5.7 illustrates airborne transport deposits measured at the height of 2 m above the ground as a function of wind speed, at locations 20 to 40 m downwind from the sprinkler head following the wind axis. For a given distance, airborne transport concentration rose with increased wind speed, particularly 2980   under the wind speed of 3.6 m.s-1. There is a sharp increase in transport values for 3.6 m.s-1 probably due to increased airflow turbulence. Whatever the wind speed, transport values decreases with distance. Airborne transport concentrations obtained at the distance of 20 m (twice the sprinkler range) were [SOURCE: Page_138]

137 [SOURCE: Page_138]

measurable even for wind speed of 1.1 m.s-1 (2.35 mL.m-2.h-1). At 30 and 40 m from the sprinkler head, this quantity falls below 0.2 and 0.1 mL.m-2.h-1, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_139]

Figure 5.7. Downwind airborne transport as a function of wind speed, at the height of 2 m above the ground [SOURCE: Page_139]

In order to simultaneously compare the effect of wind speed on aerosol plume distribution in a vertical and downwind direction, the airborne and sedimented transported volumes were normalized by 2990   dividing them by their respective highest values (20.8 for Figure 5.8a to 5.8c and 2.9 mL.m-2.h-1 for Figure 5.8 d). Note that the concentrations obtained from the Petri dishes positioned at 14 and 16 m from the sprinkler head were very high. Thus, these points were not considered in downwind sedimented transport (Figure 5.8d). At a wind speed of 0.16 m.s-1, no airborne or ground deposition was observed at any examined distance. At a distance of 20 m, for the three windy conditions (1.1, 2.4 and 3.6 m.s-1), the quantities of measured airborne transport were higher than 1 mL.m-2.h-1 up to the height of 6 m above the ground (Figure 5.8a). At a distance of 40 m from the sprinkler head, and at a height of 6 m above the ground, for the same windy conditions the airborne transport falls below 0.05, 0.1 and 0.71 mL.m - 2 .h-1, respectively. Figure 5.8d shows that the sedimented transport values decrease downwind according to distance for the three wind speeds, which illustrates the flattening and widening of the aerosol/droplet 3000   plume distribution as the downwind distance increases (Turner, 1994) [see Figure G. 4]. [SOURCE: Page_139]

As shown in Figure 5.8a, for unstable conditions (B<0, Table 5.1), the vertical dispersion of aerosols produced by the sprinkler, installed at a height of 1m above the ground, decreases with wind speed (see Figure G. 5). This agrees with the observations of Gil et al. (2007), who concluded that in low wind speed conditions, vertical drift of very fine and fine droplets is greater than the horizontal one. [SOURCE: Page_139]

138 [SOURCE: Page_139]

(a)                                                 (b) [SOURCE: Page_140]

(c)                                                   (d) Figure 5.8. Vertical profiles of normalized airborne transport as a function of wind speeds at distances of (a): 20 m, (b): 30 m, and (c): 40 m from the sprinkler. (d): Normalized sedimented transport as a function of wind speed at distances of 18 to 40 m from the sprinkler head [SOURCE: Page_140]

3010 [SOURCE: Page_140]

Effect of temperature on aerosol dispersion Figure 5.9 compares the results of two different temperatures (T= 32.5 and 22.9°C) on the vertical profile of airborne transport and sedimented transport downwind from the sprinkler head. These selected temperatures belong to Exp.2 and Exp.9 as other meteorological parameters than temperature remained almost constant. The values were normalized to their maximum: 10.1 mL.m -2.h-1 for PVC strings and 2.67 mL.m-2.h-1 for Petri dishes. [SOURCE: Page_140]

A 9.6°C decrease in temperature had a significant impact on airborne transport values. At the height of 1 m, from 32.5 to 22.9°C, airborne transport concentrations at the distances of 20, 30 and 40 m fell by 50, 60 and 30%, respectively. The noteworthy difference between the values obtained at 30 and 40 3020   m is because, at 40 m from the sprinkler head, the majority aerosols under the temperature of 32.5°C were dispersed vertically within the height range of 1 to 5 m. Besides, it can be observed that, at the temperature of 32.5°C, vertical distribution tends to be Gaussian shaped as a function distance (Figure 5.9a). [SOURCE: Page_140]

139 [SOURCE: Page_140]

Figure 5.9b shows that at 20 m, the sedimented transport is almost constant for both temperatures. This induces that the measurement method is not significantly affected by possible/potential evaporation, which is the cause of a significant proportion of dry aerosol production that cannot be collected by this measurement method. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Figure 5.9.(a): Vertical profile of normalized airborne transport and (b): normalized sedimented 3030     transport as function of temperature at the different distances from the sprinkler head. (Black dots: Exp.2: 32.5 °C; red dots: Exp.9: 22.9 °C) [SOURCE: Page_141]

According to the results of vertical distribution of aerosol plume, the Gaussian models that widely have been used for long-range (>100 m) dispersion of aerosols from wastewater treatment plant (Fileni et al., 2018; Pasalari et al., 2019) and prediction of pesticide spray drift (Hong et al., 2021) could not be reliable for estimating short-range airborne transport. [SOURCE: Page_141]

Empirical modeling Based on the trends obtained from the aerosol plume distribution in the vertical and horizontal directions, and the sedimented transport at different distances from the sprinkler, presented in the previous sections, the two following principal empirical models were selected as the most effective 3040   (among 40 others) in reproducing the experimental results. Note that these models combine the most significant parameters presented below. [SOURCE: Page_141]

-   M$_1$: Power–crosswind Gaussian [SOURCE: Page_141]

𝑇𝑎/𝑠 = 𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡 𝑑𝑖𝑠𝑡 × 𝐻 ℎ × 𝑈 𝑢 × 𝑇𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝 × 𝑅𝐻 𝑟ℎ × 𝑉𝑃𝐷𝑣𝑝𝑑 × 𝑅𝑠 𝑟𝑠 ∆𝜃 2                                                    (Eq. 5-3) {−0.5×( ) } 𝑏            𝜎𝜃 × 𝑎𝑏𝑠(𝐵) × 𝑒 [SOURCE: Page_141]

-   M$_2$ : Exponential–crosswind Gaussian [SOURCE: Page_141]

𝑟𝑠               ∆𝜃 2 [(𝑑𝑖𝑠𝑡×𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡)+(ℎ×𝐻)+(𝑢×𝑈)+(𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝×𝑇𝑒𝑚𝑝)+(𝑟ℎ×𝑅𝐻)+(𝑣𝑝𝑑×𝑉𝑃𝐷)+(              )+(𝑏×𝐵)−{0.5×( ) }] 𝑅𝑆               𝜎𝜃 𝑇𝑎/𝑠 = 𝑒 (Eq. 5-4) [SOURCE: Page_141]

140 [SOURCE: Page_141]

where, the coefficients/exponents: 𝑑𝑖𝑠𝑡 (distance), ℎ (height, for airborne transport model), 𝑢 (wind speed), 𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝 (temperature), 𝑟𝑠 (solar radiation), 𝑟ℎ (relative humidity) and 𝑏 (bulk Richardson number) are associated to each input variable, and were estimated using the non-linear regression method supplied by Matlab® R2020a. [SOURCE: Page_142]

The models that meet all the validation criteria can be found in the supplementary information 3050   section, in Table G. 5-6 (highlighted in green). Model validity was based on the significance level of each coefficient (<0.001 to 0.01), the high number of total valid observation, the coefficient of determination (R$_2$> 0.8) and the smallest RMSE as possible (see Appendix G. 3. 1). [SOURCE: Page_142]

In these analyses, the effects of spatial (distance, height, angle) and meteorological parameters (wind speed and standard deviation of wind direction) were always considered. The other parameters were only considered if they improved the level of accuracy of the final model. [SOURCE: Page_142]

5.3.5.1. Airborne transport assessment Models 1 and 2 (M$_1$ and M$_2$) are calibrated by the combination of spatial parameters (distance, height and angle of the sample from average wind direction), wind speed, and standard deviation of wind direction (which explained most of the variability of observations from PVC strings) while keeping a 3060   large number of observations (See Table G. 5: N° of obs.: 265/288=92%, 284/288=98.6 %, R$_2$=0.783 and 0.88 for model M$_1$.1 and M$_2$.1, respectively). Temperature and relative humidity explain residual variability (see Table G. 5, M1or^2.3, M1or 2.5 and M1or 2.11). The effect of the air vapor pressure deficit on the transport is low because the VPD is taken into consideration based on its components (Relative humidity and temperature) and wind speed (M$_1$ or 2.7 in Table G. 5). [SOURCE: Page_142]

Therefore, based on R² and RMSE, the best airborne transport models considering the independent variables of wind speed (𝑈), standard deviation of wind direction (σɵ), temperature (𝑇𝑒𝑚𝑝), relative humidity (𝑅𝐻) and spatial parameters are: [SOURCE: Page_142]

3100      As shown in Figure 5.12, the overall correlations between observed data from Petri dishes and simulated results of sedimented transport through the M$_1$ and M$_2$ are 0.92 and 0.93, respectively, which indicate that the data were scattered around the angle bisector (y = x) with the coefficient of determination (R$_2$) over 0.9. [SOURCE: Page_144]

Figure 5.12. Correlation between observed and simulated data from Petri dishes (sedimented transport), y=ax, (a): Power–crosswind Gaussian model, M$_1$, (b): Exponential–crosswind Gaussian model, M$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_144]

Figure 5.13 shows two examples of sedimented transport values as a function of distance (Exp 3 and 5). The other figures are available in G. 3. 2, Figure G. 9 and G. 10. These experiments have been 3110   selected because of their almost similar meteorological parameters except relative humidity (Exp. 3 with U = 3.0 m.s-1, Temp = 26.92 °C, RH = 27.22%, RS = 740.23 w.m-2 and σθ = 38.89 degrees, and Exp. 5 with U = 3.1 m.s-1, Temp = 29.43 °C, RH = 41.64% and RS = 562.45 w.m-2 and σθ = 32.55 degrees). Contrary to airborne transport, except for the first two distances, i.e., 16 and 18 m, there is no significant difference between Exp.3 and 5. Therefore, this result confirms that relative humidity had little or no- impact on the sedimented transport in our experimental conditions. In fact, sedimented transport values were obtained from the collection of the larger droplets that were transported by the wind out of the wetted area and that fell on the ground. Since the settling time of the larger droplets (>100 μm) is very short (~0.5 s) and the time of evaporation rises approximately by 𝐷𝑖2 (Ranz and Marshall, 1952b, 1952a), relative humidity effects on sedimented transport appears to be statistically lower than for airborne 3120   transport (resulted from small droplets). Thus, in the experimental conditions we faced, wind was the dominant parameter governing sedimented transport. However, to explain the role of relative humidity, more experiments would need to be performed; preferably in controlled conditions that would allow the [SOURCE: Page_144]

143 [SOURCE: Page_144]

exploration of a larger range of meteorological values, which might be more difficult to encounter in situ. [SOURCE: Page_145]

Figure 5.13. Examples of sedimented transport simulation (line/dashed line) downwind from the sprinkler head and experimental data (dots) using Petri dishes from Exp.3 (black) and Exp.5 (blue), see Table 5.1. [SOURCE: Page_145]

5.3.5.3. Study of model genericity 3130      The genericity of both our models was evaluated using other dataset including obtained field data and the empirical models developed by Tomas et al. (2019). The observation data was from three experiments, which were performed with another impact sprinkler model under an operating pressure of 350 kPa and contrasting wind speeds (1.19, 2.88, and 3.85 m.s-1). As shown in Figure 5.14, the agreement between the model 1 and the field data was excellent. As commented earlier, model 2 does not perform very well for distances higher than 30 m. Therefore, it can be concluded that model 1 could be successfully used to simulate airborne transport from different type of impact sprinklers. [SOURCE: Page_145]

(a)                                              (b) [SOURCE: Page_145]

144 [SOURCE: Page_145]

(c) 3140      Figure 5.14. Genericity of the both models with experimental data from Rain Bird 5000+ under operating pressure of 350 kPa and at height of 1m above the ground: (a) on 25 September 2013 (Wind speed:1.19 m.s-1, Temperature: 23.8 °C, Relative Humidity: 73 %); (b) on 09 October 2013 (Wind speed: 2.88 m.s-1, Temperature: 20.1 °C, Relative Humidity: 65 %); and (c) on 30 October 2013 (Wind speed:3.85 m.s-1, Temperature: 17 °C, Relative Humidity: 52 %) [SOURCE: Page_146]

Interpretation of results to estimate the potential presence of Escherichia coli bacteria In this study, we were able to determine the size of ABF colloidal particles in the solvent, which were around 1 µm in diameter and similar to the size of bacteria such as E.coli (or other pathogens with the same size) for aerodynamic behavior. Obviously, if we want to study the risk of infection by 3150   inhalation, we must take into account the infectivity of the pathogen, which we cannot address here. As explained in the introduction, our experiments can be used to offer useful information on the fraction of TWW spray that becomes aerosolized and the aerodynamic transport, which is a first step in the quantification of the microbial risk linked to TWW reuse. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Thus, given the results of the experiments in the sub section 5.3.3, regardless of the viability and die- off, or the inactivation rate of the microorganisms in the aerosol form, the following conditions are taken as an example: wind speed 2.4 m.s-1, 1 m above the ground, and 40 m from the sprinkler head (four times the sprinkler range). In addition, the sprinkler is assumed to spray treated wastewater with a concentration of 250 CFU/100 mL of Escherichia coli bacteria under the average wind direction for a 60 min exposure. Note that, according to French decree (ANSES 2012), this treated wastewater can be 3160   used for the irrigation of food crops that are not destined for industrial food processing. Thus, the potential of presence of Escherichia coli bacteria could be estimated at 1 CFU.m-2.h-1. This value reaches about 7 CFU.m-2.h-1 at wind speed of 3.6 m.s-1. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Likewise, the models developed in this study can be used to give a primary estimation on the potential presence of contaminants in aerosols resulting from the sprinkler irrigation of treated waste water, under different meteorological and spatial conditions. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Figure 5.15 shows the simulation of downwind E.coli transport as a function of wind speed using model 1 (M$_1$), under constant meteorological conditions (at normal temperature (20°C) and Pressure (1 atm), NTP, RH: 40%, σθ: 10 degrees and under the average wind direction), at a height of 1.5 m above [SOURCE: Page_146]

145 [SOURCE: Page_146]

the ground for simulating aspiration from the human breathing zone (Figure 5.15a), and on the ground 3170   surface (Figure 5.15b). The results showed that from the wind speeds of 1.4, 2.1 and 2.7 m.s -1, it is possible to detect at least one E.coli bacterium (1 CFU.m-2. h-1) at the distances of 20, 30 and 40 m from the sprinkler head, respectively. Up to 0.25 m above the ground, where there is the risk of dermal/skin contact or the deposit of the E.coli on plants, these wind speed thresholds reach 2.6, 4, and 5.4 m.s-1, at the same distances, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Overall, the most suitable irrigation management strategy when using treated wastewater would be to favor low winds, which will minimize dispersion, i.e. a wind speed < 2 m.s -1 (up to a maximum temperature of 40 °C, in summer), when the buffer zone or distance between the sprinkler head and residential areas (for example) is 40 m. Moreover, when assessing pathogen dispersion risks linked to sprinkler irrigation, pathogen reaerosolization from the soil or the plants, and their resulting 3180   multiplication in the air should also be considered. Therefore, in addition to monitoring meteorological and spatial parameters, other effective management controls such as tertiary storage and/or advanced treatment wastewater (ultrafiltration, etc.) prior to sprinkler irrigation could help reduce microorganism concentrations (O’Hara and Rubin, 2005). [SOURCE: Page_147]

(a)                                                (b) Figure 5.15. Downwind E.coli transport as a function of wind speed, at (a): height of 1.5 m above the ground, (b): deposited on the ground/surface at the Normal Temperature and Pressure (NTP), and the relative humidity of 40% [SOURCE: Page_147]

Conclusion In this study, nine field experiments were conducted to quantify the airborne and sedimented 3190   transport of the smaller droplets released from a sprinkler used for irrigation under different meteorological conditions. An accurate characterization of the fraction of spray flow spread by a sprinkler (usually applied over various crops) was carried out thanks to our tried and tested experimental protocol. The quantification of the aerosolized component was analyzed over a horizontal plane (up to 40 m from the emission source) and vertically (up to 10 m above the ground) in a downwind direction, beyond the irrigated (i.e. wetted) area. The measurements conducted in lab conditions have shown that [SOURCE: Page_147]

146 [SOURCE: Page_147]

droplets smaller than 0.15 mm (subjected to aerosolization) represented 0.15% of the total irrigated volume. [SOURCE: Page_148]

The results have also shown that the crosswind distribution of aerosol plume is Gaussian and its dispersion rate is highly dependent on the standard deviation of the wind direction and wind speed. The 3200   distribution of the aerosol plume in the vertical axis with regards to the sprinkler head presents the highest airborne transport values at heights of 1 to 2 m above the ground except for one case, i.e., Exp. 2: U = 2.7 m.s-1, Temp = 32.5°C, RH = 46.1%, RS = 279.1 w.m-2 and σθ = 35.7 degrees. The ranking analysis of parameter impacts on the transport showed that wind speed, followed by temperature, play a major role in droplet transport. Regarding sedimented transport, it was observed that the temperature effect is much lower than for airborne transport. [SOURCE: Page_148]

From these analyses, two semi-empirical models (M$_1$: power and M$_2$: exponential) were found to be the fittest for the estimation of both airborne and sedimented transport when considering both spatial variables (distance, height, angle from the average wind direction) and meteorological (wind speed, standard deviation of wind direction, temperature and relative humidity only for airborne transport) 3210   parameters. Both models were found to predict transport well using a coefficient of determination (R$_2$) higher than 0.9. However, at long distances (>30 m from the sprinkler head), model 1 performance exceeds that of 2. [SOURCE: Page_148]

These empirical models have been developed for an impact sprinkler at a single pressure, which results in a specific droplet distribution. However, their application to another dataset acquired with a different configuration has shown their genericity as they gave satisfactory results. [SOURCE: Page_148]

To complete this study, supplementary measurements using different sprinkler types are in progress: experiments using a big gun are currently being conducted by the same team in order to evaluate the genericity of the models. [SOURCE: Page_148]

The selected tracer (ABF) can be considered simulate transport of droplet nuclei containing particles 3220   around 1µm in diameter, and similar to the size of bacteria such as E.coli. Thus, the proposed approach can be used to assess pathogen exposure risks linked to sprinkler irrigation using TWW. [SOURCE: Page_148]

147 [SOURCE: Page_148]

General conclusion [SOURCE: Page_150]

Transport of aerosols resulting from sprinkler irrigation of treated wastewater (TWW) is a complex phenomenon due to the combined effect of various parameters including fine droplets (<0.15 mm) and wind speed, which are the most important vectors for pathogen transmission. Our literature review has shown very few bioaerosols measurements from TWW reuse for spray irrigation. Various samplers exist but there is no consensus on the most efficient for this or that pathogen, with a standardized protocol. Atmospheric models have been used to simulate bioaerosol transport. Most of them are based on gaussian approaches. In the case of aerosols generated by irrigation sprinklers such models are not adapted to low 3230   wind conditions or distances inferior to 100 m from the wetted radius. [SOURCE: Page_150]

In our work, we have tried to address the three following questions: [SOURCE: Page_150]

- What are the factors and conditions affecting the dispersion of contaminants during sprinkler irrigation? [SOURCE: Page_150]

- What techniques and methods should be used for the detection and sampling of (bio) aerosols generated by sprinkler irrigation? [SOURCE: Page_150]

-How to model the transport and dispersion of aerosols from spray irrigation of the treated wastewater? [SOURCE: Page_150]

Different experiments were conducted to address these questions, done in both controlled and natural conditions. [SOURCE: Page_150]

3240      First a brief review of air sampling methods has been done (Chapter 2). Various samplers were used to quantify bioaerosols. Aerosols samplers are generally classified into two categories: passive (deposit on a surface without an air pump or external power) and active (involving a mechanical component such as vacuum pump) samplers. Passive and active samplers are not comparable. Other techniques exist such as optical sampling which quantify aerodynamic particle size, scanning mobility particle counter, and are capable of detecting also total count of viable particles or viable + non-viable in real time as well as LIDAR technique. Active samplers with pumps are the most applied in various environmental conditions. The [SOURCE: Page_150]

149 [SOURCE: Page_150]

overall sampling efficiency of an active sampler is product of the aspiration (𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 ), transmission (𝜂 𝑇 ), collection (𝜂𝑐 )/retention (𝜂𝑅𝐸𝑇 ), recovery efficiency (𝜂𝑅𝑒𝑐 ), and biological efficiency (𝜂𝐵𝑖𝑜 , for microorganisms). The review has shown that many authors agree on the total lack of standardization for 3250   bioaerosol sampling methods and sample treatment. The use of higher sampling flow rate results in higher power consumption, which would shorten portable samplers’ battery life. Higher flow rate also means more intense desiccation of the collect medium which may lead to a lower recovery of the sensitive organisms. The accurate quantification of airborne volume droplets containing pathogens is of critical importance to estimate the health risks due to sprinkler irrigation with treated wastewater. There are not a lot of studies comparing the most common samplers in the same conditions. [SOURCE: Page_151]

The experiments of the part II were carried out under controlled conditions in order to assess the most suitable method for aerosol monitoring in the case of sprinkler irrigation (Chapter 3 ), and to evaluate the factors affecting the spread of small droplets (<150 µm) from sprinklers (Chapter 4). 3260   Such a work is of interest to healthcare farmers in close proximity of sprinkler irrigation with treated wastewater [SOURCE: Page_151]

The objectives of the part II were: [SOURCE: Page_151]

1) To compare different active sampling methods of airborne particles in controlled conditions from sprinkler irrigation of TWW, [SOURCE: Page_151]

2) To quantify the physical collection efficiency in low wind velocity, using a fluorescent tracer, [SOURCE: Page_151]

3) To investigate the effect of sampling time on the collection efficiency and on the culturability of the Escherichia coli (E.coli) cells sampled by the impingers (Chapter 3), and [SOURCE: Page_151]

4) To characterize the effect of air velocity and nozzle height on the airborne droplet size distributions downwind from the source using Phase Doppler Particle Analysis (PDPA), in a 3270              small scale wind tunnel (Chapter 4). [SOURCE: Page_151]

In Chapter 3, the experiments were conducted in laboratory of Engineering of the Industrial Environment and Industrial and Natural Risks (LGEI in Ales), which has developed, a sampling chamber of 1.234 × 0.63 × 0.8 m^3 dimension presenting a laminar flow with a range of air velocity (𝑈) of 0–0.09 m.s-1. We have worked with a windspeed U = 0.09 m.s-1 (and dry airflow Q_DRY=400 L.min-1). A fluorescent ABF was used a tracer to assess the physical collection efficiency of active samplers at different sampling times (5-60 min). A comparison of the physical efficiency of impingers revealed that the efficiency of AGI-4 was almost 18.5% higher than the SKC Biosample in the range of 5–30 min. [SOURCE: Page_151]

To respond the third objective, the experiments were conducted with a non pathogenic E. coli strain to evaluate the effect of sampling time on culturability of airborne E.coli cells sampled by impingers. The 3280   results showed that increasing sampling time from 5 min to 15 min, reduced the culturability of E.coli in [SOURCE: Page_151]

150 [SOURCE: Page_151]

both AGI–4 and SKC by a factor of 37% and 14%, respectively. It can therefore be inferred that culturability of AGI–4 is equal to culturability of SKC multiplied by 0.93. Also, it was showed that the BioTrak® can count total particles in real-time, which can be useful as a rapid assessment technique, especially, for quantifying the potential presence of contaminants, but its accuracy in measuring the viable particles was lower than accuracy obtained with the impingers, particularly in low relative humidity conditions. In addition, it physically detects the presence of viable bacteria but does not identify its type. [SOURCE: Page_152]

In Chapter 4, the experiments have been conducted in an open circuit wind tunnel with a working section of 0.5 m by 0.35 m and 2 m long (laboratory of Risk Sciences, IMT Mines Ales). The wind tunnel 3290   was controlled by a housing of electronic variable speed drive and the wind speed can be adjusted up to 15 ms-1. The air temperature was maintained at 20±1°C and only the data obtained under a relative humidity of about 40–50% have been retained for the analysis. In this study, the effect of air velocity and nozzle height on airborne droplet size distribution was investigated using PDPA. The results showed that the nozzle height is an important factor influencing airborne dispersion. Regardless of the distance from the nozzle tip, by decreasing a nozzle height from 0.275 to 0.1 m above the working section floor, 90% of the overall volume of droplets being less than or equal to 138 µm (𝐷𝑉90 = 138 µm ) for 0.275 m reached 58 µm (𝐷𝑉90 = 58 µm) for 0.1 m. The results also showed that air velocity plays an important role in the direct transport of small droplets, at short-range distance. At a distance of 1.65 m and a nozzle height of 0.27m above the floor, from air velocity of 1.2 to 5.0 m.s-1, 𝐷𝑉90 increased from 34 µm to 84 µm, 3300   respectively. [SOURCE: Page_152]

In the last part of thesis (Chapter 5), nine field experiments were conducted to quantify the airborne and sedimented transport of the droplets released from a sprinkler irrigation under different meteorological conditions. An accurate characterization of the fraction of spray flow spread by a sprinkler (usually applied over various crops) was carried out thanks to our tried and tested experimental protocol. The quantification of the aerosolized component was analyzed over a horizontal plane (up to 40 m from the emission source) and vertically (up to 10 m above the ground) in a downwind direction, beyond the irrigated area (i.e. wetted radius). The measurements conducted in laboratory conditions have shown that droplets smaller than 0.15 mm (subjected to aerosolization) represented 0.15% of the total irrigated volume. [SOURCE: Page_152]

3310      The results have also shown that the crosswind distribution of aerosol plume is Gaussian and its dispersion rate is highly dependent on the standard deviation of the wind direction and wind speed. The distribution of the aerosol plume in the vertical axis with regards to the sprinkler head presents the highest airborne transport values at heights of 1 to 2 m above the ground except for one case, i.e., Exp. 2: u = 2.7 m.s-1, Temp = 32.5°C, RH = 46.1%, RS = 279.1 w.m-2 and σθ = 35.7 degrees. The ranking analysis of parameter impacts on the transport showed that wind speed, followed by temperature, play a major role in [SOURCE: Page_152]

151 [SOURCE: Page_152]

droplet transport. Regarding sedimented transport, it was observed that the temperature effect is much lower than for airborne transport. [SOURCE: Page_153]

From these analyses, two semi-empirical models (M$_1$: power and M$_2$: exponential) were found to be the fittest for the estimation of both airborne and sediment transport when considering both spatial variables 3320   (distance, height, angle from the average wind direction) and meteorological (wind speed, standard deviation of wind direction, temperature and relative humidity only for airborne transport) parameters. Both models were found to predict transport well using a coefficient of determination (R$_2$) higher than 0.9. However, at long distances (>30 m from the sprinkler head), model 1 performance exceeds that of 2. [SOURCE: Page_153]

These empirical models have been developed for an impact sprinkler at a single pressure, which results in a specific droplet distribution. However, their application to another dataset acquired with a different configuration has shown their genericity as they gave satisfactory results. [SOURCE: Page_153]

152 [SOURCE: Page_153]

Perspective [SOURCE: Page_154]

To complete this study, supplementary measurements using different sprinkler types are in progress: experiments using a big gun are currently being conducted by the same team in order to evaluate the 3330   genericity of the models. For future experiments where Glass fiber filter (A/E, SKC Ltd, UK) will be used as a reference sampler next to each of the impingers, it is recommended to modify the head filter holder (two-piece plastic cassettes). It could be designed in such a way that a 70–110 mm long stainless steel probe with a cross sectional area corresponding to the inlet diameter and wall thickness of the impinger will be set to a cone (tangential design) to minimize aerodynamic blockage. It will be used by inserting the cone into the middle ring of an open-face three-piece cassette containing the glass fiber filter. Note that the exact length of the tube should be calculated according to particle size and required flow rate. In other cases, and further, in moving air, a sharp-edged, thin-walled probe will be isokinetically applied to measure the aerosol concentrations. This method can be carried out based on models described in the literature (Görner et al., 3340   2010; Paik and Vincent, 2002). After each experiment, any particles/ ABF tracer that may have deposited on the inner tube wall will be removed through a second Glass fiber filter or washing the head filter with demineralized water. The sample will be then measured using a fluorescence Spectrometer for ABF tracer or other techniques such as flow cytometry. [SOURCE: Page_154]

Future studies will focus on even higher sampling flow rate, preferably over 100 L.min-1, using the liquid- based samplers such a BioGuardian Air Sampler that are particularly needed for our understanding toward the dispersion of low bioaerosol concentrations from spray irrigation of TWW. [SOURCE: Page_154]

In addition, application of other methods such as qPCR or epifluorescent for assessment of the viability of bioaerosols are also needed to strengthen the study. [SOURCE: Page_154]

## 4460   APPENDIX

Appendix. A. Fundamentals of the behavior of aerosol particles/droplets [SOURCE: Page_177]

Droplet/particle motion is governed by physical forces including (i) drag which usually tends to slow a particle from the bulk air velocity and changes with droplet size and shape; (ii) gravity, which usually accelerates a particle to its terminal settling velocity; (iii) electrostatic forces, which attracts particles to surfaces ; and (iv) Brownian, random motion of small particle (𝐷𝑝 < 0.1µm) (Baron and Willeke, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_177]

𝑑𝑈𝑝 3𝜋𝜇𝑎 𝐷𝑝 𝑚𝑝       =       (𝑈𝑎 − 𝑈𝑝 ) + ∑ 𝐹𝑒𝑖                                                                 Eq. A- 1 𝑑𝑡   𝐶𝑐 𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_177]

where 𝐹𝑒𝑖 denotes the external force i (such as gravity and electrostatic force), and the other parameters are explained in the following. [SOURCE: Page_177]

A. 1. Drag force acting on aerosols in motion [SOURCE: Page_177]

The dynamical behavior of aerosols in motion is affected by the inertial force of aerosols as defined by 4470   the Newton Resistance Law and the viscous or drag force of the atmosphere as defined by Stokes Law (Lighthart and Mohr, 1994). [SOURCE: Page_177]

The general Reynolds number, Re, defined in Eq. A- 2 is a dimensionless number that gives a measure of the ratio of inertial forces to viscous forces and consequently determines the relative importance of these two types of forces for given flow conditions in aerosol systems. [SOURCE: Page_177]

𝜌𝑎 𝑈𝑙 𝑅𝑒 =                                                                                                Eq. A- 2 𝜇𝑎 [SOURCE: Page_177]

where 𝑈 is characteristic air velocity, 𝜇𝑎 is dynamic viscosity of the air, 𝜌𝑎 is air density, and 𝑙 is characteristic length–scale (dimension). Note that in the cases of the flow that passes, for instance, through the inlet tube of active samplers, the fibrous filter medium and working section of wind tunnel is of interest, 4𝐴 then the cross-sectional diameter for circular tubes or hydraulic diameter (𝐷𝐻 = 𝑃 ) for non-circular [SOURCE: Page_177]

sections is applied as characteristic length–scale. If the dimensionless Reynolds number varies from 0 to 4480   2000 then the flow is laminar; the Reynolds number is bigger than 4000 then the flow is turbulent and if the Reynolds number varies between 2000 < 𝑅𝑒 < 4000, then there is a transitional situation for the air flow. [SOURCE: Page_177]

In the case of the air flowing around a droplet/particle (aerosol), the particle Reynolds number, 𝑅𝑃 , is demonstrated by the following equation, [SOURCE: Page_177]

𝐷𝑝 𝑈∞ 𝜌𝑎 𝑅𝑒𝑝 =                                                                                               Eq. A- 3 𝜇𝑎 [SOURCE: Page_177]

176 [SOURCE: Page_177]

where 𝐷𝑝 is the particle diameter, and 𝑈∞ is the local relative velocity between gas (air) and aerosol, ⃗⃗𝑝 − 𝑈 defined as |𝑈     ⃗⃗𝑎 |. It is evident that if the particle and the air move in the same direction, the magnitude ̅𝑝 − 𝑈𝑎 . If the particle and the air move in opposite directions, magnitude of 𝑈∞ becomes of 𝑈 becomes 𝑈 ̅𝑝 + 𝑈𝑎 , and for the rests it would be assumed that |𝑈 𝑈                                                     ⃗⃗𝑎 | = 0 m.s-1. [SOURCE: Page_178]

Laminar flow at very small Reynolds number ≪ 1, which is the case with most aerosol is called 4490   creeping flow. As an example, for very small organism, e.g., bacteria, the particle Reynolds number is very small, typically in the range of 1 × 10−6. [SOURCE: Page_178]

A. 1. 1. Laminar flow: Stokes’ law When viscous forces dominate inertial forces, 𝑅𝑒𝑝 ≪ 1, and the type of air flow is laminar or creeping flow, the drag force predicted by Stokes' law is defined by : [SOURCE: Page_178]

3𝜋𝜇𝑎 𝐷𝑝 U∞ 𝐹𝑑𝑟𝑎𝑔,𝑆𝑡𝑘 =                                                                                         Eq. A- 4 𝐶𝑐 [SOURCE: Page_178]

where 𝐷𝑝 is the spherical particle diameter moving with relative particle velocity, 𝑈∞ . For particles of other shapes, the volume equivalent diameter, 𝐷𝑣 , and a dynamic shape factor, ꭓ, are introduced to Eq. A- 4 [𝐹𝑑𝑟𝑎𝑔,𝑆𝑡𝑘 = 3𝜋𝜇𝑎 𝐷𝑣 ꭓ 𝑈∞ ] (Kulkarni et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_178]

Additionally, 𝐶𝑐 is a correction factor, referred to as the Cunningham slip correction factor, given by (Davies, 1945) : −1.1 𝐶𝑐 = 1 + 𝐾𝑛 [1.257 + 0.4𝑒𝑥𝑝 ( 𝐾𝑛 )]                                                              Eq. A- 5 [SOURCE: Page_178]

4500      where 𝐾𝑛 = 2γ/𝐷𝑝 is the Knudsen number, a dimensionless number defined as the ratio of the mean free path, γ, to particle radius, 𝐷𝑝 /2. In fact, the Stoke’s law is based on the solution of equations of continuum regime and therefore is applicable to the limit 𝐾𝑛 ≪ 1. In non-continuum effects, the drag force exerted by the air is smaller than predicted by Stokes law that can be described as a result of slip on the particle surface. The slip correction factor is generally neglected for particles exceeding 10 µm in diameter, as the correction is less than 2% (Seinfeld and Pandis, 2016). [SOURCE: Page_178]

A. 1. 2. Drag coefficient Beyond the laminar flow and for particle Reynolds number greater than 0.1, Stokes’ law does not give a good description of the drag force and a correction for the inertial force is needed. A general equation for the drag force, which modifies Stokes drag, is: [SOURCE: Page_178]

𝐶𝐷 𝜋𝜌𝑎 𝐷𝑝2 𝑈∞ 2 𝐹𝑑𝑟𝑎𝑔 =                                                                                             Eq. A- 6 8 [SOURCE: Page_178]

A. 2. Gravitational Settling [SOURCE: Page_179]

A settling particle quickly reaches a constant velocity (known as the terminal velocity) as a result of balancing between the gravitational force and drag force. Equating the drag force to the force of gravity give the following equation (Wang, 2005), [SOURCE: Page_179]

1                 1 𝐹𝑑𝑟𝑎𝑔 = 𝐹𝑔 = 𝜋(𝜌𝑝 − 𝜌𝑎 )𝑔𝐷𝑝3 ≅ 𝜋𝜌𝑝 𝑔𝐷𝑝3                                                       Eq. A- 8 6                 6 [SOURCE: Page_179]

where 𝜌𝑝 is droplet/particle density, 𝑔 is the gravitational acceleration vector. [SOURCE: Page_179]

𝑈𝑡𝑠[𝑚. 𝑠 −1 ] ≅ 3 × 10−8 𝜌𝑝 [𝐾𝑔. 𝑚−3 ]𝐷𝑝2 [µ𝑚]            at NTP, [𝐶𝑐 = 1] and Eq. A- 10 1 µ𝑚 < 𝐷𝑝 < 100 µ𝑚 [SOURCE: Page_179]

4𝜌𝑝 𝑔𝐷𝑝                                                                                Eq. A- 11 𝑈𝑡𝑠 = √ 3𝐶𝐷 𝜌𝑎 [SOURCE: Page_179]

A. 3. Relaxation time (𝝉𝒑 ) [SOURCE: Page_180]

The time required for an aerosol particle or droplet to respond the sudden changes in air velocity, air 4530   temperature or to go from its initial velocity coming out of a nozzle to its final velocity (its terminal settling velocity or the local air velocity), is called the relaxation time (𝜏𝑝 ) or the time response of aerosol droplets/particles. This parameter would be obtained by the succession of the equations below. [SOURCE: Page_180]

The equation of motion for a spherical particle in a gas is given by: [SOURCE: Page_180]

16 with Eq. A- 11. However, this expression only applies to the terminal settling velocity. [SOURCE: Page_181]

A. 4. Stopping distance (S) [SOURCE: Page_181]

In the absence of external forces, the maximum distance a particle with an initial velocity will travel in the still air is defined as stopping distance. In Stokes region, the stopping distance, 𝑆𝑆𝑡𝑘 , can be calculated through the following equations : [SOURCE: Page_181]

𝑑𝑈𝑝  1 = (𝑈𝑎 − 𝑈𝑝 )                                                                                   Eq. A- 17 𝑑𝑡  𝜏𝑝 [SOURCE: Page_181]

The solution to this equation for a constant air velocity is : −𝑡⁄                                                                         Eq. A- 18 𝜏𝑝,𝑆𝑡𝑘 𝑈𝑝 (𝑡) = 𝑈𝑎 (1 − 𝑒                 ) [SOURCE: Page_181]

where −𝑡⁄                                                                                 Eq. A- 19 𝜏𝑝,𝑆𝑡𝑘 𝑈𝑝 = 𝑈0 (𝑒                 ) [SOURCE: Page_181]

Above equation is valid only for Stokes region. Mercer (1973) proposed an empirical equation for particles/doplets having an initial 1 < 𝑅𝑒𝑝 < 400, which can be found in Eq. E- 4. [SOURCE: Page_181]

A. 5. Stokes Number [SOURCE: Page_181]

The Stokes number, 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑉 , is an important parameter in fluid (air)–particle flows, which characterizes the ability of particles to adapt to air flow changes and that the air flow and particle trajectory pattern may differ to an extent dictated largely by 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑉 magnitude (Vincent, 2007). In other words, the inertia of a particle in a flow is characterized by 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑉 .Therefore, the Stokes number expresses the ratio of particle’s response time, 𝜏𝑝 , to a characteristic fluid time scale, 𝜏𝐹 (Crowe et al., 2011), [SOURCE: Page_181]

𝜏𝑝 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑉 =                                                                                             Eq. A- 21 𝜏𝐹 [SOURCE: Page_181]

𝜌 𝐷2 𝐶 𝑃 𝑃 𝑐 For the simplest case where the 𝑅𝑒𝑝 < 1 , the value of 𝜏𝑝 would be calculated through ( 18𝜇   ) and the 𝑎 [SOURCE: Page_181]

4570   value of 𝜏𝐹 is inferred from dimensional analysis as a ratio between a characteristic dimension, 𝑙, and a characteristic velocity 𝑈. In this case, the Stokes number that typically referred to the reference Stokes number, 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 can be rewritten as, [SOURCE: Page_181]

180 [SOURCE: Page_181]

𝜏𝑝 𝑈 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 =                                                                                         Eq. A- 22 𝑙 [SOURCE: Page_182]

Note that the definition of the characteristic velocity will be detailed in function to its application. Furthermore, in the present study, the width of body for passive samplers or inlet diameter for active samplers are taken as the characteristic dimension of sampler. [SOURCE: Page_182]

As shown in Figure A. 1, if 𝑆𝑡𝑉 << 1, the particle relaxation time is sufficiently small compared with the time scale of the flow, and therefore the particle/droplet reaches dynamic equilibrium with its surroundings and acts as gas tracers in the flow field. On the other hand, if 𝑆𝑡𝑉 >> 1, the particles will separate from any curved streamlines and continue in their original direction (Tu et al., 2012). The physical 4580   significance of 𝑆𝑡𝑉 becomes apparent for situations where the flow is distorted (divergent and/or convergent), for example, close to aerosol sampler. [SOURCE: Page_182]

Figure A. 1. Illustration of the effect of Stokes number (Stk) on the transport of particles in the flow about a bluff body (Vincent, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_182]

It should be mentioned that the theories of particle motion and trajectories based the term 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 (Eq. A- 22) have well predicted the performance of inertial impactors, and the transition of aerosols in curved- tube. [SOURCE: Page_182]

A. 6. Brownian motion and diffusion [SOURCE: Page_182]

For aerosols < 0.5–1 µm, the gravity becomes insignificant and the time scales for instance, in the 4590   common meteorological conditions relevant to sprinkler irrigation with TWW (U < 4.0 m.s-1) are much longer than the particle relaxation times, which are on the order of micro or pico seconds for ultrafine particles in air (Tsuda et al., 2013). So, the submicron particles are subjected to Brownian motion, a key mechanism of transmitting the infectious bioaerosols, such as viruses. Briefly, Brownian motion is the characteristic random wiggling motion of nanometer-sized aerosol particles as a result of collisions with surrounding gaseous molecules. If the spatial distribution of particles is not uniform, Brownian motion will lead to a net movement of particles from a region of higher concentration to a lower one. The net movement [SOURCE: Page_182]

181 [SOURCE: Page_182]

is known as Brownian diffusion (Wang, 2005). The Brownian diffusivity 𝐷𝐵 (or diffusion coefficient), which relates the air properties and the particles through viscous drag, can be evaluated by the Stokes– Einstein expression: [SOURCE: Page_183]

𝐾𝐵 𝑇𝑎𝑏𝑠 𝐶𝑐 𝐷𝐵 =              = 𝑘𝐵 𝑇𝑎𝑏𝑠 𝐵𝐵                                                                   Eq. A- 23 3𝜋𝜇𝑎 𝐷𝑝 [SOURCE: Page_183]

4600      where 𝐾𝐵 is Boltzmann's constant (1.38 × 10−23 𝑁. 𝑚. 𝑘 −1) and 𝑇𝑎𝑏𝑠 is absolute temperature. The term 𝐶 (3𝜋𝜇𝑐 𝐷 ) is the mechanical mobility (inverse the Stokes drag coefficient). 𝑎 𝑝 [SOURCE: Page_183]

It should be mentioned that the above equation estimate the Browonian motion of the small particles assuming the air flow is still. As the rate of deposition of small particles on surface collecting (often the filters) by Brownian motion from laminar or turbulent flow are higher than from still air, the movement process is defined by the expression “convective Brownian diffusion” (Wang, 2005). So, the dimensionless Peclet number, 𝑃𝑒, is used to measure the relative magnitude of the diffusional motion of the particles and the convective motion of the air toward the surface collecting, in particular the deposition on the fiber filters (Dunnett, 2013), [SOURCE: Page_183]

𝑈𝑙                                                                                        Eq. A- 24 𝑃𝑒 = 𝐷𝐵 [SOURCE: Page_183]

The parameters are already defined. As an example, for fibrous filter, the characteristic length (𝑙) is the 4610   diameter of fiber. According to the equation above, a large number of Peclet indicates an advectively dominated distribution, and a small number indicates a diffuse flow. For this, Wang et al. (2007) suggested a relation between diffusion efficiency of a filter media, 𝜂𝐷 , and Peclet number (𝜂𝐷 = 0.84𝑃𝑒 −0.43). [SOURCE: Page_183]

Furthermore, the Peclet number can also be defined as follow, [SOURCE: Page_183]

𝑃𝑒 = 𝑆𝑐 × 𝑅𝑒                                                                                     Eq. A- 25 𝜈 where Schmidt number, 𝑆𝑐, is the ratio of the momentum diffusivity to mass diffusivity (𝑆𝑐 = 𝐷𝑎 ) 𝐵 [SOURCE: Page_183]

Appendix. D. Characterization of the wind tunnel air flow [SOURCE: Page_189]

The characterization of the wind tunnel air flow was conducted at two sections downwind from the inlet of the working section (0.95 and 1.80 m) using a hot wire anemometer (Kimo VT 100, maximum error of ± 0.1 m. s–1), as shown in Figure D. 1. According to the standard BS ISO 22856 (2008), the wind tunnel was free from any additional test equipment such as spray generators, samplers or collectors or their supporting devices. The anemometer was placed on a sliding support: two cranks allowed movement of 4640   the sensor on the abscissa and ordinate axes. The wind tunnel had a net height of 0.35 m and a net width of 0.5 m, however, the wind profile could only be measured between 0.01 and 0.335 m in height and 0.065 m and 0.4 m in width. In the Tables below, the coordinates of the 64 measurement points have been presented. [SOURCE: Page_189]

The wind tunnel was capable to generate the wind speed up to 15 m.s-1, which was controlled by a housing of electronic variable speed drive (Digidrive SK, Lorey Somer) by changing the frequency (accuracy: 0.1 Hz). The frequency values were converted to mean air velocities (Curve-fitting: Fr–U) through a series of the measurements by a hot wire anemometer (see Figure D. 6). [SOURCE: Page_189]

4650     Figure D. 1. Position of measuring locations of air velocity in the working section of the wind tunnel [SOURCE: Page_189]

193 [SOURCE: Page_194]

4675   Appendix. E. Collection efficiency and performance of samplers in wind 4676   tunnel [SOURCE: Page_195]

4677   E. 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_195]

4678         In this study, the performance of two common passive samplers (PVC strings and plastic spheres), and two 4679   active samplers (AGI-4 and SKC BioSampler) was compared in the wind tunnel. [SOURCE: Page_195]

4680         As explained previously (Chapter 2), due to the specific characteristics of the different samplers (e.g. shape, 4681   flow rate (for active samplers), and physical characteristics), each sampler has a unique collection efficiency 4682   depending various parameters including particle size and meteorological conditions. The work herein describes 4683   a methodology for evaluating the performance of samplers in collecting airborne spray droplets in the size range 4684   of sub microns to 200 µm (𝐷𝑣90𝑚𝑎𝑥 = 138 µm), using a combination of experiments in a small‐scale wind 4685   tunnel and theoretical calculations under various air velocities. [SOURCE: Page_195]

4686   E. 2. Material and methods [SOURCE: Page_195]

4687   E. 2. 1. Preparation of spray solution 4688         In this study, the spray solution was prepared by adding 1g Acid Brilliant Flavine (ABF) and 2 L 4689   demineralized water and stir the mixture with a magnetic Stirrer (Thermo Scientific VARIOMAG ®, Mono 4690   direct, USA) for at least 8 hours. The spray solution at a concentration of approximately 0.5 g.L-1 was then 4691   transferred into the pressure vessel. The features of spray nozzle, hydraulic circuit, and how to keep the spray 4692   solution at a temperature of 20±1 °C were described in 4.2.5. To measure the concentration of the spray solution, 4693   the samples were regularly taken at the spray nozzle outlet. [SOURCE: Page_195]

4694   E. 2. 2. Active samplers experimental setup 4695         Based on the results obtained using PDPA (in Chapter 4), the experiments with AGI–4 and SKC BioSampler 4696   were divided into parts, 1) to assess the effect of air velocity on airborne spray transport; 2) to compare the 4697   performance of the impingers to collect a given droplet size distribution under various air velocities. [SOURCE: Page_195]

4698         In the first set of experiments, the impinger was filled with 20 mL demineralized water (DW) and the mass 4699   of the collection reservoir of the sampler containing the DW was measured by means of the balance with an 4700   accuracy of ±0.01 g. It was then stabilized in the center of cross-sectional area of the working section (Height 4701   = 0.175 m, width = 0.25 m) at 1.65 m downwind from the spray nozzle. The spray nozzle was situated at a 4702   height of 0.275 m above the working section floor. The air velocities ranged from 1.2 to 5.0 m. s -1 (Figure E. 4703   1). [SOURCE: Page_195]

4704      And in the second set, the impinger was in the same position as before, and the spray nozzle was moved to 4705   0.45, 0.85 and 1.25 m upstream of the inlet of the aspirating impinger (at its nominal air sampling velocity of 4706   1.68 (AGI-4) or 2.94 m.s-1 (SKC BioSampler)) wherein the air velocities of 2.25, 3.3 and 5.0 m.s-1 were drawn 4707   into the working section, respectively (Figure E. 1). These were the selected optimal configurations in the 4708   previous chapter, which in downwind airborne droplet size distribution was approximately equal (𝐷𝑣32 ≃ 67 4709   µm, 𝐷𝑣50 ≃ 74 µm, and 𝐷𝑣90 = 104 µm) (see 4.3.2.2). [SOURCE: Page_196]

4710      In addition, in order to evaluate the rate of re-aerosolization/evaporation of the collected airborne droplets 4711   containing the ABF in collection liquid of samplers during sampling, two pieces cassette including glass fiber 4712   filter was positioned downstream of the impinger’s suction line, as already explained in 3.5.1.2. [SOURCE: Page_196]

4713      The sampling time was 15 min, and each experiment was performed in triplicate. This sampling time is often 4714   used for both AGI-4 and SKC BioSampler (Riemenschneider et al. 2010). In addition, the effect of 15 min 4715   sampling time on physical collection efficiency of both impingers was examined in chapter 3 (see 3.6.1, 𝜂𝑝ℎ𝑦𝑠 of 4716   AGI-4 was 17.2 % higher than SKC BioSampler), [SOURCE: Page_196]

4717 4718       Figure E. 1. Schematic of placing the active sampler (AGI-4/SKC BioSampler) and spray nozzle in the 4719                                                   working section [SOURCE: Page_196]

4720   E. 2. 2. 1. Recovery of the collection liquid 4721      After sampling, the body of the impinger was completely dried with a napkin and the final mass of the 4722   collection reservoir of sampler containing the DW was measured by the balance and poured in bottles of 50 mL. 4723   The filters were recovered using the same technique as employed in 3.5.1.3. [SOURCE: Page_196]

4724      To determine the transmission losses, the volume of deposited droplets on the inner surfaces of impingers, 4725   including the inside walls of the nozzles and curved inlet section), the impinger was remounted in other clean 4726   local and 30 mL demineralized water was progressively injected into inlet’s sampler through a sterile syringe 4727   to rinse the curved inlet section. The sample directly drawn into a bottle positioned underneath the nozzle exit 4728   using an air compressor, 𝑉𝑇𝐿 . [SOURCE: Page_196]

4729      The recovered impinger samples were stored in the refrigerator, their ABF concentration (µg.L -1) was then 4730   quantified by fluorescence spectrometer, as explained previously (see 3.5.1.1). Airborne spray transport 4731   (transported volume) (𝑇𝑎−𝐼𝑚𝑝 ) based on the impingers, expressed in mL.m-3air.h-1, was calculated as follows: [SOURCE: Page_197]

𝑉𝐶 𝑇𝑎−𝐼𝑚𝑝. =                                                                                                     Eq. E- 1 𝑄𝑠 . 𝑡𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_197]

4732      where 𝑄𝑠 is the(nominal) sampling air flow in m-3air.h-1, 𝑡𝑠 is sampling time in hours, and 𝑉𝐶 represent the 4733   airborne spray volume collected by imingers in mL, according to following expression: [SOURCE: Page_197]

𝐶𝑠 . 𝑉𝑓𝑖𝑛𝑎𝑙 𝑉𝐶 =                                                                                                           Eq. E- 2 𝐶0 4734      where 𝐶𝑠 and 𝐶0 are the fluorescent concentration of each sample and the spray at the outlet of the nozzle, 4735   respectively, in μg ABF L−1; and 𝑉𝑓𝑖𝑛𝑎𝑙 is the volume of the final sampling liquid in mL. [SOURCE: Page_197]

4736   E. 2. 2. 2. Estimation of aspiration efficiency of the active samplers for droplets in moving air 4737      To estimate the aspiration efficiency of the impingers for airborne droplets, the following steps were taken 4738   for each droplet size bin (intervals: 5 microns) from a given airborne droplet size distribution (as measured by 4739   PDPA and related to a specific configuration. [SOURCE: Page_197]

4740           1) The droplet Reynolds number (𝑅𝑒𝑝 ) was calculated for each size bin using Eq. A- 3; [SOURCE: Page_197]

𝜏𝑝 .𝑈0 4741           2) For 𝑅𝑒𝑝 < 1 (Stokes' law), the Stokes number (𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 =                 𝑙 , see A. 5) was calculated for each [SOURCE: Page_197]

4742               droplet size bin upstream of the aspirating impingers, based on the following expressions, [SOURCE: Page_197]

𝜌𝑝 𝐷𝑝2 𝐶𝑐 𝑈0 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 =                                                                                                       Eq. E- 3 18𝜇𝑎 𝐷𝑖𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_197]

4743      The parameters have already been presented, and here, the characteristic dimension (𝑙) is the inlet diameter 4744   of the impingers 𝐷𝑖𝑛 . [SOURCE: Page_197]

4745      Since the Stokes number can be defined as the ratio of the stopping distance (𝑆, see A. 4) to the characteristic 𝑆 4746   dimension, 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 = 𝑙 , Mercer (1973) proposed an empirical equation to calculate the parameter of 𝑆 for initial [SOURCE: Page_197]

4747   conditions outside the Stokes region (𝑅𝑒𝑝 > 1) as follows, [SOURCE: Page_197]

1⁄ 𝜌𝑝 𝐷𝑝    1⁄                 𝑅𝑒 3                                                                            Eq. E- 4 𝑆=       [𝑅𝑒0 3 − 2.45 𝑎𝑟𝑐𝑡𝑎𝑛 ( 0 )] 𝜌𝑎                         2.45 [SOURCE: Page_197]

𝜌𝑎 𝐷𝑝 𝑈0 4748      where 𝑅𝑒0 =          µ𝑎 and 𝑈0 is the initial velocity of particle/droplet. Note that this equation derived from [SOURCE: Page_197]

4749   Klyachko's formula is accurate within 3% for (1 < 𝑅𝑒𝑝 < 400). [SOURCE: Page_197]

4750      The 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 values ranged from 0.0098 to 18.96 as a function of droplet size (5–195 µm), free stream air 4751   velocity (1.2 to 5.0 m.s-1) and the inlet diameter of the impingers (8.7 and 9.5 mm, for AGI-4 and SKC 4752   BioSampler, respectively); [SOURCE: Page_198]

4753            3) Based on the range of the 𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 values and the ratio of the free stream air velocity to the nominal 4754                air sampling velocity (𝑅), aspiration efficiency of the impingers was therefore estimated for each 4755                droplet size bin using the empirical equation of Liu et al. (1989) and Zhang and Liu (1989), 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝,𝑖 4756                (see Table 2.2); [SOURCE: Page_198]

4757            4) Finally, the overall aspiration efficiency (overall droplet size bins), 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 , for each configuration is 4758                the sum overall size bins of fractional efficiency, 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 (𝑖) , multiplied by the volume fractions, 𝑉(𝑖) [- 4759                ], of total airborne droplet size distribution as measured by PDPA as: 𝑖𝑚𝑎𝑥=39 Eq. E- 5 𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝 =      ∑      𝜂𝑎𝑠𝑝,𝑖 × 𝑉𝑖 𝑖=1 [SOURCE: Page_198]

4760      Note that these steps have been repeated for all selected configurations. [SOURCE: Page_198]

4761   E. 2. 3. Passive samplers experimental setup 4762      To quantify airborne spray transport using two passive samplers of PVC strings and spheres, the single spray 4763   nozzle was situated in vertical position and tested at two different heights (0.175 and 0.275 m above the floor), 4764   and also, 0.15 m downwind inside the working section. The spray solution was exposed to the air stream with a 4765   velocity range of 1.2 to 5.0 m.s-1 for 10s, a long enough time to detect fluorescent concentration of the samples 4766   by Fluorescent spectrometer but without saturating any samplers. Replicate measurement were performed for 3 4767   times. [SOURCE: Page_198]

4768   E. 2. 3. 1. PVC Strings 4769      As shown in Figure E. 2, 0.4 m long standard PVC strings 2 mm in diameter (PLASTTUB SAS, France) 4770   were placed in vertical array (0.025 “supplementary line”, 0.075, 0.125, 0.175, 0.225,0.275 m height) to 4771   determine the vertical profile of airborne spray transport at 0.85 m and 1.65 m (non-simultaneously) downwind 4772   from spray nozzle. [SOURCE: Page_198]

4774 4775   Figure E. 2. Overall view of the experimental set-up of the PVC strings in the wind tunnel 4776 [SOURCE: Page_199]

4777   E. 2. 3. 2. Spheres 4778        A grid of 30 nods of 5 mm diameter polypropylene plastic spheres (Marteau & Lemarié, France) was installed 4779   downwind from the spray nozzle as the same positions for the PVC strings. The spheres were equipped with a 4780   1 mm magnet to attach them to the galvanized steel arms of the support in that held them in position. Therefore, 4781   the spheres were placed in horizontal array (0.09, 0.17, 0.25, 0.33, and 0.41 m from the wall (z=0), in positive 4782   z direction) and in vertical array (0.025, 0.075, 0.125, 0.175, 0.225, and 0.275 m height), as shown in Figure E. 4783   3. [SOURCE: Page_199]

4784        Additionally, it allowed us to characterize airborne spray distribution patterns downwind from the source. [SOURCE: Page_199]

4785 4786          Figure E. 3. A schematic representation of the experimental setup of the spheres in the wind tunnel. [SOURCE: Page_199]

4808      where 𝑉𝑑𝑖𝑙 is the sample dilution volume in mL (20 mL demineralized water for the PVC string samples and 4809   5 or 10 mL for the spheres). Other parameters have already been presented. [SOURCE: Page_201]

4810      Note that for the spheres, the total airborne spray transport potential was also calculated with kriging 4811   interpolation method using Surfer 8.0 Software. Figure E. 4 shows an example of the airborne spray distribution 4812   based on the height of water deposited on each sphere in m, and the total airborne spray transport potential value 4813   calculated by the Surfer software. Although the results from both methods were almost the same and the 4814   maximum difference was around 1%. [SOURCE: Page_201]

4819   E. 2. 3. 4. Estimation of collection efficiency of droplets on passive samplers 4820      As downwind droplet size distributions were only measured along the centerline of wind tunnel cross section, 4821   it was not possible to accurately estimate the collection efficiency for the PVC strings and spheres in moving 4822   air and to compare them with the experimental results. However, a rough comparison was made with the 4823   assumption that when the nozzle is situated at a height of 0.275 m above the floor, the droplet size distribution 4824   at the midpoint (Y = 0.175 m, Z = 0.25 m) is representative of the distribution on 0.5 m-long line at the height 4825   of 0.175 m above the floor. Regarding the initial distribution of the spray plume in no wind conditions at a 4826   height of 100 mm, it can be found that the 𝐷𝑣50 throughout the spray plume ranged between 86 and 100 microns 4827   and is almost uniform. Therefore, the results will not be far from the reality. [SOURCE: Page_201]

4828      To estimate the droplet collection efficiency of passive samplers, the same steps of 1 to 3 as described in E. 4829   2. 2. 2 for aspiration efficiency of the active sampler were followed for each droplet size bin from a downwind 4830   droplet size distribution, except that in the Stokes number expression, the characteristic dimension (𝑙) is the 4831   diameter of the passive samplers (2 mm for the PVC strings and 5 mm for the spheres). [SOURCE: Page_201]

4836      Fritz and Hoffmann (2008) found a sigmoid function to fit the May and Clifford (1967) data for the collection 4837   efficiency (𝜂𝑐 𝑖 ) of a cylinder versus Stokes number, as follows, [SOURCE: Page_202]

𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 1.995                                                                            Eq. E- 10 𝑖 𝜂𝑐 𝑖 = (𝑠𝑡𝑘        +0.38)2.028 𝑟𝑒𝑓 𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_202]

E- 11 𝜂𝑐 𝑖 = −0.29 (𝑙𝑛𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 ) − 1.73 (𝑙𝑛𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 ) + 21.02 (𝑙𝑛𝑆𝑡𝑘𝑟𝑒𝑓 ) + 57.85 [%] 𝑖              𝑖                      𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_202]

4840      Finally, the overall droplet collection efficiency (overall droplet size bins) for each configuration was 4841   calculated using Eq. E- 5 in the sub-section E. 2. 2. 2. [SOURCE: Page_202]

4842   E. 3. Results and discussions [SOURCE: Page_202]

4843   E. 3. 1. Comparison of performance of the active samplers in moving air 4844      In the present study, the performance of the AGI-4 and SKC BioSampler were tested under various air 4845   velocities (Table E. 1). The discussion starts with the evaluation of the predicted aspiration efficiency values by 4846   the empirical equation of Liu et al. (1989) and Zhang and Liu (1989). At a constant droplet size distribution 4847   (2m.s-1 at 0.45 m, 3.3 m.s-1 at 0.85 m and 5.0 m.s-1 at 1.25 m), the predicted aspiration efficiency values rose 4848   with increasing the air velocity. According to the theory of Belyaev and Levin (1974), by increasing the Stokes 𝑈 4849   number and sub-isokinetic ratio ( 0 > 1), the aspiration efficiency increases and exceeds unity. In contrast, in 𝑈𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_202]

𝑈0 4850   super-isokinetic conditions (        < 1) , by decreasing the Stokes number, the aspiration efficiency becomes near 𝑈𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_203]

4851   unity. Given that the aspiration velocity and inlet diameter of SKC BioSampler was higher than AGI-4 (see 4852   Table 3.1), its aspiration efficiency was lower than the one of AGI-4 in all the configurations. [SOURCE: Page_203]

4853      The results of the experiments indicate that except for the air velocity of 1.2 m.s-1, the airborne spray collected 4854   in SKC BioSampler was higher than the one collected in AGI-4 (Table E. 1). These results were in contrast with 4855   what was stated in calm air conditions when using the ABF as a tracer. Apart from the air flow conditions, it 4856   could be due to the use of airborne spray plume in a wide rang of sizes (𝐷𝑣50 ≃ 74 µm, 𝐷𝑣90 = 104 µm), instead 4857   of droplet nuclei with a size of < 2.5 µm (90 % of the total volume), in calm air conditions. Besides, as shown 4858   in, Table E. 1, the droplets tend to deposit on the inner surfaces of the AGI-4 and the BioSampler, including the 4859   inside walls of the nozzles and curved inlet section, and the most significant deposition occurred at air velocity 4860   of 5.0 m.s-1 on the curved inlet section of AGI-4. Guo et al. (2022) also found that a large proportion of particles 4861   larger than 4 μm are present on the sampler inner wall, while particles smaller than 4 μm are rare . [SOURCE: Page_203]

4862      Our results also showed that the mass of the ABF recovered from the glass fiber filters situated downstream 4863   the impinger’s suction line, which quantify the amount of re-aerosolization/evaporation of the collected airborne 4864   droplets containing the ABF for both impingers was negligible (6.86E-03–2.69E-02 and 7.24E-03–8.42E-02 4865   mL.m-3air.h-1 for SKC Biosampler and AGI-4, respectively). [SOURCE: Page_203]

4866      Despite the wall-deposited droplets in AGI-4, considering the predicted aspiration efficiency values, it was 4867   expect that the transported volume sampled by AGI-4 would be more than SKC BioSampler, but these results 4868   may be thanks to the following reasons: i) the deposition of more droplets on the wall of the entrance of the 4869   SKC BioSampler and re-aspiration of them into the SKC BioSampler during sampling compared to AGI-4 𝐷 4870   ( 𝑂𝑢𝑡⁄𝐷      for SKC Biosampler and AGI-4 is 1.35 and 1.26); ii) the empirical equation was developed for 𝑖𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_203]

4871   laminar flow conditions, but at the air velocities of 1.2 to 5.0 m.s -1, the turbulent flow (Re > 4000) occurred in 4872   the wind tunnel. [SOURCE: Page_203]

4873      Even with the presence of numerous confounding factors, our results indicate that at the optimal 4874   configurations which the airborne droplet size distribution and the volume of airborne spray were almost equal 4875   (2.25 m.s-1 at 0.45 m and 3.3 m.s-1 at 0.85 m from the nozzle head), the transported volume sampled by both 4876   impingers were almost equal. It can be therefore concluded that the measurement and the analysis data were 4877   well done. Note that air velocity of 5.0 m.s-1 at 1.25 m from the nozzle has been subjected to the same droplet 4878   size distribution, but the volume of airborne spray was not equal to the others. Therefore, it seems that the 4879   aspiration efficiency of both impingers when using airborne spray plume with such density would not be much 4880   affected by (sub/super)isokinetic conditions. [SOURCE: Page_203]

4881 [SOURCE: Page_203]

4882 [SOURCE: Page_203]
