4883   Table E. 1. Comparison of performance of the SKC BioSampler and AGI-4 in moving air 𝜂𝑎                    𝑇𝑎−𝐼𝑚𝑝.             Volume of deposited [mL.m-3air.h-1]         droplets on the inner Distance          U                                                                               surface [m]             [m.s-1]                                                                   [mL.m-3air.h-1] AGI-4          SKC           AGI-4          SKC          AGI-4         SKC [SOURCE: Page_204]

0.45             2.25          124.3          77.8          25.0           36.1         0.63           0.76 0.85              3.3          175.4         104.8          27.1           37.7         1.26           0.87 1.25              5.0          270.3         157.9          28.0           44.3         3.85           1.61 1.2           85.3          67.3          0.18           0.15        0.026          0.008 2.25          121.3          80.8           9.6           10.7          0.9           0.53 1.65 3.3          170.0         104.4          18.9           21.9         1.85           0.97 5.0          266.3         156.5          23.4           37.3          3.1           1.50 4884 [SOURCE: Page_204]

4885   E. 3. 2. Distribution of airborne spray volume in the vertical direction using PVC strings 4886      Figure E. 6a to 6c show the vertical profile of airborne spray volume (Eq. E- 9) obtained from PVC strings 4887   under various air velocities (1.2, 2.25, 3.3 and 5.0 m.s-1) at distances of 0.85 and 1.65 m downwind from the 4888   spray nozzle tip positioned at heights of 0.175 and 0.275 m above the floor. All the values were normalized to 4889   a maximum value of 0.045 mL (Figure E. 6c, for U= 5.0 m.s-1, and measurement point of 0.125 above the floor). [SOURCE: Page_204]

4890      Regardless of the air velocity values or downwind distances, airborne spray volume decreased with 4891   increasing nozzle height, which agrees with previous researches (Hassen et al., 2014; Zhao et al., 2014). As 4892   demonstrated in Table E. 2, at the air velocity of 2.25 m.s-1, close to the reference air velocity employed for 4893   spray drift studies in wind tunnel [2.0 m.s-1,(ISO 22856, 2008)], from a nozzle height of 0.275 to 0.175 m, the 4894   total airborne spray transport potential (Eq. E- 7, for PVC strings) fell by about 67.7% at both distances from 4895   the source. [SOURCE: Page_204]

4896      For a given distance and adjusted release height, airborne spray volume rose with increasing air velocity. At 4897   a distance of 0.85 m downwind from a spray nozzle height of 0.275 m, the highest volume of airborne spray 4898   under an air velocity of 5.0 m.s-1 was distributed vertically within the height of 0.125 m. Furthermore, at the 4899   same distance, the volume of airborne spray transported to the height of 0.025 under air velocity conditions of 4900   5 m.s-1 was even lower than the value for 2 m.s-1. Given a neutral stability in air and almost uniform wind profile 4901   developed in the wind tunnel, it can therefore be concluded that for an air velocity of 5.0 m.s-1, the spray droplet 4902   has completely transported by the air flow before it reaches the ground/floor. At 1.65 m from the source, for the 4903   air velocity of 5.0 m.s-1, this highest volume of airborne spray shifts to height of 0.075, due to the other 4904   predominant forces (e.g. gravity force) that affect, particularly the trajectory of larger droplets in airborne spray 4905   plume. [SOURCE: Page_204]

4906      Another issue to consider is that, as shown in Figure E. 6, with decreasing spray nozzle height from 0.275 to 4907   0.175 m, at the same air velocity and vertical interval between the nozzle tip and downwind measurement point, 4908   the transported airborne spray volume decreases up to 30 %, particularly under high air velocity conditions. It 4909   could be caused by the effect of entrained air and vortex generated in spray jet that resulted in modification of [SOURCE: Page_204]

4910   initial droplet size distribution characterized at a specific axial downward distance under calm air conditions, 4911   and certainly due to ground boundary layer effect on droplet dynamic behaviors. [SOURCE: Page_205]

4912      In fact, for short range airborne spray transport studies, their results are strongly influenced by the initial 4913   release conditions. In cases where the spray nozzle mounted in downward direction, such as some types of spray 4914   nozzles used for center pivot and linear irrigation systems, fogger mist sprinklers, and pesticide sprayer, vertical 4915   profile of airborne spray transport near the source depend largely on the ratio of the air velocity to the induced 4916   air jet speed (𝑈𝑎 /𝑈𝑗 ), spray angle and initial droplet size and velocity. Ghosh and Hunt (1998) found that for 4917   the case of a weak air velocity (𝑈𝑎 /𝑈𝑗 ≤ 0.1), in the zone immediately below the nozzle, the spray centerline 4918   acts as a line sink for the external flow, and small droplets (50 µm) tend to aggregate towards the spray 4919   centerline. When the air velocity is relatively strong, the spray droplets can act as a porous resistive body over 4920   a certain distance below the nozzle, which deflects the flow lines. Besides, for a strong air velocity (𝑈𝑎 /𝑈𝑗 ≥ 4921   0.3), the 50 µm droplets immediately escape from the spray jet into the air flow and this detrainment is enhanced 4922   with narrower spray angles. [SOURCE: Page_205]

4923 [SOURCE: Page_205]

4924                                                          (a)                                                    (b) [SOURCE: Page_205]

4925                                                       (c)                                                   (d) 4926   Figure E. 6. Distribution of airborne spray volume in the vertical direction under various air velocities using 4927   PVC strings at 0.85 and 1.65 m downwind from the nozzle height positioned at a height of (a-b) 0.175 m, (c-d) 4928   0.275 m above the floor. 4929 [SOURCE: Page_206]

4930   E. 3. 3. Graphical presentation of downwind airborne spray volume using spheres 4931      Figure E. 7 shows the effect of air velocity and nozzle height on distribution of airborne spray volume at two 4932   distances (0.85 and 1.65 m) downwind from the nozzle tip. All the values were normalized to a maximum value 4933   of 2.49E-03 mL, which correspond to the measurement point : Y = 0.125 m; Z = 0.25 m, Nozzle height = 0.275 4934   m above the floor, and under the air velocity of 5 m.s-1 (see Figure E. 7o). [SOURCE: Page_206]

4935      Figure E. 7 shows that regardless of the magnitude of air velocity and distance from the nozzle, the highest 4936   airborne spray volume occurred in downwind direction along spray plume centerline. This is in accordance with 4937   the results obtained from the measurements of initial droplet distribution across the plume in section 4.3.1, 4938   which were expressed that the range of the droplets size at the center of the spray was higher than towards the 4939   edge of the spray. [SOURCE: Page_206]

4940      Whatever the distance, Figure E. 7i to 7n indicate that downwind airborne spray volume enhanced with 4941   increasing the air velocity, resulting in more compacted plume. However, as shown in Figure E. 7o and 7p, at 4942   the air velocity of 5 m.s-1, the airborne spray plume is wider compared to 3 m.s-1. This may be because of the 4943   direct transport of the larger droplets at the edge of the spray under such strong air velocity. [SOURCE: Page_206]

4944      Figure E. 7 also shows that the effect of nozzle height, downwind distance and air velocity on distribution 4945   of the airborne spray volume in vertical direction is similar to the results obtained using PVC lines. Therefore, 4946   both passive samplers are able to correctly characterize the dispersion of airborne droplet/ spray plume. The 4947   total airborne spray transport potential values obtained using both samplers for each configuration have been 4948   analyzed and compared in the next section (Table E. 2). [SOURCE: Page_206]

4970   E. 3. 4. Comparison of collection efficiency of the passive samplers in moving air 4971      A comparison was made between the results of total airborne spray transport potential (% of spray volume 4972   that has been transported) using PVC strings and spheres in order to evaluate their efficiency for collecting the 4973   airborne spray plume under various air velocities and spatial configurations, as shown in Table E. 2. [SOURCE: Page_209]

4974      The results showed that regardless of airborne droplet size distribution corresponding to each configuration 4975   (nozzle height, distance and air velocity), the collection efficiency of 2 mm diameter PVC strings was higher 4976   than the one of 5 mm diameter spheres. There is a general agreement between our experiment and the results of 4977   May and Clifford (1967) (Table E. 3). [SOURCE: Page_209]

4980      As can be seen in Table E. 3, whatever the type of the samplers, as the air velocity increased, this ratio got 4981   closer to one, which can be related to the transport of larger droplets with greater inertia (the increase in the 4982   proportion of larger droplets in droplet size distribution) at higher air velocities, and consequently higher Stokes 4983   number. It should be rappelled that at 0.85 m downwind from the spray nozzle, as the air velocity increased 4984   from 1.2 to 5.0 m.s-1, the 𝐷𝑣90 increased from 90 µm to 124 µm, respectively. This parameter rose from 37 to 4985   84 µm at 1.65 m, respectively [see Figure 4.10]). This result is in agreement with the results of Parkin and 4986   Young (2000). [SOURCE: Page_210]

4987      However, as shown in Figure E. 8, the ratio of observed collection efficiency of spheres to PVC strings (a 4988   ratio smaller than one , based on the data presented in Table E. 4) was lower than the ratio of the estimated ones 4989   by May and Clifford (1967) data for all of the air velocities. [SOURCE: Page_210]

4992 4993     Figure E. 8. Comparison of percentage difference between the collection efficiency of PVC strings and 4994   Spheres from observation (experimental) data and curves fitted to May and Clifford (1967) data under various 4995                    air velocities at a distance of (a) 0.85 m (b) 1.65 m from the nozzle head. [SOURCE: Page_211]

4996   E. 4. Conclusion 4997 4998      This chapter reported a methodology for evaluating the performance of two passive samplers (PVC strings 4999   and spheres) and two active samplers (AGI-4 and SKC BioSampler) in collecting airborne spray droplets in the 5000   size range of sub microns to 200 µm (𝐷𝑣90𝑚𝑎𝑥 =138 µm) in a small‐scale wind tunnel. The experimental data 5001   were then compared to the predicted ones from the empirical equations. [SOURCE: Page_211]

5002      The results showed that except for the air velocity of 1.2 m.s-1, the airborne spray transport collected in SKC 5003   BioSampler was higher than the one collected in AGI-4. At a distance of 1.65 m from the nozzle head, from 5004   2.25 to 5.0 m.s-1, transported volume sampled by SKC Biosampler and AGI-4 rose by 71.3, 59 % respectively. [SOURCE: Page_211]

5005      Given that at the same airborne droplet size distribution and the volume of airborne spray (2.25 m.s-1 at 0.45 5006   m and 3.3 m.s-1 at 0.85 m from the nozzle head) the transported volume sampled by both impingers were almost 5007   the same, it seems that the aspiration efficiency of both impingers when using airborne spray plume with such 5008   density would not be much affected by (sub/super)isokinetic conditions. However, it would be necessary to 5009   perform supplementary tests in more controlled conditions (e.g. the size of droplets). [SOURCE: Page_211]

5010      To observe vertical profiles and distributions of airborne spray volume obtained from PVC strings and 5011   spheres can be understand the details on the spatial behaviors of spray plume under the effect of air velocity and 5012   nozzle height from the sol/target. [SOURCE: Page_211]

5013      The results also showed that regardless of the air velocity, collection efficiency of 2 mm diameter PVC 5014   stings was higher than 5 mm diameter spheres. Therefore, the PVC strings are a good choice for airborne spray 5015   transport measurement in field-study. [SOURCE: Page_211]

210 [SOURCE: Page_211]

5016   Appendix. F. Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) set-up [SOURCE: Page_212]

5017      Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) is an optical measurement technique where the velocity field of an entire 5018   region within the flow is measured simultaneously. The technique yields quantitative visualizations of the 5019   instantaneous flow patterns, which are typically used to support the development of phenomenological models 5020   for complex flows or for validation of numerical simulations (Sciacchitano, 2019). A preliminary study has been 5021   conducted to determine the length of disturbance of air stream upstream the sampler inlet under non-isokinetic 5022   conditions using this technique, but a summary of experimental set-up has only been presented in this thesis and 5023   the results could be published later. [SOURCE: Page_212]

5024   F. 1. Smoke liquid as a tracer [SOURCE: Page_212]

5025      Air seeding provides an assessment of the airflow around the impingers by means of PIV. For the 5026   experiments in open circuit wind-tunnel, the supply of tracers is very often critical for the feasibility of the PIV 5027   measurements and it is important that seeding particles be non-toxic, non-corrosive, non-abrasive, non-volatile 5028   and chemically inert (Melling, 1997). Considering these requirements, the smoke is an ideal tracer, which have 5029   been often used for gas flow studies (Hamdi et al., 2014; Leger et al., 2001). [SOURCE: Page_212]

5030      In this study, the pro high-density smoke liquid (HQ Power VDLSLHT$_5$, Belgium) was used to generate the 5031   smoke seeding particles. The smoke particles were generated by heating to vaporization using an DMX- 5032   controlled smoke machine (EHQ POWERTM. The machine was positioned at a distance of about 0.5 m in front 5033   of wind tunnel entrance, so that the smoke stream was conducted uniformity into the working section (Figure 5034   F. 1). The smoke was drawn into the measurement region before starting the experiments. [SOURCE: Page_212]

5035      The measurements were started waiting the observation of homogenous smoke flow pattern in the measuring 5036   field through preview acquire of images by Dynamic Studio software (Dantec Dynamic, Denmark). [SOURCE: Page_212]

5037 5038                  Figure F. 1. Placement of the smoke machine in front of the wind tunnel entrance 5039 [SOURCE: Page_212]

5040   F. 2. Components of PIV [SOURCE: Page_213]

5041      The laser system consisted of a 200 mJ laser beam with a wavelength of 532 nm emitted by a double-cavity 5042   Nd:YAG laser at a rate of 15 Hz (Dantec Dynamic, Denmark), an integrated power supply containing control, 5043   power and cooling units, and a manual remote control box. [SOURCE: Page_213]

5044      The laser beam coming out from the laser head was adjusted through a base and then directed to a groove 5045   (approx. 0.3 m long and 0.02 m wide) created on the roof along the centerline of the working section, using a 5046   short light guide arm (see Figure F. 3). Eventually, the laser beam was converted to a plane of laser sheet 5047   approximately 3 mm thickness along x–y axis by light sheet optics to illuminate the region of study. [SOURCE: Page_213]

5048      A HiSence 4MC camera (Dantec Dynamic, Denmark) with a resolution of 2048 × 2048 pixels, a pixel pitch 5049   of 7.4 µm, and a sampling frame rate of 4 Hz was used for image acquisition (see Figure F. 2). It runs with 12- 5050   bit resolution and re-sampled 8-bit resolution to gain space on the hard disk. The camera measurements were 5051   carried out with a lens 300 mm for a field of view of 91 × 91 mm. [SOURCE: Page_213]

5052 5053                              Figure F. 2. HiSence 4MC camera and the Tamron’s lens 5054 [SOURCE: Page_213]

5055   F. 3. Layout of measurements using PIV [SOURCE: Page_213]

5056      As shown in Figure F. 3, the center point of the circular inlet of the impingers was situated at 1.4 m downwind 5057   from the entrance of working section, and in the center of cross-sectional area (Height = 0.175 m, width = 0.25 5058   m). In the field of view of 91 × 91 mm where instantaneous air velocity vectors in the vicinity of the aspirating 5059   impingers were measured, the origin of the coordinate system was defined at the lower left corner of the original 5060   image with X increasing to the right and Y increasing upward (Figure F. 3). The impingers were operated at 5061   their nominal flow rate (almost 6 and 12.5 L.min-1) using the BioLite air sampling pump (SKC Ltd. USA), and 5062   recording the flow rate at a rate of once per 30 seconds by a linear flowmeter (TSI series 4100, USA) positioned 5063   between the impinger and the pump through a transparent tube. Note that the flow rate value measured by the 5064   flowmeter deviated from the nominal one, whereas, at almost constant temperature of 20 °C, the recorded 5065   deviations were small (±0.2 °C). The average value during each experiment were therefore applied as an 5066   aspiration flow rate for the following analysis. To characterize the air velocities patterns near the aspirating [SOURCE: Page_213]

5067   impingers under non-isokinetic conditions, four air velocities of 0.1, 1.2, 2.25, 3.3 and 5.0 m.s-1 were examined 5068   by seeding the smoke in air flow in sufficient and almost stable concentration. [SOURCE: Page_214]

5071 [SOURCE: Page_214]

5072   F. 4. Acquisition images [SOURCE: Page_214]

5073                         ⃗⃗) was based on determining the displacement of the smoke seeding particles recorded The flow velocity (𝑢 5074   on two successive frames (∆𝑥⃗) that were separated by the time delay between two laser pulses (∆t), as follow: [SOURCE: Page_214]

∆𝑋⃗  1 ∆𝑥⃗                                                                                     Eq. F. 1 𝑢 ⃗⃗ =       = × ∆𝑡 𝑀 ∆𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_214]

5075      where ∆𝑋⃗ is the tracer particle displacement and 𝑀 is the optical magnification (𝑀 = 1⁄𝑆 ). Each PIV 𝑓 [SOURCE: Page_214]

5076   configuration (air samplings and various upstream airflow velocities) led to different time delay in order to 5077   minimize the out-of-plane displacement of particle images between the paired captures. The recommended in- 5078   plane displacement is limited to 1⁄4 of the size of the interrogation window (IA), whose size defines the spatial 5079   resolution of the measurement. However, the amplitude of the displacement correlation peak is negligible when 5080   the in-plane displacement is less than 1⁄4 of the size of the IA (ITTC – Recommended and Procedures and 5081   Guidelines, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_214]

5092   G. 1. 2. Meteorological parameters during the experimentations [SOURCE: Page_216]

5094 5095   Figure G. 1. Variation of wind speed, wind direction and standard deviation of wind direction from the height 5096   of 2 to 10 m [SOURCE: Page_216]

5097 [SOURCE: Page_216]

5098   G. 1. 3. Compute the standard deviation of wind direction (𝝈𝜽 ) 5099      Among several equations to compute the standard deviation of wind direction, 𝜎𝜃 (Essenwanger, 1986; 5100   Ibarra, 1995; Mori, 1987; Nelson, 1984; Turner, 1986; Weber, 1992), Yamartino (1984) was found to acquire 5101   the best results for most cases (EPA, 2000), which is defined as: [SOURCE: Page_216]

𝜎𝜃 = 𝑠𝑖𝑛−1 (𝜖)[1.0 + 0.1547𝜖 3 ]                                                                  Eq. G- 1 [SOURCE: Page_216]

5102      where, by showing that fluctuations in wind direction and speed from their respective mean values were 5103   uncorrelated with one another, the intermediate variable, ϵ is computed as: [SOURCE: Page_216]

𝑈 𝜖 = √1 − ( 𝑣⁄𝑈 )2                                                                                 Eq. G- 2 𝑎 [SOURCE: Page_216]

5104      where, [SOURCE: Page_216]

1                                          Eq. G- 3 𝑈 = [(−𝑛−1 ∑ 𝑈𝑖 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜃𝑖 )2 + (−𝑛−1 ∑ 𝑈𝑖 𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜃𝑖 )2 ] ⁄2 [SOURCE: Page_216]

5105      and 𝑈𝑣 is the vector mean wind speed; 𝑈𝑎 is average wind speed, and 𝑈𝑖 , 𝜃𝑖 , n are denoted the wind speed, 5106   wind direction, and number of recorded data, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_217]

5107   G. 1. 4. Atmospheric stability 5108      As shown in Table 5.1 (in see 5.2.4) , the highest standard deviations of wind direction are encountered in 5109   the experiments with the lower wind speed which is somewhat consistent with the results of Mahrt (2011), who 5110   stated that the wind direction variability is inversely related to the average wind speed. It should be mentioned 5111   that Exp.4 had a minimum wind speed (0.16 m.s-1). However, due to recording zero wind speed in most cases, 5112   the probability of inaccuracy of the estimated value for σ Ɵ is very high. It is worth mentioning that Takeuchi 5113   (1963) concluded that the standard deviation of wind direction at the same height decreases with stability. Note 5114   that this conclusion is more consistent with our results than the previous fact (see Table 5.1). [SOURCE: Page_217]

5115   G. 1. 5. Correlation analysis between input variables: Pearson method 5116      The bivariate correlation analysis between all input variables was carried out using Pearson correlation 5117   coefficients (Figure G. 2) without experiment 4 conducted in calm air (0.16 m.s-1) that confuse the correlation 5118   analysis between meteorological parameters. The Pearson test for meteorological variables indicated that: [SOURCE: Page_217]

5119          −    Wind speed and temperature were not correlated (R$_2$< 0.35); [SOURCE: Page_217]

5120          −    Bulk Richardson number was negatively correlated with solar radiation and standard deviation of 5121               wind direction, in accordance with Takeuchi (1963); [SOURCE: Page_217]

5122          −    Relative humidity and solar radiation and also temperature show R²=-0.38 and -0.30, respectively; [SOURCE: Page_217]

5123          −    Other correlation coefficients were smaller than ±0.3. [SOURCE: Page_217]

5124      Consequently, the meteorological parameters can be considered as independent variables for application in 5125   airborne and sedimented transport models. [SOURCE: Page_217]

5126      Additionally, The results shows that the airborne transport (Ta) was positively correlated with wind speed, 5127   Bulk Richardson number (unstable conditions), temperature, and negatively correlated with distance and spatial 5128   variables (height, distance, and angle). The sedimented transport (Ts) was also negatively correlated with 5129   relative humidity and positively with vapor pressure deficit. Although, the significance level of relative humidity 5130   is quite small (Table G. 3). Evidently, solar radiation is a determinant parameter for classifying air stability, but 5131   it has no impact on transport or at least their correlation is not linear. [SOURCE: Page_217]

5132      It should be noted that the global regressions of Ta or Ts in function of the independent variables are basically 5133   non-linear which are presented in the next section. Consequently, the Pearson correlation coefficients, which 5134   estimate the binary linear association between each input and transport, were not high. [SOURCE: Page_217]

Table G. 3. P-Value (Pearson) between variables and airborne transport (Ta) and sedimented transport (Ts) PVC strings                  Petri dishes Variables          Ta        Variables           Ts Ta               0           Ts                0 H            <0.0001          _                _ Dist          <0.0001        Dist           <0.0001 ΔƟ            <0,0001         Ɵ             <0.0001 U            <0.0001         U             <0.0001 Temp            0.0002       Temp             0.002 RH              0.867        RH              0.031 VPD              0.188       VPD              0.001 Rs             0.041         Rs             0.063 σθ             0.001         σθ             0.0001 B              0.001         B             <0.0001 Values in bold are significant at alpha = 0.05 level 5138 [SOURCE: Page_218]

5139   G. 2. Effect of meteorological and spatial parameters on the transport [SOURCE: Page_218]

5140   G. 2. 1.Effect of standard deviation of wind direction on transport 5141      As shown in Table G. 4, the best-fit curves derived from observation data (through CuveExpert 1.4 Basic (∆𝜃−𝑏)2 5142   software) tend to Gaussian shape (𝑓(∆𝜃) = 𝑎 × 𝑒𝑥𝑝[−0.5 ×         2𝑐 2 ]). The values of the coefficient c for the [SOURCE: Page_218]

5143   regression models were highly correlated with the ones of σθ for these two experiments. It should be noted that 5144   the coefficient ‘a’ is a function of the combination of spatial and meteorological parameters, particularly, wind 5145   speed. The distribution of aerosol plumes was not perfectly symmetric with respect to the average wind direction 5146   (b≠0). In other word, the peak of transport was not exactly in coincidence with average wind direction. However, 5147   to simplify the generalization of the empirical models presented later, this coefficient was assumed to be zero. [SOURCE: Page_218]

Since, the difference between maximum and 5169   minimum values are considerable, an attempt was made to compare the residual of each observed value, which [SOURCE: Page_220]

5170   is the difference between the observed and predicted value by both models, at the same condition (the number 5171   of valid observations). The results indicated that, in most cases, when using the model 2, the residuals obtained 5172   from the large values, which belong to the measuring points near to the sprinkler head, were less than model 1. 5173   These results are observable through the curve–fittings presented in the next figures. Besides, in some cases, 5174   the increase in the number of valid observation data and R$_2$ of a model has been due to validation of more 5175   observation data with larger values, which consequently have resulted in an increase of the RMSE values. [SOURCE: Page_221]

5176   G. 3. 1. 1.Simulation of downwind airborne transport [SOURCE: Page_221]

5177      Figure G. 6 illustrates the trends of downwind airborne transport as a function of distance for two heights of 5178   1 and 6 m, simulated by two models. Overall, the curves indicate that both models fitted well to the observation 5179   data. Additionally, the function of the downwind distance for the model 1 and the model 2 are similar, 5180   respectively, to German drift Model (%𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑡 = 𝐴𝑍 −𝐵 ) and Dutch drift Model (%𝑑𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑡 = 𝑎0 𝑒 −(𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡×𝑎1 ) + 5181   𝑏0 𝑒 −(𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡×𝑏1 ) ) which have been developed for pesticide spray drift studies. Therefore, it can be concluded that 5182   both models can be used to characterize transported volume downwind from wetted area up to at least 4 times 5183   the sprinkler range. [SOURCE: Page_221]

5184      At the height of 1 m above the ground, the airborne transport for the experiment N°5 (U = 3.09 m.s-1, Temp 5185   = 29.43 °C, RH = 41.64% and RS = 562.45 w.m-2 and σθ = 32.55 degrees) was 1.16–1.56 times higher than the 5186   experiment N°3 (U = 3.03 m.s-1, Temp = 26.92 °C, RH = 27.22%, RS = 740.23 w.m-2 and σθ = 38.89 degrees). 5187   At the height of 6 m, this ratio decreased to 0.64–1.05. According to these results, lower standard deviation of 5188   wind direction and higher relative humidity, albeit at lower altitudes, tend to increase downwind airborne 5189   transport (see also Figure G. 7). Indeed, this unexpected effect of relative humidity on the airborne transport 5190   might be due to the inter-correlation of relative humidity and solar radiation. Consequently, the positive sign of 5191   relative humidity coefficient in the models 1 and 2 may also have been the result of its combination with 5192   parameter of height. However, for explaining the role of relative humidity, it would be necessary to perform 5193   more experiments. [SOURCE: Page_221]

Simulation (lines/dashed lines) of downwind airborne transport and experimental data 5205      (black dots) from PVC strings at two heights of 1 and 6 m above the ground which related to: (a) 5206                            Exp.2, (b) Exp.3, (c) Exp.5, (d) Exp.9 (see table. 1) [SOURCE: Page_224]

5207   G. 3. 1. 2. Simulation of vertical dispersion of aerosol plume 5208      Figure G. 7a to 7l show simulated airborne transport as a function of height, for three distances. 5209   These curves indicate that both models globally predict the trend of vertical dispersion well. [SOURCE: Page_224]

5210      However, both models have some limitations. In fact, for the experiments with incomplete Gaussian 5211   shape such as Exp.3 (class stability: B) at distance of 30 and 40 m from the sprinkler head, the 5212   exponential and power models cannot take into consideration the dome in 2 and 4 meter heights. 5222   G. 3. 1. 3. Simulation of horizontal dispersion of aerosol plume 5223      Figure G. 8 compare the measured and simulated distribution of airborne droplet plume in the crosswind 5224   direction. The curve–fits show that in most of the experiments, both models predicted well. The comparison 5225   between measured and curve fits from Exp.8 (carried out under low wind speed) shows that the lower transport 5226   are modelled less accurately, even though in the study of the genericity the models, the M$_1$ performed well for 5227   the low wind speed. 5239      Figure G. 9 shows the sedimented transport as function of distance. Contrary to the airborne transport, except 5240   for the first two distances (16 and 18 m), there is no significant difference between Exp.3 and 5. Therefore, this 5241   result confirms that the relative humidity has low or no-impact on the sedimented transport. As shown in Fig 5242   S10d, experiments with a relative low temperature, sedimented transport at distances close to the sprinkler (up 5243   to 22 m) are modelled with less accuracy than ones at longer distances, considering relative differences between 5244   measured and modelled sediments. It is evident that under similar meteorological conditions, as temperature 5245   decreases, more downwind sedimented transports are measured near the wetted area. It might be because fewer 5246   number of experiment realized under relative low temperature so that the models less accurately fit these 5247   measuring points. [SOURCE: Page_229]

5248                                                         (a)                                                     (b) [SOURCE: Page_229]

5249                                                           (c)                                              (d) 5250       Figure G. 9. Simulation (line/dashed line) and experimental data (black dots) of downwind sedimented 5251              transport using Petri dishes for: (a) Exp.2, (b) Exp.3, (c) Exp.5, (d) Exp.9 (see table. 1) [SOURCE: Page_229]
